  [G1.1] Essential facts (personal details) stated in the report are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   John Sullivan, age 69; Kim Sullivan, age 65; both married; three grown-up children all financially independent; both in good health.

    File:     Fact find [P5] records a date of birth of 07/05/1960 and age 65 for a family member (spouse), consistent with Kim Sullivan age 65. The report age of 65 for Kim is corroborated. John Sullivan's age of 69 is not directly corroborated by a date of birth or explicit age entry in the retrieved passages — the nearest numeric value found is 75 in a different context. The cashflow modelling charts in [P10], [P32], [P34] use age labels '69/65' as the starting pair, consistent with the report. Client agreement [P3] references 'Sullivan' with no first names. The fact find [P5] shows a spouse date of birth 07/05/1960 giving age 65. No fact-find entry explicitly recording John's date of birth or age 69 was retrieved.

    Discrepancies:

      - John Sullivan's reported age of 69 is not directly corroborated by a retrieved fact-find entry showing his date of birth or age; the value 69 does not match any retrieved numeric value in the passages (nearest is 75 in an unrelated context).

      - The cashflow charts use '69/65' age labels which are consistent with the report but are derived from the suitability report inputs rather than an independent KYC record.

      - No date of birth is present in the canonical model for either client, so exact DOB matching cannot be performed.

    Kim Sullivan's age of 65 is corroborated by the fact find date of birth 07/05/1960 in [P5] and the age field of 65 in the same passage. John Sullivan's age of 69 is stated in the report and reflected consistently in cashflow modelling age labels (69/65) in [P10] and [P32], but no independent KYC document in the retrieved passages explicitly records his date of birth or age as 69. The figure 69 does not match any value found in the passages (the value-match check found no corroboration). This is a missing-evidence situation for one client's age rather than a contradiction, but the report asserts a personal fact (age 69) with no supporting KYC record in the pack. Marital status (Married) and dependants (three grown-up children, financially independent) and health (both in good health) are asserted in the report but not separately corroborated by retrieved fact-find passages — no passage explicitly confirms marital status or dependants for either client. The absence of corroborating KYC evidence for John's age and the marital/dependants details constitutes a potential concern under the 'evidence absent' rule. The spouse-vs-client guard does not apply here as the mismatch is absence of evidence rather than a cross-client confusion.

  [G1.2] Tax status stated in the report is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report asserts on page 20 (Annual Allowance section) that the client has not triggered the MPAA and does not have a lower annual allowance. The report also references the Annual Allowance as currently £60,000 per annum for 2024/2025 and mentions carry-forward provisions. The report's income/tax position is described in the capacity for loss section: the client receives a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week, earns approximately £300 per week net as an HGV driver, and receives £20 per week from the Sea Cadets. The report does not explicitly state an income tax band for the client. The canonical model records mpaaTriggered = false. The fields incomeTaxBand, annualAllowance, and territorialProfile are absent from the extracted model.

    File:     The fact find (category B) records the client's highest rate of income tax paid as 0% and shows net basic monthly income of £1,200.00 from employment (CDH Recruitment, HGV Driver) plus a State Pension of £230 per month (recorded as monthly in the fact find income table, though the suitability report states £230 per week). No explicit MPAA trigger status is recorded in the supporting documents. The Aviva policy information shows pension input amounts of £0 for 2021/2022 through 2025/2026, with £756.14 in 2020/2021, indicating no recent contributions that would suggest a MPAA trigger event. The suitability report narrative (category I, page 19) states the Annual Allowance is £60,000 for 2024/2025 as a general legislative statement and that the client has not triggered the MPAA. No HMRC or provider document directly confirms or denies MPAA status. The fact find records 0% as the highest rate of income tax, which is consistent with a basic or non-taxpayer position, though this conflicts with the income levels described in the suitability report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The fact find records the State Pension income as £230.00 per month in the income table, whereas the suitability report (capacity for loss section) states the client is 'in receipt of a full State Pension of approximately £230 per week'. These are materially different figures: £230/month ≈ £2,760/year versus £230/week ≈ £11,960/year.

      - The fact find records 'Highest rate of income tax paid' as 0%, yet the combined income described in the suitability report (State Pension ~£230/week + HGV earnings ~£300/week net + Sea Cadets £20/week ≈ £550/week gross equivalent) would typically place the client above the personal allowance threshold, making a 0% tax rate difficult to reconcile unless net figures are used throughout. This is a potential inconsistency in the recorded tax status.

      - The incomeTaxBand, annualAllowance (as applied to this client), and territorialProfile are absent from the canonical model, meaning the report's implicit tax treatment (basic rate relief on contributions, marginal rate on drawdown withdrawals) cannot be fully verified against a recorded tax position.

      - No supporting document (HMRC correspondence, provider confirmation, or fact find field) directly evidences that the MPAA has not been triggered, beyond the report's own assertion.

    The most material discrepancy is the State Pension frequency mismatch: the fact find income table records £230/month whereas the suitability report states £230/week. These differ by a factor of approximately 4.3 and the weekly figure of £230 (≈£11,960/year) is broadly consistent with the full new State Pension rate for 2024/2025 (approximately £221.20/week), making the suitability report's weekly figure more plausible on its face. However, the fact find's monthly figure of £230 (≈£2,760/year) is irreconcilable with a full State Pension entitlement. This is a material factual inconsistency between two documents and affects the income figure that feeds into the retirement income need assessment. The fact find's 0% income tax rate is also difficult to reconcile with the income levels described in the report, though it is possible the 0% field reflects that the client pays no income tax currently if his net pay is below the personal allowance after accounting for pension contributions — but this is not explained in either document. The MPAA non-trigger assertion is plausible given the Aviva pension input history (£0 contributions in recent years, no drawdown crystallisation evidenced), but no independent document confirms it. The generic legislative statement about the £60,000 annual allowance is a legislative fact, not a client-specific assertion, so the guard applies and no finding arises from that alone. The territorial profile is absent; the Aviva T&Cs note payments cease if the client moves outside the UK, but there is no positive evidence of non-UK residency, so this is a gap rather than a contradiction. Overall, the State Pension frequency discrepancy between the fact find and the suitability report is a material factual inconsistency that affects a figure relied upon in the income/retirement need analysis. The 0% tax rate in the fact find against the income profile described in the report is a further concern. These together warrant a Potential Concern at Moderate severity, as they relate to income figures used in the needs analysis rather than directly driving a transfer value or charge calculation.

  [G1.3] Employment details are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   Client 1 (JS) is employed full-time as an HGV driver through an agency, earning approximately £300 per week net, with an intended retirement age of 71. Client 2 (Kim/KS) is employed with an intended retirement age of 66.

    File:     The fact find (P5) records JS as Employed, occupation HGV Driver, employer CDH Recruitment. The income table (P13) shows a net basic monthly income of £1,200 for JS under 'Basic Income, Employed, CDH Recruitment'. The suitability report cashflow section (P15) states JS receives £300 per week as an HGV driver. The cashflow tool baseline scenario (P3) shows employment income of £16,640 for JS and £13,200 for the combined household. No retirement age for either client is explicitly recorded in the fact-find passages retrieved. The risk profile document (P19) shows an investment period of 2 years and an investment of £106,000, implying a near-term retirement horizon consistent with the report's narrative but not stating a retirement age directly.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the employer is an 'Agency (unspecified)', but the fact find (P5/P13) names the employer as 'CDH Recruitment' — a specific named employer, not merely 'unspecified agency'.

      - The report states JS earns 'around £300 per week net'; the fact find (P13) records a net basic monthly income of £1,200.00. £1,200/month equates to approximately £276.92/week (×12÷52), not £300/week. The suitability report's own cashflow section (P15) uses £300/week, which annualises to £15,600, while the baseline cashflow tool (P3) shows employment income of £16,640 for JS — a further discrepancy between the two report-side figures.

      - No corroborating evidence for JS's intended retirement age of 71 was found in any retrieved passage. The figure 71 was not matched in any document.

      - No corroborating evidence for Kim's intended retirement age of 66 was found in any retrieved passage. The nearest figure found is age 65 (P14, ONS life expectancy reference for a 65-year-old female), which is a different context entirely.

    Three issues arise. First, the employer name: the report describes the employer as an unspecified agency, but the fact find clearly names CDH Recruitment. This is a minor factual inconsistency with no bearing on the recommendation but is a data mismatch. Second, the income figure: £300/week net (£15,600 p.a.) stated in both the report narrative and cashflow section does not reconcile with the fact find's £1,200/month net (£14,400 p.a.), nor with the cashflow tool's £16,640 employment income for JS. Three different figures appear across the file for what should be the same income. The £300/week figure is used in the cashflow modeller per P15, but the baseline cashflow tool in P3 inputs £16,640 — a fourth variant that does not match any of the others exactly. Employment income underpins affordability and cashflow, so this inconsistency is material. Third, the intended retirement ages of 71 (JS) and 66 (Kim) have no supporting evidence in the retrieved passages; neither figure appears in any fact-find or supporting document in the pack. The guard notes that a later file note may supersede a fact-find entry, but no such note is in the pack either. Absence of corroboration for retirement ages, combined with the income figure inconsistency across multiple documents, constitutes a material concern. The employer name discrepancy is lower severity. Taken together, particularly given the income figure drives the cashflow model, this is a Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G1.4] Income and expenditure figures are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   C1 (John): employment income £300/week net (HGV driver via agency); additional income £20/week (Sea Cadets volunteer); State Pension £230/week. C2 (Kim): employment income £1,100/month net; State Pension £230/week (not yet in payment). Household expenditure £1,700/month. Total assets £6,000 (cash). No liabilities.

    File:     Fact Find [P10]: C1 employment income shown as £1,200/month net; State Pension £230/month. Fact Find [P1]: Total Monthly Expenditure £1,718. Fact Find [P13]: Cash £6,000. Fact Find [P10]: Total Net Monthly Income £1,430. Suitability report [P17] corroborates £300/week, £20/week, £1,100/month, £230/week State Pension, £1,700/month expenditure. Cashflow [P9] confirms Kim £1,100/month and state pension £11,973 per annum.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states C1 employment income £300/week net; fact find [P10] shows 'Basic Income' net monthly amount £1,200.00. £300/week × 52/12 = £1,300/month, not £1,200/month — a 7.7% gap exceeding the 5% tolerance, and incompatible as a weekly-to-monthly conversion.

      - Report states C1 State Pension £230/week; fact find [P10] shows State Pension net monthly £230.00 (i.e. monthly, not weekly). £230/week × 52/12 = £996/month, which is very different from £230/month — the frequency recorded in the fact find appears to be monthly, conflicting with the report's weekly figure.

      - Report states household expenditure £1,700/month; fact find [P1] shows calculated total monthly household expenditure £1,718 — a difference of £18 (1.1%), within the 5% rounding tolerance.

      - Report states cashflow uses state pension income £11,973 per annum [P9]; £230/week × 52 = £11,960/year, not £11,973 — marginal rounding difference.

    Two material discrepancies arise. First, C1's employment income: the report states £300/week net, which annualises to £15,600/year or £1,300/month. The fact find [P10] records net monthly basic income of £1,200.00. The weekly-to-monthly guard specifies that £300/week = £1,300/month (×52÷12), not £1,200/month. The fact find figure of £1,200/month cannot be reconciled with the report's £300/week even after frequency conversion — the gap is £100/month (7.7%), exceeding the 5% tolerance. This is not a gross/net mismatch (both are net). This is a material factual discrepancy between the report and the fact find. Second, C1's State Pension: the report states £230/week; the fact find [P10] shows State Pension net amount £230.00 at Monthly frequency. These cannot both be correct — if it is £230/week that is ~£996/month; if it is £230/month that is ~£53/week. The cashflow model [P9] uses £11,973 per annum, which is close to £230/week × 52 = £11,960 (rounding only), suggesting the report's weekly figure was used in the model. However the fact find records it as monthly — a significant frequency discrepancy. The expenditure difference of £18 (£1,700 vs £1,718) is within rounding tolerance and does not constitute a finding. The £11,973 vs £11,960 annualisation difference is also within rounding tolerance. The employment income discrepancy (£300/week vs £1,200/month net in fact find) and the State Pension frequency conflict (weekly in report vs monthly in fact find) are both material and unresolved. The employment income figure drives affordability assessment and the State Pension figure is central to retirement income planning, so these are high-severity findings.

  [G1.6] State pension entitlement is consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Client 1 (JS) is in receipt of his full State Pension of £230 per week (gross, in payment). Client 2 (Kim/KS) will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age (not yet in payment). Both are stated as full entitlement. The cashflow modelling uses £11,973 per annum for each client's state pension income.

    File:     The suitability report itself (P9) states £230 per week for JS (in payment) and £230 per week for Kim (deferred). The cashflow section of the report (P5) states 'You receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum' and 'Kim will receive state pension income of £11,973 per annum from age 67.' The intelliflo baseline scenario (P1) records state pension age as 66 for JS and shows Kim retiring at age 71 in 2027. The fact find (P15) has a 'State Pension Entitlement' section with fields for State Pension Retirement Age, Basic State Pension, Additional State Pension, Pension Credit, Spouses Pension and BR19 Projection, but all these fields appear blank — no values are recorded. No separate state pension forecast or HMRC Check Your State Pension document is present in the pack.

    Discrepancies:

      - £230 per week × 52 = £11,960 per annum, but the cashflow modelling uses £11,973 per annum — a difference of £13 p.a. These do not reconcile at ×52 within the stated tolerance.

      - The report states Kim will receive her state pension 'when she reaches State Pension age' but the cashflow narrative (P5) says 'from age 67', while the intelliflo baseline (P1) records Kim's state pension age as 66 — an apparent one-year discrepancy between the cashflow narrative and the planning tool input.

      - The fact find state pension entitlement section (P15) is blank: no state pension age, amount, BR19 projection or other corroborating figure is recorded for either client.

      - No independent state pension forecast (e.g. DWP letter, HMRC Check Your State Pension, BR19) is present in the evidence pack to corroborate the £230 per week figure for either client.

    Two issues arise. First, an arithmetic inconsistency: £230 × 52 = £11,960, but the cashflow modelling inputs £11,973 for both JS and KS. This is a £13 discrepancy that does not reconcile within the ×52 tolerance specified by this check. It is a self-contained internal inconsistency — the report states £230 per week in the narrative and £11,973 per annum in the cashflow — and no guard explains it away. Second, the cashflow narrative (P5) says Kim's state pension starts 'from age 67', while the intelliflo planning tool (P1) records Kim's state pension age as 66. The guard notes that state pension age and retirement age are different things and can be conflated; here the retirement age for Kim in P1 is 71, so the '66' figure in P1 appears to be her state pension age. This creates a discrepancy between the cashflow narrative ('age 67') and the planning tool input ('66'). Third, and most importantly for this check's 'evidence absent' rule: the fact find state pension section is entirely blank and no independent corroborating document (DWP forecast, HMRC portal output, BR19) exists in the pack. The £230 per week figure is asserted in the report for both clients but is unsupported by any category B or E evidence. The check's own guidance states that where state pension entitlement evidence is absent, the outcome is Potential Concern. The arithmetic mismatch between the weekly and annual figures used in the cashflow further reinforces this. The £13 annual difference is low in monetary terms but is a factual inconsistency within the report itself. The missing corroborating evidence is the more material concern.

  [G1.7] Existing products (pensions, investments and protection plans) are consistent with the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Five existing pension arrangements: (1) People's Pension 6197175/PP £6,601.55 / £6,601.55 TV, contributions £1,193.33 PA client + £897.13 PA employer, fund: Global Investments (up to 85% shares) 100%; (2) Scottish Widows ZU4225575 £211.32 / £211.32 TV, £0 contributions, funds: SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & Three CS1; (3) Aviva SP50163762 £3,744.36 / £3,744.36 TV, £0 contributions, fund: Aviva pension my future focus consolidation; (4) Zurich P10026-719-001/DL £103,439.24 / £103,439.24 TV, £0 contributions, fund: Managed AP; (5) Standard Life D2301334000 £3,002 / £3,002 TV, £0 contributions, fund: SL at retirement universal (PP 10 year) pension fund. Report also states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000' (page 3) and 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000' (page 4 capacity for loss section).

    File:     Provider documents confirm: Aviva SP50163762 plan value £3,744.36 / TV £3,744.36 as at 22 Sep 2025 [P22]; fund: Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2 [P22]. Zurich P10026-719-001/DL fund value £103,439.24 / TV £103,439.24 (Managed AP accumulation, 492.05 units @ £210.221) as at 24 Sep 2025 [P18]. Standard Life D2301334000 current transfer value £3,002.02 as at 30 Sep 2025 [P14]; fund: SL At Retirement Universal (PP10 Year) Pension Fd 100% [P16]; Standard Life policy value statement shows £2,994.30 on 30 Sep 2025 [P24]. People's Pension 6197175/PP transfer value £6,601.55 [P54]; fund: Global Investments (up to 85% shares) [P30]. Scottish Widows ZU4225575 transfer value £211.32 [P54]. Policy research report confirms all five transfer values [P54]. Arithmetic sum of all five plans: £116,998.47 (derived). Zurich illustration projects plan worth equivalent of £106,000 in today's prices at retirement [P25] — this is an illustration projection, not a current value. Fact find records a pension value of £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026 under an Aviva Platform policy AV2936864 [P1], which does not correspond to any of the five existing arrangements listed in the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Report states Standard Life current value as £3,002 (rounded); provider transfer value document [P14] states £3,002.02 — a £0.02 difference. The Standard Life policy value statement [P24] shows £2,994.30 on 30 Sep 2025, which differs from the £3,002 stated in the report by £7.70. These are from the same date (30 Sep 2025 / 1 Oct 2025 valuation), suggesting a discrepancy between the transfer value (£3,002.02) and the fund value (£2,994.30) — the report uses the transfer value figure but rounds it.

      - 2. Report states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000' (page 3 narrative). The arithmetic sum of all five stated individual plan values is £116,998.47. Even rounding, this does not approximate to £110,000. The £110,000 figure matches only the three switching plans (Aviva £3,744.36 + Zurich £103,439.24 + Standard Life £3,002 = £110,185.60), suggesting the narrative incorrectly describes the combined total of all five plans.

      - 3. Report states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000' in the Capacity for Loss section (page 4). The arithmetic sum of the five plans is £116,998.47; the sum of the three switching plans is £110,185.60. Neither matches £106,000. The £106,000 figure appears to derive from the Zurich illustration projection value [P25] ('equivalent of £106,000 in today's prices'), not a current fund value.

      - 4. Fact find [P1] records a pension value of £116,555.45 as at 23/07/2026 under Aviva Platform policy AV2936864-001. This policy number and value do not correspond to any of the five existing arrangements listed in the suitability report. The report lists Aviva SP50163762 (£3,744.36), not AV2936864. This may be the new Aviva Pension Portfolio recommendation, but the fact find records it with a future valuation date of 23/07/2026 and no policy start details matching the recommendation — the relationship between this entry and the five existing arrangements is unresolved.

      - 5. The contributions for People's Pension are stated as £1,193.33 PA (client) and £897.13 PA (employer) in the report. No supporting evidence in the case file corroborates these specific annual contribution figures for the People's Pension — the People's Pension transaction history [P2-P10] shows small monthly contributions but no schedule that aggregates to these annual amounts.

      - 6. The report lists Scottish Widows fund holdings as 'SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 & SW Pension Portfolio Three CS1'. The Scottish Widows policy info [P41] does not explicitly confirm these fund names in the retrieved passages, though the policy info file is present in the pack.

      - 7. Derived figures show EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,438-£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002. This means the charge comparison for Standard Life in the report is based on the wrong plan value, making the Standard Life charge figures unreliable.

      - 8. Report page 8 states Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (saving). Charges table page 11 shows impact of recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' (increase/more expensive under new plan). These are contradictory directions — one says switching saves costs, the other says it costs more.

    The comparison can be largely performed. Provider documents confirm four of the five plan values to the penny or within rounding: Aviva £3,744.36 [P22], Zurich £103,439.24 [P18], People's Pension TV £6,601.55 [P54], Scottish Widows TV £211.32 [P54]. For Standard Life, the transfer value of £3,002.02 [P14] supports the report's £3,002 (rounded), but the policy value statement [P24] shows £2,994.30 — a minor difference likely reflecting the distinction between fund value and transfer value, which the guard notes is not automatically a mismatch. This is a low-severity presentation point. The more significant issues are: (a) The report's page 3 narrative states 'approximately £110,000' as the combined pension value of all five plans. The arithmetic sum is £116,998.47, and even on a rounded basis this does not approximate to £110,000. The £110,185 figure is the total of only the three plans being switched, so the narrative appears to have applied the switching total to describe all five plans — a material internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction. (b) The page 4 'approximately £106,000' figure does not match the sum of any combination of the five plans; it appears to derive from the Zurich illustration projection [P25] which states 'equivalent of £106,000 in today's prices' — a projected future value in real terms, not a current fund value. Using this as a current value total in a capacity for loss section is a material error. (c) The fact find [P1] records an Aviva Platform policy AV2936864-001 with a value of £116,555.45 at 23/07/2026. This does not match any of the five existing arrangements and may represent the new recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio post-consolidation, but the fact find records it without context that makes this clear. The date 23/07/2026 is future-dated, which is unusual for a fact find entry and suggests it may be a projected or post-transfer value rather than a pre-advice existing arrangement. This warrants clarification but does not directly contradict the existing arrangement values. (d) The People's Pension contributions of £1,193.33 PA and £897.13 PA are stated in the report but not independently corroborated by a contributions schedule or employer confirmation in the case file — only a long transaction history of small amounts is present. (e) The EA5 Standard Life charge rows in the derived figures are computed using Zurich's value (£103,438-£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, confirming the extraction finding that the Standard Life charge comparison is based on the wrong fund value. This, combined with the contradictory cost comparison figures (page 8 says 0.08% saving; page 11 table shows +0.26% increase), represents a material internal inconsistency on a figure relevant to the recommendation rationale for Standard Life. The chart label swap (A/D labels reversed between two charts on page 13) is a presentation inconsistency but does not affect underlying values. The 2.94%/2.95% mid growth rate discrepancy is a low-severity rounding issue. Overall, the most material findings are: the two inconsistent total pension values stated in the narrative (£110,000 and £106,000 versus the actual sum of £116,998.47); and the contradictory Standard Life charge impact figures (page 8 versus page 11 table), the latter of which rests on a charge row computed against the wrong plan value.

  [G1.8] Data mismatches and unsupported figures within the report itself

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations unverified

    Report:   The report contains the following internal figures and assertions: (1) Page 3 states combined pension value is 'approximately £110,000'; Page 4 states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000'; the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values is £116,998.47 — none of the three agree. (2) Page 9 states the Aviva Pension Portfolio total transfer value is £110,185, consistent with the sum of the three switching plans (£110,185.60), but the page 3 'approximately £110,000' narrative appears to describe all five plans yet is closer to the transfer-only subtotal. (3) Page 8 states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving); Page 11 charges table shows Standard Life impact as '+0.26%' (an increase — i.e. the recommended solution is more expensive for this plan). (4) Page 7 states Aviva existing plan is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution'; Page 11 table shows '-0.06%' for Aviva, which is directionally consistent. (5) Page 13 first performance chart: A=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation, D=Zurich Managed Pn AP; Page 13 volatility chart key: A=Zurich Managed Pn AP GTR in GB, D=Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation — labels A and D are swapped between the two charts. (6) Page 11 states the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive on a like-for-like basis and £264 underperformance tolerance; also states 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees and £4,672 outperformance required. (7) Page 11 narrative refers to 'a mid-growth rate of 2.94%'; Page 18 critical yield table labels mid growth rate as '2.95%'. (8) Derived figures confirm: EA5 charge rows (annual charge £186.19 at 0.18% and impact £268.94 at 0.26%) both imply a fund value of ~£103,439 which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's value of £3,002 — the EA5 rows are computed on the wrong plan.

    File:     This requirement compares the report against itself. No external supporting documents are required or assessed here. The derived figures computed from the report's own extracted values are treated as settled arithmetic: the five individual plan values sum to £116,998.47; EA5 charge rows imply a fund base of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not £3,002 (Standard Life's value).

    Discrepancies:

      - (1) Combined pension value stated as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3 vs 'approximately £106,000' on page 4 vs arithmetic sum of £116,998.47 — three mutually inconsistent figures for the same set of plans.

      - (2) Page 3 narrative 'approximately £110,000' appears to describe all five plans but is arithmetically close only to the three-plan transfer subtotal of £110,185.60, not the five-plan total of £116,998.47, suggesting a mislabelling of scope.

      - (3) Standard Life charge direction contradiction: Page 8 states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (recommended is cheaper); Page 11 table shows impact as '+0.26%' (recommended is more expensive). These are directly contradictory on direction and magnitude.

      - (4) EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows are computed on a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's current value) rather than Standard Life's own current value of £3,002.00, making the EA5 annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) and the impact row (£268.94 at 0.26%) unreliable for Standard Life comparison purposes.

      - (5) Page 13 performance chart and volatility chart have swapped A/D labels: first chart A=Aviva My Future Focus, D=Zurich Managed Pn AP; second chart key A=Zurich Managed Pn AP, D=Aviva My Future Focus.

      - (6) Mid-growth rate stated as '2.94%' on page 11 but labelled as '2.95%' in the critical yield table on page 18.

      - (7) The 0.24% like-for-like saving (£264) and 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees (£4,672) are both presented on page 11 without reconciliation, creating potential client confusion, though they measure different things.

    This group compares the report against itself and the derived figures computed from its own extracted values. No external evidence is needed or available. Multiple genuine internal inconsistencies are confirmed: (A) The three pension total figures (£110,000 / £106,000 / £116,998.47) are mutually inconsistent and none can be explained by an 'as-at date' guard since no different valuation dates are stated. This is a direct self-contradiction on a figure that influences the capacity for loss assessment and the framing of the recommendation. (B) The Standard Life charge impact is stated contradictorily as a saving of 0.08% on page 8 but an increase of 0.26% on page 11; the derived figures confirm the EA5 rows were computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, which explains the magnitude discrepancy and constitutes an arithmetic error in the charges table. This is a figure that directly bears on the replacement analysis justification. (C) The swapped A/D chart labels on page 13 are a presentation error that misidentifies which fund is being compared in the volatility chart. (D) The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor but present. Items (A), (B) and (C) are material: (A) affects the financial summary and CfL framing; (B) directly undermines the cost justification for the Standard Life switch and the arithmetic shows the wrong fund value was used in the EA5 rows; (C) could mislead a client reading the performance comparison. The like-for-like vs including-adviser-fee comparison (item 7 in discrepancies) measures genuinely different things and is not a self-contradiction, though the lack of reconciliation is a presentation concern. Severity is assessed as High because the report contradicts itself on the same figure (pension totals) in multiple places and the Standard Life charge direction contradiction involves a figure that drives the replacement recommendation — no external evidence is needed to confirm either finding.

  [G1.10] Values expressed in different frequencies or formats reconcile (weekly/monthly/annual, gross/net, percentages and totals)

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states five income items: (1) Client 1 earns £300 per week net; (2) Client 1 receives £20 per week from Sea Cadets volunteer role; (3) Client 1 receives full State Pension of £230 per week; (4) Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net; (5) Kim will receive full State Pension of around £230 per week. Expenditure is stated as £1,700 per month. The report also states combined pension value of approximately £110,000 (page 3) and approximately £106,000 (page 4 capacity for loss section), while the Standard Life plan is described as 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (page 8) but the charges table shows +0.26% impact for Standard Life (page 11). Growth rate is stated as 2.94% in one place and 2.95% in another.

    File:     The fact find (Category B) records: Client 1 basic income as £1,200 net per month (not £1,100); State Pension as £230 per month (not £230 per week); total net monthly income as £1,430; total monthly expenditure as £1,718 (not £1,700). The fact find income table shows two income lines: Basic Income (CDH Recruitment) £1,200 net monthly and State Pension £230 monthly. The five individual pension values sum to £116,998.47 (£6,601.55 + £211.32 + £3,744.36 + £103,439.24 + £3,002.00), matching neither the £110,000 nor the £106,000 figure in the report. The EA5 charges row in the report is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89 implied) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, making the Standard Life charge comparison unreliable.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net; fact find records £1,200 net per month (Basic Income, CDH Recruitment) — a difference of £100/month.

      - Report states Client 1 receives State Pension of £230 per week; fact find records State Pension as £230 per month — a material frequency mismatch (£230/week = ~£996/month vs £230/month).

      - Report total monthly expenditure stated as £1,700; fact find calculated total monthly household expenditure is £1,718 — a difference of £18/month.

      - Report states combined pension value of approximately £110,000 (page 3); arithmetic sum of all five individual plan values is £116,998.47 — a difference of approximately £6,998.

      - Report states pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 (page 4, capacity for loss); arithmetic sum of all five individual plan values is £116,998.47 — a difference of approximately £10,998.

      - Report uses two different combined pension totals internally: approximately £110,000 (page 3) and approximately £106,000 (page 4) — an internal discrepancy of approximately £4,000.

      - Report states Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution (page 8 narrative); charges table (page 11) shows +0.26% impact for Standard Life — a contradictory figure for the same comparison.

      - EA5 charges row is computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002 — the row is based on the wrong plan value, rendering the Standard Life charge comparison arithmetic unreliable.

      - Report refers to a mid growth rate of 2.94% in one place and 2.95% in the critical yield table — a minor internal inconsistency.

      - Fact find total net monthly income is £1,430; this does not reconcile with the income figures the report states (£300/week + £20/week + £230/week + £1,100/month would be approximately £2,378/month if State Pension is weekly as stated in the report, or approximately £1,650/month if State Pension is monthly as in the fact find at £230/month — neither equals £1,430).

    Several material discrepancies exist between the suitability report and the supporting evidence, and within the report itself.

   

    1. KIM'S INCOME: The report states Kim earns approximately £1,100 per month net, but the fact find (P3) records her net basic monthly income as £1,200. This is a £100/month difference on a named figure — not a rounding issue.

   

    2. STATE PENSION FREQUENCY — CRITICAL: The report states Client 1 receives a State Pension of £230 per week. The fact find (P2) records the State Pension as £230 per month. These use the same cash amount but different frequencies. £230/week annualises to approximately £11,960/year; £230/month annualises to £2,760/year — a difference of over £9,000/year. The full UK State Pension is currently approximately £11,502/year (circa £221/week or £958/month), so the weekly figure is plausible and the monthly figure of £230 is implausibly low. However, this check must rely on the evidence in the file, not external knowledge. The fact find says monthly; the report says weekly — this is a direct frequency mismatch for the same stated amount. The frequency guard (weekly annualises at 52 not 4.33×12) does not explain this away; £230/week and £230/month are simply different figures.

   

    3. EXPENDITURE: The report states expenditure of £1,700/month. The fact find calculated total is £1,718. The difference is £18, which is within the low/rounding category. However the fact find also separately itemises individual expenditure lines summing to £1,718.

   

    4. TOTAL PENSION VALUES — INTERNAL INCONSISTENCY: The report uses £110,000 (page 3) and £106,000 (page 4) as combined pension totals, while the sum of the five individually stated plan values is £116,998.47. None of these three figures agrees with any other. The £110,000 matches the transfer value of only the three plans being switched (£110,185.60), not the total of all five. This is a material internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction.

   

    5. STANDARD LIFE CHARGE: The report narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, implying switching saves 0.08%. The charges table (page 11) shows +0.26% impact for Standard Life, implying the new plan is 0.26% more expensive. These are contradictory directions/magnitudes. Additionally, the derived figures confirm EA5's charge row is computed using Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002 value — meaning the row is arithmetically based on the wrong plan. This is a material error in a cost comparison that supports a transfer recommendation.

   

    6. GROWTH RATE: 2.94% vs 2.95% is a minor rounding difference.

   

    The State Pension frequency mismatch and Kim's income figure both drive the total income picture, which in turn underlies the retirement income needs analysis. The charge figure inconsistency for Standard Life (0.08% vs 0.26%, computed on the wrong fund value) directly affects a cost justification for one of the transfers. These are material concerns that go beyond contextual details.

   

    The gross/net guard does not apply here as both the report and fact find reference net figures. The rounding guard does not explain the £100 income difference or the frequency mismatch.

  [G1.11] Facts used in calculations, cashflow modelling, projections and assumptions match the facts stated in the report narrative

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   Cashflow was modelled to age 100. Assumptions: 0% surplus income saved; general expenditure inflation 2%; savings inflation 2%; state pensions inflation 2.5%; Aviva personal pensions growth 5%; retirement age for critical yield 75; low growth rate 0.00%; mid growth rate 2.95% (also referenced as 2.94% on page 11); high growth rate 5.88%; initial adviser charge £3,305; ongoing annual adviser charge 1.00%; high inflation stress test 6%; market crash stress test -30%. Cashflow inputs include Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002. Expenditure £1,700/month. Kim income £1,100/month. State pension £11,973 p.a. (John) and £11,973 p.a. (Kim from age 67). Cash savings £6,000. ONS life expectancy: male age 69 → 86; female age 65 → 88. No shortfall projected in baseline or any stress test. Combined pension value stated as 'approximately £110,000' on page 3 and 'approximately £106,000' on page 4.

    File:     Intelliflo proposed scenario (P7) shows asset tree with New Aviva pension £110,185, Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211, Savings £6,000, total pensions £116,997. Baseline scenario (P15) confirms pensions total £116,997 across five plans: Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002, Peoples pension £6,601, Scottish Widows £211. Cashflow inputs in suitability report list only Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 — People's Pension and Scottish Widows not listed as cashflow inputs in report narrative. Proposed scenario (P12) confirms 0% surplus income saved, general inflation 2%, state pension 2.5%. Critical yield table (P11) shows mid growth rate 2.95%. Pension switch report (P16/P17) uses mid growth rate 2.95% and also shows 2.94% in one comparison table. Cashflow runs to age 100 confirmed (P1). £1,700 expenditure confirmed (P1). £1,100 Kim income confirmed (P1). £11,973 state pension confirmed (P1). £3,305 initial adviser charge confirmed (P17/P27). 1.00% ongoing adviser charge confirmed (P17). £6,000 savings confirmed (P15). Combined pension value of approximately £110,000 (page 3 of report) and approximately £106,000 (page 4) both contradict the arithmetic sum of £116,998.47. The cashflow modeller asset tree in P7 shows £116,997 as the pension total at plan inception, contradicting both narrative figures.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report page 3 states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000'; arithmetic sum of five individual plans is £116,998.47 and cashflow tool shows £116,997 — a difference of approximately £7,000.

      - Report page 4 (Capacity for Loss) states 'pension funds totalling approximately £106,000'; arithmetic sum is £116,998.47 and cashflow tool shows £116,997 — a difference of approximately £11,000.

      - Report page 3 figure (£110,000) and page 4 figure (£106,000) differ from each other by £4,000 within the same document.

      - Report cashflow section (page 14) lists only three pension inputs (Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002); the cashflow tool asset tree (P7) includes People's Pension £6,601 and Scottish Widows £211 in the opening pension values — it is ambiguous whether these were modelled or omitted.

      - Mid growth rate stated as 2.95% in critical yield table (page 18) but as 2.94% in page 11 narrative — minor internal inconsistency.

      - The EA5 (Standard Life) charge row in the charges table is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), as confirmed by the derived figures; any comparison using that row is unreliable.

      - Standard Life recommendation narrative (page 8) states plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (saving); charges table (page 11) shows impact of '+0.26%' (increase) for Standard Life — contradictory.

      - Performance chart labels A and D are swapped between the two charts on page 13 (first chart: A=Aviva, D=Zurich; second chart key: A=Zurich, D=Aviva).

    Multiple material internal inconsistencies are present and are confirmed by both the derived figures and the cashflow tool evidence. (1) The combined pension total is stated inconsistently across the report (£110,000 on page 3, £106,000 on page 4) and neither figure matches the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values (£116,998.47) or the cashflow tool opening value (£116,997 per P7/P15). These are material figures that appear in the narrative and capacity-for-loss sections. The £110,000 figure appears to coincide with the transfer value of only the three switching plans (Aviva + Zurich + Standard Life = £110,185.60), suggesting page 3 may have used transfer-only values but labelled them as combined — this is a genuine factual mismatch, not a rounding difference. The page 4 figure of £106,000 cannot be reconciled with any sub-total from the file. (2) The cashflow inputs section of the report lists only the three switching pensions, while the cashflow tool itself (P7) includes all five pensions in the opening asset tree. The report therefore does not disclose that People's Pension (£6,601) was included in the cashflow model, creating an unsupported assertion that the model ran only on the three transferred plans. (3) The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid growth rate discrepancy is minor (likely rounding) but is a genuine internal inconsistency recorded in the extraction. (4) The EA5 charge row using Zurich's fund value instead of Standard Life's is confirmed by derived figures; this undermines the Standard Life charge comparison and is consistent with the narrative/table contradiction on whether the Standard Life plan is cheaper or more expensive than the new solution (0.08% saving stated in narrative vs +0.26% shown in table). (5) Performance chart label swap is a presentation inconsistency. The guards do not explain these mismatches: the cashflow was run (so the 'never run' guard does not apply); no as-at date change explains a £7,000–£11,000 variance in the same document; the property modelling guard is not relevant here. The most material findings are the contradictory combined pension totals (driving the recommendation framing and capacity for loss) and the Standard Life charge arithmetic error (driving the cost-saving rationale). These meet the threshold for Potential Concern.

CHK-002 — Objectives, Needs and Time-Horizon Alignment

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G2.1, G2.2, G2.3, G2.4, G2.5, G2.6, G2.7). The report states household expenditure of 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the fact find records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 — a minor variance but the report rounds down. The report assigns a formal priority rank (1, 2, 3) to the three objectives; no explicit priority ranking is recorded in the fact find or file notes — the ordering is implicit in the fact find narrative only. The file note (C) records a 10-year investment growth horizon ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'), whereas the report's retirement objective states retirement within two years — these timeframes are not explicitly reconciled in the report, though they could relate to different aspects (income drawdown vs underlying investment term). 3 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G2.1] Stated goals and their priority order

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Three objectives stated and ranked: (1) Maintain current lifestyle and afford ongoing household expenditure of approximately £1,700 per month throughout lifetime; (2) Retire within two years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) Consolidate pension arrangements and ensure appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a streamlined approach rather than maintaining five separate plans. All three are marked as client-stated.

    File:     Fact find (B) records: retirement planning as the advice area; goal details stating 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' Household expenditure recorded at £1,718 per month. File note (C) records the client is not reliant on pension funds and wishes them to have best growth potential over a 10-year period. Cashflow modelling (F) shows retirement event for John at age 71 in 2027 (i.e. approximately two years from the 2025 advice date). The suitability report itself (I) repeats the same three objectives. No explicit priority ranking is recorded in the fact find or file notes beyond the ordering implicit in the fact find narrative.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states household expenditure of 'approximately £1,700 per month'; the fact find records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00 — a minor variance but the report rounds down.

      - The report assigns a formal priority rank (1, 2, 3) to the three objectives; no explicit priority ranking is recorded in the fact find or file notes — the ordering is implicit in the fact find narrative only.

      - The file note (C) records a 10-year investment growth horizon ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'), whereas the report's retirement objective states retirement within two years — these timeframes are not explicitly reconciled in the report, though they could relate to different aspects (income drawdown vs underlying investment term).

      - The 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' column in the policy research document (G) is blank for all switching and retention recommendations, meaning no individual recommendation is explicitly linked to a named objective in the supporting file.

    The three objectives stated in the report are substantively corroborated by the evidence. The fact find (B/P12) records the retirement income target of £1,700 net per month, the two-year retirement horizon, and the consolidation goal in the client's own words. The suitability report (I/P2) reproduces these consistently. The cashflow model (F/P8) shows retirement modelled in 2027, consistent with the two-year horizon from the 2025 advice date. The expenditure figure discrepancy (£1,700 vs £1,718) is immaterial and attributable to rounding; it does not affect suitability. The absence of an explicit written priority ranking in the fact find is a low-level concern — the objectives do not materially compete (consolidation serves the retirement goal; the lifestyle goal is the income target restated), so the absence of a recorded priority order does not undermine suitability. The 10-year investment horizon referenced in the file note (C/P5) for growth purposes is not contradicted by the two-year retirement target: the client is moving into drawdown (not annuity), so a longer underlying investment horizon is consistent. However, the report does not explicitly reconcile these two timeframes, which could benefit from clarification. The blank 'objective achieved' column in the policy research (G/P36) means the link between individual recommendations and stated objectives rests on boilerplate in the suitability report rather than contemporaneous file evidence — this is a low-to-moderate concern under the 'recommendations linked to objectives only by boilerplate' test. No vulnerability factors in the evidence materially affect this objectives assessment. Overall, the core objectives are evidenced and consistent; the concerns are the missing explicit priority ranking and the absence of documented recommendation-to-objective linkage in the supporting file.

  [G2.2] Retirement target date and investment time horizon details

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The canonical model records two intended retirement ages: 71 and 66. One objective has a termYears of 2 years (short-term). The timeHorizon values across objectives are LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm. No targetDate or targetAge fields were extracted from the report.

    File:     The intelliflo planning documents (P10, P16) show two clients: JS with a 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027, and KS with a 'Retire' event at age 66 in 2026. The policy research sheet (P34) records: 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month.' The risk profile report (P11) records the investment period as 2 years and investment of £106,000. The pension switch report (P1) uses a retirement age of 75 throughout its illustrations for all existing and new plans. The Zurich policy info (P15) records a selected retirement age of 65 and a planned retirement age of 75. The Standard Life illustration (P20) illustrates to Normal Retirement Date (age 69+ already passed). The Aviva policy info (P12) states benefits must be taken before age 75. The cashflow modelling (P18, P19) runs to age 104/100.

    Discrepancies:

      - The pension switch report (P1) uses retirement age 75 for all switch illustrations, whereas the report asserts retirement ages of 71 (JS) and 66 (KS) — a gap of 4 years for JS and 9 years for KS.

      - The risk profile report (P11) records an investment period of 2 years and an investment of £106,000, which aligns with the 2-year termYears in the canonical model, but the pension switch report projects to age 75 (approximately 6 years from the client's current age of 69), creating a conflict between the ATR-justifying horizon (2 years) and the switch illustration horizon (6 years to age 75).

      - The Zurich policy info (P15) lists a selected retirement age of 65 (already past) and a planned retirement age of 75, neither matching the report's stated retirement age of 71 for JS — though this is a contractual default, not necessarily the client's intended date.

      - One objective is classified as LongTerm in the canonical model while the client is described as retiring in approximately 2 years; LongTerm classification is not reconciled with the short planning horizon stated elsewhere.

      - The retirement age of 71 for JS appears nowhere corroborated in the figure-match check (nearest match is 55 in P15, 22.5% away), though it is supported qualitatively by the intelliflo planning timeline in P10 and P16.

      - The cashflow modelling runs to age 104/100 but no explicit long-term investment objective is articulated in the report to justify a LongTerm horizon classification alongside the 2-year term.

    The report asserts retirement ages of 71 (JS) and 66 (KS), and these are qualitatively corroborated by the intelliflo planning documents (P10, P16) which show 'Retire (John) age 71 in 2027' and 'Retire (Kim) age 66 in 2026'. The 2-year termYears in the canonical model is also supported by the policy research sheet (P34) ('retire in the next 2 years') and the risk profile report (P11) ('Investment Period: 2 years'). So far these elements are consistent. However, a material tension exists: the pension switch report uses age 75 as the illustration retirement age throughout (P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P7), which is 4 years beyond JS's stated retirement age of 71. The guard acknowledges that the switch analysis routinely uses a different figure from the fact find, and that a plan's contractual retirement age (here 75) is not the client's intended date. Under the guard, this divergence is expected and not automatically a finding on its own. The more significant concern is the conflict between the ATR-justifying horizon and the switch strategy horizon: the risk profile was set using a 2-year investment period (P11), yet the switch recommendations project growth and assess critical yields to age 75 (approximately 6 years away). A risk profile justified on a 2-year horizon but applied to a pension consolidation illustrated over 6 years is a mismatch between the term used to justify the risk profile and the term used to justify the strategy — this is the 'High' severity scenario identified in the decision logic. Additionally, one of the three objectives is classified as LongTerm with no explicit supporting goal or horizon to justify that classification alongside the predominant 2-year/short-term framing. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to affect this specific horizon/time analysis based on available evidence. On balance, the 2-year ATR horizon versus the 6-year switch illustration horizon is a material tension that requires clarification.

  [G2.3] Access requirements — emergency, planned and ad-hoc

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report asserts two access needs: (1) an emergency fund of £6,000 held in cash savings, confirmed as sufficient by the client to cover unexpected emergencies; (2) a planned access note that pension fund withdrawals or transfers will usually take 10–15 working days for administration, disinvestment and banking processes, and the client is comfortable with this because they hold liquid cash for instant access needs. No ad-hoc withdrawal requirements beyond these are stated.

    File:     The fact find (category B) records cash savings of £6,000. The cashflow planning documents (category F) confirm £6,000 in a Cash Account (Savings) owned by JS as an opening liquid asset in both the baseline and proposed scenarios, and the proposed scenario asset statement shows Liquid Assets of £6,000 at 2025 (age 69/65). No evidence of any additional access or liquidity requirement beyond the emergency cash buffer is recorded in the file. The report's statement that the client confirmed the emergency fund sufficient and is comfortable with pension access timescales is not independently corroborated in the fact find or any category B/D document, but the factual amount is supported.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's assertion that the client 'confirmed' sufficiency of the emergency fund and comfort with pension access timescales is self-referential (suitability report asserting client confirmation) and is not corroborated by any fact find entry, client agreement or meeting note in categories B, D or E. No fact find field records this confirmation.

      - No ad-hoc or irregular access requirements are documented anywhere in the file; while the report does not assert any, the check requires that access needs have been positively established rather than simply omitted — the fact find does not contain a field confirming no further access requirements were identified.

    The £6,000 emergency fund figure is directly corroborated by the fact find ([P19]) and the cashflow planning files ([P1], [P2], [P5]), so the factual basis of the assertion is supported. The report's characterisation of the fund as sufficient and the client's comfort with 10–15 working day pension access timescales are asserted in the suitability report itself but are not independently evidenced in any supporting document in the pack. This is a moderate concern: the client-confirmation language ('you have confirmed') lacks a corresponding fact find entry or file note to corroborate it. However, the underlying factual position (£6,000 liquid cash, pensions accessible subject to standard timescales) is evidenced and internally consistent across all planning documents. The absence of a corroborating client confirmation does not rise to the highest severity levels (no locked capital against a known access date, no conflict between investment term and goal horizon) but does represent an objectives/needs evidencing gap. No vulnerability indicators in the pack materially affect this specific finding.

  [G2.4] Foreseeable life changes

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts five foreseeable life changes/planning assumptions: (1) John expects to retire within the next two years (estimated ~October 2027), ceasing HGV driver and Sea Cadets income; (2) Kim intends to continue working until age 66 then retire fully, at which point her employment income will cease and her State Pension will commence; (3) the household retirement income target is £1,700 net per month; (4) from 6 April 2027, unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; (5) the minimum pension access age rises from 55 to 57 from 6 April 2028. The report also states John is currently aged 69, Kim is aged 65, and current household expenditure is approximately £1,700 per month.

    File:     [P6] (suitability report page 3) confirms: 'You expect to retire within the next two years, while Kim intends to continue working until she reaches 66, at which point she plans to retire fully.' [P9] (Fact Find) confirms the client 'Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month' and records a Regular Income (Today's Money) of £1,700.00. [P8] (proposed scenario cashflow tool) shows John's 'Retire' event at age 71 in 2027 and Kim's 'Retire' event at age 66 in 2026 — consistent with the report's assertions about John retiring in ~2 years and Kim retiring at 66. [P1] (baseline cashflow tool) also shows John 'Retire' age 71 in 2027 (not age 69+2=71, consistent) and Kim 'Retire' age 66 in 2026. [P19] (Fact Find expenditure) records calculated total monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00, close to but not exactly £1,700 as stated in the report. [P9] also confirms a retirement income target of £1,700 per month and a 'Short Term' time horizon. The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) and the minimum pension access age change (6 April 2028) are legislative facts noted in the report and are not client-specific evidence items per the guard. No specific evidence in the file contradicts these legislative references. [P8] notes minimum pension age as 55 for both JS and KS in the cashflow tool (not yet updated to 57), which is a minor inconsistency with the report's forward-looking statement.

    Discrepancies:

      - The Fact Find [P19] records total monthly household expenditure as £1,718.00, whereas the suitability report states 'approximately £1,700 per month' — a £18/month difference. The report uses £1,700 as both the current expenditure figure and the retirement income target, conflating two slightly different figures.

      - The cashflow tool [P8] records the minimum pension age as 55 for both JS and KS, whereas the report states the minimum pension access age is rising to 57 from 6 April 2028 — the cashflow model does not appear to reflect this legislative change.

      - The cashflow tool [P8] shows Kim's retire event at age 66 in 2026, which implies Kim retires in 2026. The report states Kim intends to work until age 66. Kim's DOB is 07/05/1960 [P8], making her 66 in May 2026. This is consistent, but the cashflow also shows John retiring at age 71 in 2027, which is consistent with 'within the next two years' from report date October 2025 (John DOB 07/06/1956, age 71 in June 2027). No discrepancy here on John, but the cashflow shows Kim retiring at 66 in 2026 and receiving State Pension at age 66, whereas the report states Kim 'will receive her full State Pension of around £230 per week when she reaches State Pension age' — the current UK State Pension age for Kim's cohort is 66, consistent.

      - The Fact Find [P9] records the time horizon as 'Short Term', which is potentially inconsistent with a two-year accumulation horizon followed by a long drawdown period; the report does not explicitly characterise the investment time horizon as short-term.

    The principal foreseeable life changes asserted in the suitability report are well-supported by the evidence. John's planned retirement within two years is confirmed by the Fact Find [P9] and the cashflow modelling tool [P8], which records a 'Retire' event for John at age 71 in 2027. Kim's planned retirement at age 66 is confirmed by [P6] and [P8]. The household retirement income target of £1,700 net per month is confirmed by [P9] and [P3]. The IHT legislative change (6 April 2027) and minimum pension access age change (6 April 2028) are legislative facts applicable to all clients and are covered by the guard — they are not client-specific foreseeable changes requiring bespoke client evidence. The minor discrepancy between the Fact Find expenditure figure of £1,718 [P19] and the report's stated £1,700 is immaterial given the report uses the word 'approximately' and the difference is within 2%; the report appears to have rounded, which is acceptable. More notable is that the cashflow modelling tool [P8] still records the minimum pension access age as 55 for both clients, having not been updated to reflect the legislated change to 57 from April 2028. This is a gap in the cashflow model rather than a direct contradiction of the report's assertion. The Fact Find's characterisation of the time horizon as 'Short Term' [P9] sits in some tension with the long-term nature of pension drawdown planning, but the report itself does not state a specific investment term label. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence pack that would materially affect this assessment — the clients are described as being in good health [P6] and no vulnerability indicators are noted. Overall, the foreseeable life changes and objectives stated in the report are substantively corroborated by the evidence, with only minor and explicable differences. No material unsupported assertion is identified.

  [G2.5] Conflicting goals

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report records three objectives ranked 1, 2, 3 in priority order: (1) to ensure the client can continue to afford their lifestyle through their lifetime; (2) to retire in 2 years' time with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) to have a clearer understanding of pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement, preferring a streamlined approach across fewer providers. No target date or target amount is extracted from the model for any objective. The recommended strategy consolidates three pensions into a new Aviva Platform pension invested in two balanced multi-asset funds, with a critical yield horizon to age 75 and an ongoing adviser charge of 1% p.a. The cashflow modelling uses a retirement age of 75 for the primary client (JS, born 07/06/1956, currently ~69) but the suitability report states retirement in '2 years' time', implying retirement at approximately age 71, not 75.

    File:     [P4] confirms the three objectives as stated. The cashflow model (proposed scenario, [P11]) records JS retiring at age 71 in 2027 and KS retiring at age 66 in 2026. The critical yield table ([P8], [P32]) uses age 75 as the maturity/retirement age for the pension comparison. The cashflow baseline ([P9]) shows pensions becoming accessible from 2026/2027 onwards. The fund recommendation ([P3]) references 'retirement objectives' without specifying a horizon. The investment pathway section ([P6]) notes the client requires a tailored solution reflecting 'withdrawal requirements'. No explicit access date conflicts are recorded in the file notes, and no competing capital claim between objectives is visible in the evidence.

    Discrepancies:

      - The critical yield and pension switch comparison use age 75 as the planning horizon ([P8], [P32]), but the stated retirement objective is in '2 years' time' (i.e. approximately age 71 per [P4], corroborated by [P11] which records JS retiring at 71 in 2027). This means the growth horizon used to justify the recommended strategy (age 75) is 4 years longer than the client's stated retirement date (age ~71), creating a potential mismatch between the term used to justify risk and the actual access date.

      - No target date or target amount is attached to any of the three objectives in the canonical model, making it impossible to confirm whether the planning horizon fully aligns with each objective or whether any two objectives compete for the same capital pool.

      - The cashflow modelling retirement event for JS is age 71 (2027) per [P11], but the critical yield assessment targets age 75 per [P8] — these two documents use different planning end-points for the same client.

    The structural conflict test requires examining whether (a) two objectives claim the same capital, or (b) a growth horizon used to justify the strategy outlasts a stated access date. On (a): the three objectives do not appear to compete for the same capital — objective 1 (lifestyle sustainability) and objective 2 (retirement income) are complementary, and objective 3 (consolidation/clarity) is a structural preference rather than a capital claim. No finding on (a). On (b): the report states the client wants to retire in 2 years (approximately age 71, corroborated by [P11] which records a retirement event at age 71 in 2027 for JS). However, the critical yield table ([P8]) and the pension switch report ([P32]) both use age 75 as the planning horizon — the growth rate needed to justify the transfer is calculated to age 75, not to the client's stated retirement age of ~71. This is a material discrepancy: the term used to justify the risk profile and the cost of switching (4.8% critical yield at mid-growth to age 75) is 4 years longer than the client's stated access date. If the client intends to access income at age 71, the shorter accumulation period would produce a more demanding critical yield and a less favourable cost-benefit position than presented. This is a genuine structural concern — the investment horizon justifying the strategy outlasts the stated goal horizon by approximately 4 years. The vulnerability overlay does not alter this conclusion: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would change the analysis of this specific horizon mismatch.

  [G2.6] Recommendations are appropriate for the stated goals given access needs, term and foreseeable changes

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report identifies three objectives: OBJ2 (long-term) and OBJ3 (short-term, appearing twice). All five recommendations are linked to either OBJ2 and OBJ3 or OBJ3 alone. The sole recommended plan is a Personal Pension (Aviva Pension Portfolio, NP1) with a transfer value of £110,185, selected retirement age of 75, no regular contributions, and Flexi Access Drawdown as the intended access mechanism. The client is stated to want £1,700 per month income in retirement, with state pension income expected to cover bills in retirement so that the pension can keep growing for large ad hoc spends. The cashflow runs to age 100 with no projected shortfall. The fund recommendation (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% / CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%) is described as aligned with retirement objectives and risk profile. The report acknowledges the new plan is 4.24% more expensive than existing plans including adviser fees, and the critical yield at mid growth is 4.8%. The report states the client is 69 years old (retirement age 75 implies a 6-year term). Flexi Access Drawdown is noted as preferred for flexibility of access.

    File:     The fact find (P24) records total monthly household expenditure of £1,718 and total net monthly income of £1,430, giving a monthly deficit of -£288, with no expected changes in expenditure. The policy research note (P1) records the client needs £1,700 net per month income in retirement. The cashflow model (P10) confirms: state pension income of £11,973 p.a. covers bills in retirement; pension is expected to grow for large ad hoc spends; annual retirement expenditure is approximately £1,700; no shortfall is projected to age 100; the cashflow includes Aviva transfer £3,744, Zurich transfer £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 but not the People's Pension (£6,601.55) or Scottish Widows (£211.32). The Zurich policy information (P21, P23) shows a retirement date of 7 June 2031 (age 75), consistent with the report. The Aviva existing plan (P22) confirms access from age 55 (57 from April 2028), with no restriction on the client aged 69. The pension switch report (P15, P17) confirms critical yield at mid growth of 4.8% to age 75, and the new plan being materially more expensive including charges. The fund recommendation (P13) states the investment strategy is based on retirement objectives in isolation as requested, with a diversified multi-asset approach. The intelliflo proposed scenario (P12) states the plan is forward-looking and in real terms. No vulnerability indicators affecting objectives or access needs are evidenced in the retrieved passages. The People's Pension (P19) is retained (not transferred) and is not included in the cashflow model per P10.

    Discrepancies:

      - The cashflow model (P10) explicitly lists only three pension inputs (Aviva £3,744, Zurich £103,439, Standard Life £3,002 = £110,185), omitting the retained People's Pension (£6,601.55) and the Scottish Widows encashment (£211.32). The report does not explain this omission, and it is noted as an ambiguity in the extraction metadata.

      - The report states retirement annual expenditure of approximately £1,700 (P10), while the fact find (P24) records monthly expenditure of £1,718 (approximately £20,616 p.a.). The £1,700 figure in the cashflow appears to be monthly not annual, yet is described as annual in the cashflow narrative — this is internally inconsistent and is not reconciled in the report.

      - The canonical model records the mid growth rate projection as 2.95% in the critical yield table (P14, P15), while the suitability report narrative (P9) states 'based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%' — a minor discrepancy of 0.01% already noted in the extraction metadata.

      - All five recommendations are linked to OBJ2 and/or OBJ3, but the content of OBJ1 is not visible in the canonical extract for this check, and the time horizon array shows [LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm] — meaning two of three objectives are short-term, yet the sole recommended plan is a pension locked until at least age 57 (client is 69, so this restriction does not apply) with a 6-year accumulation term to age 75.

      - The report states 'Your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement. Meaning your pension will be allowed to keep growing for large ad hoc spends' (P10), yet the cashflow projects retirement expenditure of approximately £1,700 — if this is monthly (consistent with the fact find figure of £1,718/month and the policy research note of £1,700/month), the state pension of £11,973 p.a. (approximately £998/month) would not cover monthly bills of £1,700, contradicting the report's narrative assertion.

      - The People's Pension (£6,601.55) is retained but excluded from the cashflow model. No explanation is given in the report as to why a retained asset with material value is excluded from the retirement income projection.

    The report's recommendation of a Personal Pension (Aviva Pension Portfolio) with Flexi Access Drawdown is broadly aligned with the client's stated retirement goal: the client is 69, retirement age is set at 75, there is no access restriction concern (minimum pension age does not bite), and FAD is the preferred mechanism. The product wrapper and access mechanism are appropriate for the stated objectives on their face.

   

    However, there are three material concerns that prevent a clean No Issue finding.

   

    First, the £1,700 retirement expenditure figure is critically ambiguous. The policy research note (P1) and the fact find (P24) both record £1,700 per month as the income need and approximately £1,718 per month as current expenditure respectively. The cashflow narrative (P10) states 'you expect your annual expenditure to be approximately £1,700', which if taken literally would mean £142/month — clearly inconsistent with the fact find. The most likely reading is that £1,700/month was intended, making annual expenditure approximately £20,400. Against this, state pension of £11,973 p.a. (approximately £998/month) would not cover monthly bills, directly contradicting the report's assertion that 'your state pension income will cover your bills in retirement'. If the state pension does not cover bills, the pension fund would need to be drawn down earlier or more heavily than the cashflow model assumes, and the cashflow conclusion of no shortfall to age 100 may be unreliable. This is a material concern going to whether the recommendation can deliver the stated objective.

   

    Second, the People's Pension (£6,601.55, retained) is excluded from the cashflow model. The report does not explain this omission. While individually modest, this asset forms part of the client's retirement pot and its exclusion means the cashflow projection is incomplete, though directionally conservative.

   

    Third, the objective descriptions (OBJ1, OBJ2, OBJ3) are not available in the evidence pack beyond their IDs and time horizons. The linking of all five recommendations to OBJ2/OBJ3 cannot be confirmed as specific rather than boilerplate without the objective text. The guard against boilerplate linking therefore cannot be cleared.

   

    The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid growth rate discrepancy is minor and already flagged in the extraction metadata; it does not affect the suitability conclusion materially.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are evidenced in the retrieved passages for this client that would alter the assessment of objectives, access needs or time horizon alignment.

   

    Overall, the £1,700 annual vs monthly ambiguity — and the consequent potential contradiction of the state pension adequacy assertion — is a material concern that the report does not resolve, and the objective descriptions required to verify non-boilerplate linkage are absent.
