  [G2.7] Goals, assumptions or planning requirements stated in the report that are not supported by the evidence

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts three client-stated objectives: (1) maintain current lifestyle and afford ongoing household expenditure throughout lifetime; (2) retire within two years with a household income of £1,700 net per month; (3) consolidate pension arrangements and ensure they are appropriately structured for retirement. Planning assumptions include: 0% surplus income saved; 2% general expenditure inflation; 2% savings inflation; 2.5% state pension inflation; 5% Aviva personal pensions growth; cashflow runs to age 100; retirement age used for critical yield is 75; low/mid/high growth rates of 0.00%/2.95%/5.88%; initial adviser charge of £3,305; ongoing annual adviser charge of 1.00%. The report states these are client-stated objectives, sourced from the suitability report's 'Your Objectives' section (page 3).

    File:     The fact find (B) records under retirement planning: 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The fact find also records a regular income target of £1,700.00 per month, a time horizon of 'Short Term', and monthly household expenditure of £1,718.00. The file notes (C) confirm the client is not reliant on pension funds and wants best growth potential over a 10-year period. The intelliflo planning scenario (F) is present in the file. The critical yield assumption uses retirement age 75, which is materially inconsistent with the stated retirement objective of retiring within two years (the client's date of birth is 07/06/1956, making him approximately 68-69 at the time of advice, so retiring in two years means approximately age 70-71, not 75).

    Discrepancies:

      - The critical yield calculation uses a retirement age of 75, whereas the stated objective is to retire within two years (approximately age 70-71 given DOB 07/06/1956 and advice date circa September 2025). These are materially different planning horizons used in the same report for what should be the same event.

      - The file notes state the client 'wish for them to have the best growth potential over a 10-year period', implying a 10-year investment horizon, which conflicts with the stated retirement objective of retiring in 2 years and also conflicts with the critical yield retirement age of 75 (approximately 6 years from advice date).

      - The policy research document (G) shows the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for each plan recommendation (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life), meaning recommendations are not linked to specific objectives in the supporting research.

      - The mid growth rate is stated as 2.95% in the critical yield section (page 18) but as 2.94% elsewhere (page 11) — an internal inconsistency noted in the extraction.

      - The cashflow modelling projects to age 100 and uses a 5% growth rate for the Aviva personal pensions, but no source document corroborating the specific assumption values (2%, 2.5%, 5%, 0%) has been retrieved from the file — these appear to be adviser/tool-generated rather than client-stated, which is appropriate for planning assumptions but should be noted.

      - The client's current monthly disposable income is negative (income £1,430 vs expenditure £1,718 = -£288/month deficit), yet the report assumes 0% of surplus is saved; the fact find shows no current surplus to save, which is consistent, but the report does not address how the £1,700/month retirement income target will be met given the current deficit position or the transition to retirement.

    The three core objectives are all evidenced in the fact find (P5) and are consistent with the report's summary — the substance matches even if the wording differs, and the guard permitting summarisation applies. The lifestyle/expenditure objective is supported by the expenditure data in P8. The consolidation objective is explicitly recorded in P5 and P6. The retirement income target of £1,700/month in two years is clearly evidenced in P5.

   

    However, a material concern arises with the critical yield assumption using retirement age 75. The client was born 07/06/1956 and the fact find was completed 19/09/2025, making him approximately 69. Retiring in two years means approximately age 71. The critical yield table uses age 75 — a four-year discrepancy from the stated objective. This is not a minor rounding issue; it materially affects the critical yield calculation and therefore the replacement analysis. This is not explained by any guard: it is not a provider illustration assumption, and the report itself states the objective as retiring in two years.

   

    The file notes reference a 10-year investment horizon ('best growth potential over a 10-year period'), which conflicts with both the 2-year retirement objective and the age-75 critical yield assumption. This suggests the adviser may have applied a longer accumulation horizon for fund selection purposes while the client's actual retirement intention is much shorter — but this tension is not acknowledged or reconciled in the report.

   

    The policy research document (P23) shows blank fields for 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' for every plan. This means the linkage between recommendations and objectives is unsupported in the research file, though the suitability report narrative does connect recommendations to objectives in prose. This is a moderate concern — the recommendations are linked to objectives only at the narrative level without the structured evidence that would normally corroborate client authorship of the linkage.

   

    On vulnerability: no specific vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the evidence that materially affect this check beyond what is already captured.

   

    The planning assumptions (inflation rates, growth rates) appear tool-generated, which is appropriate and expected — the guard confirms provider/tool assumptions are evidenced by the illustration, not by the client. The intelliflo planning scenario (F) is present and would corroborate these.

   

    The most significant finding is the retirement age used in the critical yield (75) contradicting the stated 2-year retirement objective (age ~71), which falls under the high severity band (investment term justifying the risk/critical yield contradicts stated goal horizon).

CHK-003 — Risk, Capacity for Loss and Investment Strategy Match

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G3.2, G3.3, G3.4, G3.5, G3.6, G3.7, G3.9, G3.10). Report's CFL section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000'; the arithmetic sum of all five existing arrangements is £116,998.47 (derived figure); neither the all-plans total nor the switching-plans total (£110,185) matches £106,000. The risk profile tool [P17] used £106,000 as the investment amount for the agreed risk level projection, suggesting £106,000 may have been used as a working figure in the advice process, but it does not reconcile with any stated arrangement total. The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report [P20] and [P5] records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no contemporaneous file note corroborates the detailed CFL reasoning set out in the suitability report. 4 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G3.2] Capacity for loss assessment

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   CFL rated 'Medium', assessed separately from ATR. Basis: John receives full State Pension ~£230/week, HGV driver earning ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week; Kim contributes £1,100/month and will also receive full State Pension; combined income comfortably meets household expenditure of ~£1,700/month; savings ~£6,000; no outstanding liabilities; pension funds totalling ~£106,000 form basis of retirement provision; client confirmed any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living.

    File:     Risk profile report [P20] records the capacity for loss section for the goal with 'No additional notes were recorded'. [P5] likewise records 'No additional notes were recorded' under the Capacity for Loss questionnaire section. [P10] shows the CFL questionnaire responses for John Sullivan dated 18/09/2025 were captured. [P11] corroborates the income and expenditure figures quoted in the report (£230/week State Pension, £300/week net employment, £20/week Sea Cadets, Kim £1,100/month, household expenditure £1,700/month, savings £6,000). The cashflow/planning tool [P1] shows liquid assets of £6,000 and pensions of £116,997 (not £106,000). [P17] shows the risk profile tool used an investment figure of £106,000 for the agreed risk level projection at risk 5. The derived figure from the canonical model confirms the sum of all five arrangements is £116,998.47; the report's CFL section cites £106,000 which matches neither the sum of all arrangements nor the sum of the three switching plans (£110,185).

    Discrepancies:

      - Report's CFL section states pension funds total 'approximately £106,000'; the arithmetic sum of all five existing arrangements is £116,998.47 (derived figure); neither the all-plans total nor the switching-plans total (£110,185) matches £106,000.

      - The risk profile tool [P17] used £106,000 as the investment amount for the agreed risk level projection, suggesting £106,000 may have been used as a working figure in the advice process, but it does not reconcile with any stated arrangement total.

      - The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report [P20] and [P5] records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no contemporaneous file note corroborates the detailed CFL reasoning set out in the suitability report.

      - The CFL questionnaire responses are on file [P10] but the risk profile report's CFL notes section is blank — there is no documented link between the questionnaire answers and the 'Medium' CFL rating or the reasoning in the suitability report.

      - The client confirmation that 'any potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on your day-to-day living' is asserted in the suitability report but is not supported by any separate file note or recorded discussion in the risk profile document.

      - Report page 3 states combined pension value 'approximately £110,000'; CFL section (page 4) states 'approximately £106,000' — these two figures within the report itself are inconsistent with each other and with the arithmetic sum of £116,998.47.

    The report provides a reasonably detailed CFL assessment covering income sources, expenditure, savings and pension assets, and flags it as separately assessed from ATR (isSeparatelyAssessedFromAtr = true). The income and expenditure figures are corroborated by [P11]. However, several concerns arise. First, the pension fund figure cited in the CFL section — 'approximately £106,000' — is inconsistent with the arithmetic sum of the five arrangements (£116,998.47, a derived figure that cannot be recomputed away) and also inconsistent with the report's own page 3 figure of 'approximately £110,000'. The £106,000 figure appears to originate from the risk profile tool [P17], which used it as an input for projection purposes, but that does not make it an accurate representation of the client's pension assets for CFL purposes. An understated asset base in a CFL assessment could affect the robustness of the conclusion. Second, the risk profile report's dedicated CFL notes field is blank [P20][P5] — 'No additional notes were recorded' — meaning the detailed reasoning in the suitability report has no corroborating contemporaneous record in the case file. While the CFL questionnaire responses are on file [P10], there is no documented link between those responses and the 'Medium' rating or the narrative reasoning. The client confirmation of no detrimental impact on living standards is asserted but unsupported by any separate file note. Third, the CFL assessment does not appear to be merely a restatement of ATR — it references specific financial data — so the 'moderate' guard for CFL restated as ATR does not fully apply. However, the absence of corroborating notes for the CFL conclusion and the materially inaccurate pension fund total cited in the CFL rationale constitute a material concern. The overall CFL rating of 'Medium' may be supportable on the underlying facts, but the documented basis for it is incomplete and contains a factual inaccuracy in the pension asset figure. No vulnerability factors have been identified in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment.

  [G3.3] Tolerance for loss and need-level risk

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the client is aware that funds may go up as well as down and that higher risk brings potential for larger drops in value. No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary amount is quantified as a tolerance for loss. The need-level risk path (/objectives[]/needLevelRisk) is absent from the extracted model, meaning the report is silent on any formal need-level risk assessment. The ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the agreed rating was overridden upward to 5 (Balanced), with the report stating the adviser felt the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite and that the client agreed to proceed at risk rating 5.

    File:     [P18] confirms the override narrative: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire.' [P19] and [P26] (Risk Profile Report) show the questionnaire natural risk level was 4 (Cautious Balanced). [P14] confirms the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced) and the investment used in the projection is £106,000. [P26] shows the ATR questionnaire responses including 'I would describe my investment approach as: Cautious', 'I associate the phrase investment risk with the idea of: Caution', 'Compared to other people, I would describe myself as: More cautious', and the anxiety threshold of 'More than £4,000' on a £20,000 investment (i.e. >20%). [P11] shows the Capacity for Loss questionnaire responses on file. [P24] notes that no additional notes were recorded in the capacity for loss or agreed risk level sections of the risk profile report. [P23] includes a stress test: an immediate market crash of -30% combined with 6% inflation showing no shortfall. No specific tolerance for loss percentage or monetary amount is recorded anywhere in the evidence. No need-level risk figure is present in any passage.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report overrides the questionnaire ATR of 4 upward to 5 but provides only a brief narrative justification ('didn't fully reflect your risk appetite') with no documented evidence of a deeper assessment, structured discussion notes, or client-specific rationale beyond the client's stated wish for growth — the risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' for both the agreed risk level and capacity for loss sections ([P24])

      - The ATR questionnaire responses in [P26] are consistently cautious (self-described as cautious, anxiety at >£4,000 loss on £20,000 i.e. >20%, associates risk with caution) yet the agreed rating was moved upward, not downward or maintained — the file evidence does not affirmatively support the upward override

      - No specific tolerance for loss percentage or monetary amount is quantified in either the report or the supporting evidence, despite a stress test modelling a -30% market crash being present in the file ([P23])

      - The need-level risk path is absent from both the report and the evidence — no formal need-level risk assessment is documented anywhere in the pack

      - The capacity for loss section in the risk profile report records 'No additional notes were recorded' ([P24]), meaning the file provides no contemporaneous notes supporting the capacity for loss narrative in the suitability report

    The report asserts a risk rating of 5 (Balanced) following an upward override from the questionnaire result of 4 (Cautious Balanced). This override is confirmed in [P18] and [P19]. However, the file evidence does not substantively support the upward direction of the override. The ATR questionnaire responses in [P26] are uniformly cautious — the client self-describes as cautious, associates risk with caution, and sets an anxiety threshold of more than £4,000 on £20,000 (20%+). The risk profile report records no additional notes in either the agreed risk level or capacity for loss sections ([P24]), so there is no documented rationale beyond the adviser's assertion that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite. The report relies on the client's stated preference for growth and non-reliance on the pension fund, but these are objective-based rationales rather than evidence of a higher genuine risk tolerance. An upward override from questionnaire result with no contemporaneous notes and questionnaire responses that are all cautious-directional is a material documentation gap. On tolerance for loss: the report contains only a generic market risk awareness statement with no quantification. The -30% stress test in [P23] does show modelled resilience, but the report does not connect this to a stated tolerance threshold — the guard acknowledges absence of quantification is a documentation gap rather than a contradiction, but the combination of an unquantified tolerance and an upward ATR override supported only by growth-objective reasoning elevates the concern. Need-level risk is absent from both the report and all file evidence; this path was not extracted because it is not present in the document. No vulnerability flags have been identified in the evidence that materially alter this assessment. Overall, the upward ATR override is the primary concern: it is asserted in the report, the direction is inconsistent with the questionnaire responses on file, and the supporting file records no contemporaneous notes to justify it.

  [G3.4] Time horizon used to support the risk profile

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts an investment term of 10 years (/riskAssessment/perClient[]/investmentTermYears = 10). The objectives time horizons are recorded as LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm (/objectives[]/timeHorizon).

    File:     The risk profile report (P3) shows the investment period used in the risk profiling tool as 2 years, with an investment amount of £106,000. The SIPP research report (P9) lists the agreed risk level as 5. The pension switch report (P12) shows a retirement age of 75 and a time to retirement of 5 years and 6 months for the Standard Life plan. The cashflow modelling (P5) shows JS retiring at age 70 in 2026 (i.e. approximately 1 year from the report date of September 2025) and KS retiring at age 71 in 2027. JS is currently 69 years old and KS is 65.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report asserts a 10-year investment term, but the risk profiling tool (P3) used an investment period of 2 years when producing the agreed risk level projections.

      - The suitability report asserts a 10-year investment term, but the pension switch report (P12) states a time to retirement of 5 years and 6 months for the Standard Life plan (retirement age 75).

      - The cashflow modelling (P5) shows JS retiring in 2026 (age 70, approximately 1 year from report date) and KS in 2027 (age 71), which is inconsistent with a 10-year investment term being used to support a higher risk profile.

      - The objectives are recorded as LongTerm, ShortTerm, ShortTerm — two of three objectives are ShortTerm, which is inconsistent with a 10-year term being applied uniformly across the risk assessment.

      - The risk profile report projection was run on £106,000 over 2 years, yet the agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) is presented as appropriate — the mismatch between the 2-year projection horizon used in the tool and the 10-year term asserted in the report is unexplained.

      - The figure 10 appears in P9 (table of contents page reference '10') but not as a confirmed investment term; it is a page number, so corroboration of the 10-year figure from the canonical model is not substantiated by a passage confirming a 10-year investment term.

    The suitability report asserts a 10-year investment term to support the agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), which is one notch above the questionnaire result of 4. This term is central to the risk override justification: a longer horizon can legitimately support a higher risk rating. However, the evidence materially contradicts this. The risk profiling tool (P3) ran the agreed risk level projection on a 2-year investment period — not 10 years — using £106,000. This is the document that formally records the agreed risk level, and it used a 2-year horizon, not 10. The pension switch report (P12) uses a retirement age of 75 with 5 years and 6 months to retirement, which implies a 5-6 year horizon, not 10. The cashflow model (P5) shows both clients retiring within 1-2 years of the report date. Two of the three recorded objective time horizons are ShortTerm. The guard states that money left invested through retirement can legitimately have a longer horizon than the retirement date — but the report does not say this. There is no explanation in the evidence that the 10-year term represents a post-retirement drawdown phase or that the clients intend to keep funds invested beyond retirement access. The override from ATR 4 to agreed level 5 was justified in part by the adviser's view that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite (noted in the extraction ambiguities), but no documentary explanation links the 10-year term to the risk override. The discrepancy between the 10-year term in the report, the 2-year term in the risk tool, the 5.5-year term in the switch report, and the 1-2 year retirement horizon in the cashflow is material and unexplained. This undermines the evidential basis for both the agreed risk level of 5 and the investment strategy. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability flags in the evidence appear to have a direct bearing on the time horizon assessment, though the absence of a formal vulnerability assessment outcome (noted in extraction) means this cannot be fully confirmed. The primary concern remains the unsupported 10-year term used to justify the risk level.

  [G3.5] Investment strategy and asset allocation

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   Target risk rating 5. Two holdings: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%). Strategy type: BespokePortfolio. Total allocation 100%. Asset allocation detail absent from extracted model.

    File:     Risk profile report [P22] records natural ATR questionnaire score of 4 (Cautious Balanced) with agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced) after interactive discussion. SIPP research report [P10] confirms agreed risk level 5 (Balanced). [P16] states 'The risk mapping of the new portfolio is 5 which matches the client's agreed risk level.' [P8] shows investments as CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (GBP) 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (GBP) 50%. [P11] (suitability report fund table) shows HSBC 50%/risk 5/0.18% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%/risk 5/0.29%. HSBC fund factsheet [P12] states the fund's own HSBC risk profile is rated 3 on HSBC's 1-5 scale. KIID [P3] rates the fund as category 4 on the SRRI 1-7 scale. KIID [P2] states the CT/HSBC fund is classified in category 4 on SRRI 1-7 scale. Defaqto research [P14] lists both funds as risk level 5 investments on Defaqto's scale. The ATR override from 4 to 5 is documented in [P22] with the agreed level recorded as 5 Balanced and a client discussion noted.

    Discrepancies:

      - HSBC fund factsheet [P12] states the fund's own risk profile is rated 3 on HSBC's internal 1-5 scale, whereas the report assigns it risk rating 5 — these are different scales.

      - KIID [P3] rates HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio as SRRI category 4 on the regulatory 1-7 scale, whereas the report assigns it risk rating 5 — again different scales.

      - KIID [P2] rates the fund (CT/HSBC range) as category 4 on the SRRI 1-7 scale, whereas the report assigns risk rating 5.

      - The questionnaire produced a natural ATR of 4; the agreed/override rating is 5 — this upward override is present in the file but requires scrutiny as to justification and client acknowledgement.

      - Asset allocation detail (/solution/investmentStrategy/assetAllocation[]) is absent from the extracted model; the report is silent on underlying asset class breakdown.

    The central question for this check is whether the strategy risk rating of 5 equals the client's agreed rating on the same scale, and whether the fund risk ratings assigned in the report are supported by the evidence. On the agreed portfolio-level risk rating, the evidence strongly supports a rating of 5: [P22] records the agreed risk level as 5 Balanced after an interactive discussion starting from natural ATR of 4; [P10] and [P16] both confirm agreed risk level 5 and that the new portfolio maps to 5. The upward override from questionnaire score 4 to agreed level 5 is documented with a rationale (the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite, the client agreed) and is recorded in the risk profile report [P22], which satisfies the requirement for an evidenced justification and client involvement. This is consistent with the ambiguity flagged in the extraction. The fund-level risk rating of 5 assigned to each fund in the report is corroborated by the Defaqto research [P14] which lists both CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C as risk level 5 investments on Defaqto's scale, and [P16] confirms the portfolio risk mapping is 5. However, the fund factsheet [P12] states the HSBC fund's own risk profile is rated 3 on HSBC's internal 1-5 scale, and the KIIDs [P2][P3] rate both funds at SRRI category 4 on the regulatory 1-7 scale. These are different scales from the firm's client risk scale (Defaqto 1-10 or similar). The guard 'Fund-level risk ratings from a provider are not necessarily on the firm's client risk scale' applies here: the HSBC internal rating of 3/5 and SRRI of 4/7 reflect different measurement frameworks from the firm's Defaqto-based 1-10 client risk scale. The report assigns risk rating 5 to each fund, which matches the Defaqto scale used in the research [P14][P16], not the HSBC proprietary or SRRI scales. This difference in scale is real and listed in discrepancies, but the guard explains why it is not a genuine mismatch: the firm uses a Defaqto-based scale and the research confirms both funds sit at level 5 on that scale. The combined blend guard also applies: [P16] explicitly confirms the portfolio risk mapping is 5. Allocations total 100% (50%+50%) and are corroborated by [P8] and [P11]. OCFs of 0.18% and 0.29% are corroborated by [P11] and [P9]. The absence of explicit asset allocation breakdown in the report is a gap but no passage provides a breakdown that contradicts any stated allocation — the KIID [P2] notes equities 45–70%, bonds 20–55% as the typical range for the HSBC fund, which is consistent with a balanced risk 5 profile. No vulnerability factors are identified in the passages that would materially affect this assessment. Overall the strategy risk rating is supported, the override is evidenced, the fund ratings on the firm's scale are corroborated by Defaqto research, and the scale differences explained by the guard are not substantive concerns. No material unsupported or contradictory statements remain after applying the guards.

  [G3.6] Risk override justification where the agreed rating differs from the questionnaire

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report states the ATR questionnaire produced a rating of 4 (Cautious Balanced), but this was overridden upward to 5 (Balanced). The justification given is: (1) the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite'; (2) the client confirmed he is not reliant upon his pension funds, wishes for best growth potential over a 10-year period, and feels a balanced approach would achieve this goal. The client acknowledgement recorded is: 'You confirmed that you wish to increase your Risk rating to 5- Balanced investor, you are aware that this is higher than your agreed risk rating and are happy to take on this risk with your pension.'

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), consistent with the suitability report. The SIPP research report (category G) also records agreed risk level 5 (Balanced). However, the 'Agreed risk level' notes section in the risk profile report states 'No additional notes were recorded', and the 'Capacity for loss for the goal' section similarly states 'No additional notes were recorded'. There are therefore no contemporaneous file notes documenting the rationale for the override or the client's agreement to it within the risk profiling tool itself. The file note (category C, P11) contains ATR wording that mirrors the suitability report narrative but contains an internal inconsistency: it states 'We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6.' — the final sentence refers to Risk rating 6, not 5, which is inconsistent with both the suitability report and the risk profile report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The file note (P11) states 'You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6' whereas the suitability report (P4) and risk profile report (P2/P6) consistently record the agreed rating as 5. This is a direct numerical conflict within the supporting file.

      - The 'Agreed risk level' notes section in the risk profile report (P1) records 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning the override rationale is not documented within the risk profiling tool itself, only in the suitability report narrative and a draft file note.

      - The 'Capacity for loss for the goal' and 'Capacity for loss questionnaire' sections in the risk profile report (P3, P14) also record 'No additional notes were recorded', meaning no contemporaneous narrative was captured in the risk profiling system to support the override decision.

      - The file note ATR wording (P11) is otherwise near-identical to the suitability report wording (P4), suggesting it may be a draft or template rather than an independent contemporaneous record, which limits its weight as corroborating evidence.

    An override has occurred (questionnaire: 4, agreed: 5), so this group is applicable. The suitability report itself contains a justification and a client acknowledgement, and the risk profile report corroborates the natural rating of 4 and the agreed rating of 5. The SIPP research report independently records the agreed level as 5. So far the override is evidenced directionally. However, two concerns arise. First, the file note in P11 — the closest thing to a contemporaneous adviser note recording the override conversation — contains a clear error: it states the client is 'happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 6', not 5. This directly contradicts every other document in the pack and raises a material question about the reliability of the file note as evidence. It may be a typographical error, but it is nonetheless a discrepancy in the supporting evidence. Second, the risk profile report's dedicated 'Agreed risk level' notes section contains no narrative at all ('No additional notes were recorded'), meaning the override rationale was not captured within the profiling tool at the time of the assessment. The justification appears only in the suitability report narrative itself. Taken together, the override is asserted in the report and partially corroborated by the agreed-level outputs in the risk profile and SIPP research reports, but the contemporaneous file note contains a conflicting risk rating (6 vs 5) and the risk profiling system holds no explanatory notes. This constitutes a material concern: the file does not cleanly and consistently evidence the override justification and client acknowledgement. No vulnerability factors in the evidence appear to materially affect this specific finding. Severity is assessed as High given the questionnaire result has been overridden upward and the supporting file evidence is inconsistent.

  [G3.7] Recommendation or strategy exceeds risk tolerance, taking account of capacity for loss

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) The client's agreed ATR rating is 5 (Balanced), overriding a questionnaire result of 4 (Cautious Balanced). The adviser felt the questionnaire did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite; the client agreed to proceed at risk rating 5. (2) The target risk rating for the recommended investment strategy is also 5. (3) Capacity for loss is rated 'Medium'. The report states the client confirmed potential underperformance would not have a detrimental impact on day-to-day living, referencing income from State Pension (~£230/week), HGV employment (~£300/week net), Sea Cadets role (~£20/week), and partner Kim's employment income (~£1,100/month) plus her future State Pension. The client is not reliant on pension funds. (4) Investment term is 10 years. (5) Recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (50%) and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (50%), both described as 'Balanced' at risk level 5. (6) Loss scenarios referenced in the report: £10,400 at 9.6%, £12,000 at 9.4%, £15,000 at 10%. (7) The report does not assert that the existing arrangements' provider risk ratings are comparable to the client's agreed scale.

    File:     [P3] Risk profile report: The natural/questionnaire risk level is 4 (Cautious Balanced). Following interactive discussion considering natural ATR, capacity for loss and required return, the agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced). [P2/P1] SIPP research report: Agreed risk level confirmed as 5; recommended funds are CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C, each 50%. [P5] Suitability report passage: 'We felt that this didn't fully reflect your risk appetite. We agreed that you are a risk rating 5 rather than a 4 as per the questionnaire. You are happy to proceed with a Risk rating of 5.' Client confirmed awareness this is higher than questionnaire result, not reliant on pension funds, wishes best growth potential over 10-year period, balanced approach to achieve goal. Client aware funds may go up and down. [P7/P18] Pension switch report: 'The client's agreed risk level is 5' for both Zurich and Standard Life switch analyses. [P24] Risk profile report appendix: ATR questionnaire responses scored as 4 (Cautious Balanced). Capacity for loss section: 'No additional notes were recorded.' [P25] File notes: 'Aviva current risk – 2 / Zurich current risk – 7 / Standard Life current risk – 4'. Existing arrangement risk ratings recorded on provider scales not comparable to client's agreed scale (confirmed by canonical model flags isComparableToClientScale: false). [P5] Capacity for loss: income sources enumerated; underperformance would not have detrimental impact; medium capacity for loss rated.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. ATR questionnaire result is 4 (Cautious Balanced) but the agreed/override rating used for the recommendation is 5 (Balanced) — the report acknowledges this divergence and provides a rationale, but this is a material difference between questionnaire output and agreed rating.

      - 2. Capacity for loss section in the risk profile document ([P24]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' for capacity for loss for the goal — no quantitative or narrative capacity for loss assessment is recorded in the risk profiling tool itself, only in the suitability report narrative.

      - 3. The loss scenario figures in the derived figures (£10,400 at 9.6%, £12,000 at 9.4%, £15,000 at 10%) imply fund values of £108,333, £127,660 and £150,000 respectively — none matches the actual transfer value of £110,185 or the total existing plan values, raising a question about what fund value the loss scenarios were computed against.

      - 4. Existing arrangement risk ratings are on provider scales (People's Pension: 9, Scottish Widows: 4, Aviva: 2, Zurich: 7, Standard Life: 4 on Standard Life scale) and are explicitly flagged as not comparable to the client's agreed scale of 5 — no mapping is provided. The report does not claim alignment for retained plans on this scale, but the incomparability means alignment cannot be confirmed.

      - 5. The risk profile document's capacity for loss section records no notes ([P24]), meaning the documented basis for the 'Medium' CFL rating rests entirely on the suitability report narrative, with no corroborating contemporaneous record in the risk profiling tool.

    The central question is whether the recommended strategy (risk rating 5, Balanced, 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced / 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced) exceeds the client's risk tolerance and capacity for loss. On the strategy-to-agreed-rating comparison: both the risk profiling tool ([P3]) and the pension switch research ([P7], [P18]) confirm the agreed risk level is 5, and the recommended funds are explicitly rated and selected at risk level 5 in the SIPP research report ([P1], [P2]). The strategy does not exceed the agreed rating — they match. The override from questionnaire result 4 to agreed rating 5 is the most significant issue. The report in [P5] provides a rationale: the adviser believed the questionnaire did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite; the client agreed and confirmed awareness that 5 is higher than the questionnaire result. The risk profile document ([P3]) formally records the agreed level as 5 following interactive discussion. This is an upward override. The decision logic for this check flags 'High: questionnaire result overridden upward with no evidenced justification.' The justification given in the report is that the client confirmed they are not reliant on pension funds and wish for best growth potential over 10 years. However, the capacity for loss section in [P24] records no additional notes, so the documented basis for the override in the risk profiling tool itself is thin. The rationale exists in the suitability report narrative but is not corroborated by notes in the risk profiling tool. The client acknowledgement is present in [P5]. On capacity for loss: the report rates CFL as Medium and provides a narrative basis (multiple income sources, pension not relied upon). This is supported by [P5]. However, [P24] records no capacity for loss notes in the risk profiling tool, meaning CFL is asserted narratively but not documented as a separate formal assessment with recorded notes. This is consistent with the Moderate severity flag: 'capacity for loss asserted as a restatement of attitude to risk' — the CFL narrative in the report is linked to the client's income position but the risk profiling tool contains no recorded CFL notes, and there is no separate quantitative CFL assessment on file. On the loss scenarios: the derived figures show the implied fund values for the loss percentages do not match £110,185 (the actual transfer value). This means the loss tolerance figures cited in the report are not computed on the actual fund value being invested, raising a concern about whether the loss scenarios presented to the client accurately reflected their actual exposure. On existing arrangement risk ratings: the canonical model flags all as not comparable to the client's scale, and the report does not assert alignment. The guard applies: incompatible scales mean no comparison can be made, and the report makes no alignment claim for these. This is not a finding in itself. No vulnerability information that would materially affect this assessment is present in the evidence pack. Weighing these issues: the upward override is explained and client-acknowledged, but the risk profiling tool's capacity for loss section is empty, the CFL assessment relies entirely on suitability report narrative, and the loss scenario figures are computed on an unidentified fund value rather than the actual £110,185. These collectively represent material gaps in the documentation supporting the override and CFL determination, rather than an outright breach where strategy exceeds tolerance. The outcome is Potential Concern at Moderate/High severity given the upward override with thin documented CFL corroboration.

  [G3.9] Recommended funds and portfolio align with the client's attitude to risk, capacity for loss and investment term

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) ATR questionnaire produced rating 4 ('Cautious Balanced') but the adviser overrode this to an agreed rating of 5 ('Balanced') on the basis it 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite, with the client's agreement. (2) Capacity for loss assessed as 'medium', separately from ATR, based on surplus income (State Pension ~£230/week, HGV earnings ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets £20/week; spouse income £1,100/month; household expenditure ~£1,700/month comfortably met), savings ~£6,000, no liabilities, and client confirmation that underperformance would not detrimentally affect daily living. Pension funds stated as ~£106,000. (3) Investment term: 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: bespoke CIP portfolio — 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5, OCF 0.18%) and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5, OCF 0.29%); target risk rating 5 'Balanced'; benchmark IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares. Rationale: funds individually and combined reflect risk profile 5; diversification; active/passive blend; client preference for simplicity; competitive charges; peer-relative performance; CIP governance. (5) The agreed medium CFL is stated as consistent with agreed ATR level 5.

    File:     [P15] Risk profile report (category D): Natural risk level confirmed as 4 ('Cautious Balanced') from ATR questionnaire completed 18/09/2025. Agreed risk level confirmed as 5 ('Balanced') following interactive discussion considering ATR, CFL and required return. Description of Balanced investor at level 5 matches the report's description. [P6] SIPP research report (category G): Confirms agreed risk level 5 Balanced, with matching description. Notes the agreed level considered natural ATR, CFL and required return. [P12] SIPP research report: Investment table confirms CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C 50% at agreed risk level 5. [P16] Suitability Report (category I): Fund recommendation table confirms HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% risk rating 5 OCF 0.18%, CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50% risk rating 5 OCF 0.29%. [P9] KIID HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio (category H): Fund rates itself as 3 out of 5 on its own internal HSBC OpenFunds risk scale (where 1=lower, 5=higher); SRRI rating is 4. Recommended minimum holding period 5 years or more. [P1] KIID (category H): SRRI rating 4 for the HSBC fund. [P5] KIID (category H): risk and reward indicator referenced without explicit rating visible in passage. [P10] SIPP research report shortlist: Both CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C appear on the risk level 5 shortlist filtered by Defaqto. [P11] SIPP research report MiFID II costs: HSBC ongoing 0.18%, CT Universal MAP 0.29%, consistent with report. Investment term: no specific 10-year investment term confirmation found in the retrieved passages; the KIID recommends a minimum 5-year holding period. The capacity for loss figure of ~£106,000 in pension funds does not match the arithmetic sum of the five plans (£116,998.47) or the narrative figure of ~£110,000 on page 3 of the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states pension funds totalling approximately £106,000 in the capacity for loss section; the arithmetic sum of all five plans is £116,998.47 and the report's own page 3 narrative states approximately £110,000 — neither matches £106,000.

      - The ATR questionnaire result is 4 but the agreed/recommended risk rating is 5; the report states the adviser felt the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite, but no independently documented justification for the upward override beyond that assertion is present in the canonical model (though the risk profile report in D does confirm the agreed level was reached through discussion).

      - The HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio KIID (P9) states the fund is rated 3 out of 5 on the HSBC OpenFunds internal risk scale and has an SRRI of 4, whereas the report assigns it a risk rating of 5 (matching the agreed client profile). These are different scales, but the mismatch between the fund's own internal/SRRI rating and the profiler's rating of 5 requires the guard to be applied.

      - The report states the investment term is 10 years; the HSBC KIID recommends a minimum holding period of 5 years. This is directionally consistent (5 years minimum vs 10-year term) but the 10-year term is not independently corroborated in the retrieved passages.

      - The capacity for loss is described as 'medium' and stated as 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. The assessment basis cites specific financial data (income, expenditure, savings, pension assets) separately from ATR, but the conclusion ties it back to ATR level 5, which introduces an element of circularity.

    The core alignment question — does the recommended portfolio match the agreed risk rating of 5 — is supported by the evidence. The risk profile report (P15, category D) confirms the natural ATR of 4 and the agreed ATR of 5 reached through an interactive discussion process considering ATR, CFL and required return. The SIPP research report (P6, P12) corroborates agreed level 5 Balanced and confirms both recommended funds were selected from the risk level 5 shortlist (P10). The fund risk ratings of 5 assigned in the suitability report (P16) match the agreed client profile. The SRRI and HSBC internal scale discrepancy for the HSBC fund (SRRI 4, internal scale 3/5) is explained by the guard: a fund's SRRI or risk-and-reward indicator runs on its own scale and does not map onto the profiler's scale; these are corroborative rather than directly comparable values. The HSBC fund's minimum recommended holding period of 5 years is shorter than the client's 10-year investment term, which the guard confirms is not a mismatch. The upward override from ATR 4 to agreed 5 is recorded in both the suitability report and the risk profile report (P15), and the process description in P6 and P15 provides a methodology basis (interactive discussion, projections, CFL and required return considered). However, the sole stated justification in the report — that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite — is sparse; no specific factors are documented to explain why level 5 rather than 4 is appropriate for this client. This is a moderate concern. More materially: the capacity for loss section states pension funds of approximately £106,000, which conflicts with both the arithmetic total (£116,998.47) and the report's own page 3 figure (~£110,000). While this is primarily a data accuracy issue flagged under CHK-001, it affects the CFL assessment here to the extent that the asset base used to assess loss capacity is materially misstated. The CFL conclusion ('medium, consistent with agreed ATR level 5') is supported by the income/expenditure/savings data cited, which is specific to the client, and the client's own confirmation of no detrimental impact. The CFL is separately assessed from ATR in structure, though the conclusion links the two. On balance, the portfolio-to-risk-profile alignment is well evidenced; the upward override has some process basis but minimal client-specific documented justification; the pension asset figure used in the CFL assessment is internally inconsistent. These together constitute a moderate potential concern.

  [G3.10] Unsupported, contradictory or unexplained statements about risk, loss capacity, term or strategy

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) ATR questionnaire produced rating 4 ('Cautious Balanced'); the adviser overrode this upward to 5 ('Balanced') on the basis that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite; the client confirmed agreement and acknowledged the higher risk. (2) Capacity for loss assessed as 'medium', separately from ATR, based on: State Pension ~£230/week, HGV driving income ~£300/week net, Sea Cadets income £20/week, spouse (Kim) contributing £1,100/month plus future State Pension, combined income exceeds household expenditure of ~£1,700/month, savings ~£6,000, no outstanding liabilities, pension funds totalling approximately £106,000, and client confirmation that underperformance would not detrimentally affect day-to-day living. Medium CFL described as 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5'. (3) Investment term stated as 10 years. (4) Recommended strategy: 50% HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (risk rating 5) and 50% CT Universal MAP Balanced C (risk rating 5), both on a 1-10 scale, target risk rating 5, benchmark IA Mixed Investment 20-60% Shares TR in GB, described as reflecting the client's risk profile individually and combined. (5) No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or amount is stated; tolerance for loss limited to a general market-risk awareness statement.

    File:     [P1] and [P8] (Risk profile report): Natural/questionnaire risk level recorded as 4 ('Cautious Balanced'). The agreed risk level recorded as 5 ('Balanced') following interactive discussion considering ATR, CFL and required return; the description of Balanced investor at level 5 matches the report. [P2] and [P5] (SIPP research report): Agreed risk level confirmed as 5 Balanced with identical description. Investments listed as CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc (GBP) 50% and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio Accumulation C (GBP) 50%, consistent with the report. [P16] (Risk profile report, ATR questionnaire responses): Client's questionnaire responses include 'Cautious' investment approach, anxiety threshold 'More than £4,000' fall on £20,000, associates risk with 'Caution', 'More cautious' compared to others — all tending toward caution, supporting the raw score of 4. No written notes recorded explaining the override. [P3] and [P4]: 'No additional notes were recorded' in Investment Target, Agreed Risk Level, Investor Experience and Capacity for Loss questionnaire sections. [P8]: Investment amount used in risk profile tool is £106,000, investment period is 2 years (not 10 years). [P17] (Suitability report passage): Client confirms willingness to proceed at risk rating 5; 10-year growth objective cited as part of the override justification. [P18] (Suitability report, fund recommendation): Funds rated 5 on the same scale, matching agreed ATR of 5. [P22] (CT Universal MAP KIID): Fund classified in SRRI category 4 ('medium fluctuations historically'); asset allocation equities 45–70%, bonds 20–55%. [P5] (SIPP research report): Confirms same two funds at 50/50.

    Discrepancies:

      - The investment period in the risk profile tool (P8) is recorded as '2 years', whereas the suitability report states the investment term is 10 years. This is a direct conflict between the projection tool input and the term stated in the report.

      - The pension fund total used in the report's capacity for loss section is stated as 'approximately £106,000', but the arithmetic sum of the five individual plan values is £116,998.47; the three plans being switched sum to £110,185.60. Neither the '£106,000' figure nor the report's separate '£110,000' narrative figure matches the arithmetic sum. The risk profile tool also uses £106,000 as the investment amount (P8).

      - No written justification notes are recorded in the risk profile tool for the override from rating 4 to rating 5 (P3: 'No additional notes were recorded' in Agreed Risk Level section; P4: 'No additional notes were recorded' in Capacity for Loss section). The report asserts the adviser felt the questionnaire didn't fully reflect the client's risk appetite, but the supporting tool captures no contemporaneous narrative for the override.

      - The ATR questionnaire responses (P16) are consistently cautious in character (self-described as 'Cautious', anxiety at falls over £4,000 on £20,000, associating risk with 'Caution', 'More cautious' than others) and produced a score of 4. The override to 5 is explained only by a general statement that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' risk appetite and a client preference for growth over 10 years, with no specific factor identified that counters the cautious questionnaire responses.

      - The CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund is classified in SRRI category 4 in its KIID (P22: 'This Fund is classified in category 4 because its price or simulated data has shown medium fluctuations historically'), whereas the suitability report assigns it a risk rating of 5 on the firm's 1–10 scale. These are different scales, but the report does not acknowledge or reconcile the discrepancy between the KIID category 4 classification and the firm's rating of 5 applied to the fund.

      - No specific maximum acceptable loss percentage or monetary tolerance for loss is quantified in the report; tolerance for loss is addressed only by a generic market-risk awareness statement.

      - Capacity for loss is described as 'consistent with your agreed Attitude to Risk level of 5', which risks conflating ATR and CFL rather than treating them as independently assessed factors, even though some separate income and asset factors are cited.

    Several issues require consideration after applying the applicable guards. First, the investment period conflict (2 years in the risk tool vs 10 years in the report) is a direct factual discrepancy in the case file. The risk profile tool projections (P8) were run on a 2-year horizon, yet the report bases the override justification and strategy rationale on a 10-year term. This means the projection tool used to arrive at the agreed risk level did not model the actual intended investment horizon, which is a material inconsistency in how the risk level was reached and evidenced. Second, the override from rating 4 to 5 is an upward override. The report explains it by saying the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite and references the client's growth objective and 10-year term. However: (a) the risk profile tool records 'No additional notes were recorded' in both the Agreed Risk Level and Capacity for Loss sections (P3, P4), so there is no contemporaneous documented rationale in the tool itself; (b) the ATR questionnaire responses (P16) are all consistent with a cautious profile — self-described cautious, more cautious than others, anxiety at falls over £4,000 on a £20,000 investment — making the basis for the upward override harder to substantiate; and (c) the growth objective and non-reliance on pensions are client-stated preferences that may inform the agreed level but do not on their own constitute documented evidence that the questionnaire result was incorrect. The override is therefore recorded in the report but not corroborated by notes in the risk profiling tool. Under the decision logic, an upward override with no evidenced justification in the supporting documentation is rated High severity. Third, the SRRI category 4 assigned to the CT Universal MAP Balanced C fund in its KIID (P22) versus the firm's rating of 5 applied to it in the report and research documents is a cross-scale comparison. The guard cautions against treating comparisons across incompatible scales as straightforward mismatches. However, the report asserts that 'the funds reflect your risk profile both individually and combined' and that 'should there be any portfolio drift, the risk being taken remains the same' — these are positive claims of alignment that the KIID classification at least partially undermines, and the report does not acknowledge the difference. This is a gap in the explanation rather than a clear contradiction, but it leaves the alignment claim unsupported for the CT fund. Fourth, capacity for loss: the report does cite specific, client-specific income and asset factors and states these were assessed separately from ATR. The conclusion that CFL is 'consistent with' ATR 5 is stated after the separate factors are listed. While the phrasing risks suggesting CFL merely mirrors ATR, the substance of the assessment does enumerate independent factors. The pension fund value of '£106,000' used in the CFL section is however inconsistent with the arithmetic sum of the five plans (£116,998.47) and also with the '£110,000' used elsewhere in the report — this is a pre-existing internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction metadata, noted here for completeness as it affects the accuracy of the CFL asset statement. Fifth, no specific loss tolerance percentage or monetary amount is quantified. The severity guidance rates this Low where no large-loss scenario is modelled; the report does not model a large-loss scenario, so this is a low-severity gap. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially affect this assessment (the extraction notes absence of vulnerability assessment outcomes for both clients). No amplifying concern on vulnerability grounds arises here. Overall, the most significant issues are: (1) the investment period used in the risk tool (2 years) contradicts the 10-year term stated in the report and used to justify the override — High/Moderate concern; (2) the upward ATR override from 4 to 5 has no contemporaneous written rationale in the risk profiling tool to support it, and the questionnaire responses are consistently cautious — High severity per the decision logic. These are material concerns that are not explained by any guard.

CHK-004 — Knowledge, Experience and Product Complexity

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 7 requirement(s). 7 raised a concern (G4.1, G4.2, G4.3, G4.4, G4.5, G4.6, G4.7). The report describes the client's knowledge as 'Limited', but the fact find questionnaire records multiple answers of 'No understanding / knowledge' against direct questions about pension planning, stocks and shares, and unit trusts — which is below even a 'Limited' knowledge level as those terms are ordinarily understood in a suitability context. The risk profile description used to justify the agreed risk level 5 states 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' — but the file records no investment experience and no understanding of financial matters beyond cash savings, creating a tension between the agreed risk profile narrative and the evidenced knowledge level. No additional adviser notes were recorded in the investor experience section or the capacity for loss discussion section of the risk profile report, meaning there is no documented explanation or contextualisation of the gap between the questionnaire responses ('No understanding / knowledge') and the 'Limited' characterisation in the suitability report. 2 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G4.1] Client's stated product and investment knowledge level

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report records the client's knowledge level as 'Limited'. The report also states in the capacity for loss section that 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements' and you 'understand that returns are not guaranteed'. The client is described as a Balanced (risk 5) investor proceeding with a consolidated Aviva pension SIPP invested in two balanced multi-asset funds (CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc).

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the client's own questionnaire responses as: 'No understanding / knowledge' against questions on stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, and pension planning/income in retirement. Against the general experience question the response is 'No understanding / knowledge'. Against current accounts/savings the response is 'Very little understanding / knowledge'. The client has not invested in unit trusts, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last 5 years. The only investment activity recorded is cash/savings accounts, done 'by myself'. No additional notes were recorded in the investor experience or capacity for loss discussion sections. The suitability report itself acknowledges experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The recommended products are mainstream balanced multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio, risk rated 3 within its own range; CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) held inside an Aviva Personal Pension/SIPP — products of moderate complexity aimed at a balanced investor.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report describes the client's knowledge as 'Limited', but the fact find questionnaire records multiple answers of 'No understanding / knowledge' against direct questions about pension planning, stocks and shares, and unit trusts — which is below even a 'Limited' knowledge level as those terms are ordinarily understood in a suitability context.

      - The risk profile description used to justify the agreed risk level 5 states 'If you have some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters then you may be suited to a Balanced approach' — but the file records no investment experience and no understanding of financial matters beyond cash savings, creating a tension between the agreed risk profile narrative and the evidenced knowledge level.

      - No additional adviser notes were recorded in the investor experience section or the capacity for loss discussion section of the risk profile report, meaning there is no documented explanation or contextualisation of the gap between the questionnaire responses ('No understanding / knowledge') and the 'Limited' characterisation in the suitability report.

      - The ATR questionnaire result was 4 (Cautious Balanced) but was overridden to 5 (Balanced). The Balanced investor description references 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', which is not evidenced by the knowledge questionnaire responses on file.

    The report records the client's knowledge as 'Limited'. The questionnaire evidence in the risk profile report (category D, passage P1 and P10) records responses of 'No understanding / knowledge' across pension planning, stocks and shares, unit trusts, and investing generally, with only 'Very little understanding / knowledge' for cash/savings. These responses sit below what would ordinarily be characterised as even 'Limited' knowledge. The suitability report itself acknowledges experience is 'limited to your workplace pension arrangements' (P15), which is consistent with the questionnaire showing no active investment in pension products in the last five years. There is therefore a discrepancy between the label 'Limited' in the report and the questionnaire-assessed responses which record 'No understanding / knowledge' for the directly relevant product types including pension planning. The guard note is relevant: 'No understanding / knowledge' recorded against a direct question is an assessed answer, not a self-deprecating aside, and a report characterising the same client as 'Limited' (rather than 'None') does not squarely contradict this, but does overstate it marginally. However, the more material concern is the absence of any adviser notes in the investor experience section (P3) or capacity for loss section explaining how the knowledge gap was addressed, how the client was brought to understand the products being recommended, or why a Balanced (risk 5) approach — described as suitable for someone with 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding' — was agreed for a client whose questionnaire records no such experience or understanding. The recommended products (two mainstream balanced multi-asset OEIC/UCITS funds inside an Aviva personal pension) are of moderate rather than high complexity, which mitigates the concern somewhat. Nevertheless, the Balanced investor profile narrative in the risk profile (P2, P12, P14) explicitly references 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters' as a characteristic of suitability, and the file does not evidence either. The ATR was also overridden upward from 4 to 5 (P15), and the justification for the override relies in part on the client's stated comfort with risk — but no adviser notes document how understanding of the higher-risk approach was confirmed. No vulnerability factors are recorded that would materially alter this assessment. The outcome is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: the report overstates the client's knowledge relative to the questionnaire responses, there are no adviser notes documenting how comprehension of the recommended products was established, and the Balanced investor description references experience and understanding that the file does not evidence — albeit the products themselves are not highly complex.

  [G4.2] Previous investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) states: (1) the client has previously taken out cash savings products; (2) the client has not taken out a stocks & shares investment by himself; (3) the client has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work, with a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards.

    File:     The risk profile questionnaire (P1) records: 'Do you understand how pension planning and income in retirement works and do you know the risks associated with them?' — Response: 'No understanding / knowledge'. 'Do you understand how stocks and shares work and do you know the risks associated with them?' — Response: 'No understanding / knowledge'. 'Have you invested in Current Accounts, Savings/Deposit Accounts, Cash ISA, National Savings and Investments within the last 5 years?' — Response: 'Yes, by myself'. 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' — Response: 'No'. The risk profile report (P14) notes a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) and an agreed risk level of 5 (Balanced), with no additional notes recorded for investor experience (P7). The fact find (P15/P17) records cash savings of £6,000. The file confirms the client holds multiple workplace/group pension plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, People's Pension, Scottish Widows) as evidenced by policy documents (P8, P9, P2, P3).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' in relation to holding group pensions; however, the risk profile questionnaire (P1) records 'No understanding / knowledge' in response to the direct question about understanding pension planning and income in retirement and the associated risks. These are directly contradictory.

      - The report does not describe the client as having no understanding of pension planning, whereas the questionnaire explicitly records 'No understanding / knowledge' for that topic — the report's characterisation of 'reasonable understanding' is not supported and is contradicted by the formal questionnaire response.

      - The risk profile questionnaire (P1) records 'Have you invested in pension planning and income in retirement products within the last 5 years?' as 'No', whereas the client demonstrably holds multiple pension plans; however this is a questionnaire response and may reflect no active investment decisions rather than non-ownership — this is a secondary discrepancy.

      - No additional notes were recorded in the investor experience section of the risk profile report (P7), meaning there is no documented elaboration or override explaining why the 'No understanding / knowledge' responses were departed from in the suitability report's more positive characterisation.

    The central issue is a direct contradiction between what the suitability report asserts about the client's understanding of pension planning and what the formal risk profiling questionnaire records. The report states the client is 'comfortable with how investments work' and has 'a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards', grounding this in his long-term holding of group pensions. The risk profile questionnaire (P1), however, records 'No understanding / knowledge' to the explicit question about understanding pension planning and income in retirement. This is not a nuanced or borderline difference — it is a direct contradiction between the report's positive characterisation and the questionnaire's lowest possible rating. The applicable guard notes that 'a client recorded as having no understanding of pension planning is the material answer where the recommendation is a pension'. The recommendation here is a pension consolidation into an Aviva Pension Portfolio — a self-invested pension with multi-fund investment capability — which is a moderately complex product. The report's reliance on passive holding of group pensions to infer 'reasonable understanding' is not supported by the questionnaire evidence; the guard also notes that 'plans held do not prove understanding'. The report's statement that the client has not taken out a stocks and shares investment is consistent with P1 (which records 'No' to recent stocks and shares investing and 'No understanding / knowledge' of how stocks and shares work). The cash savings experience is corroborated by P1 ('Yes, by myself' for savings/deposit/cash ISA products) and P17 (£6,000 cash savings in the fact find). There is no vulnerability recorded in the file that bears on this check. The key finding is that the report overstates the client's pension knowledge relative to the file: it asserts reasonable understanding where the questionnaire records no understanding, and does so without any documented override or additional adviser notes (P7 records 'No additional notes were recorded' for investor experience). This constitutes a material discrepancy warranting a Potential Concern.

  [G4.3] Complexity of recommended products and investments, including risks and features

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (inferred, not explicitly stated) classifies the recommended product — an Aviva Platform Personal Pension (Flexi-Access Drawdown) — as complex, driven by: pension transfer from multiple existing arrangements; Flexi-Access Drawdown with flexible income withdrawal; investment into multi-asset funds with ongoing management; and IHT implications from April 2027. The report states the client understands pension freedoms and prefers the flexibility of FAD. Death benefit options (pre/post age 75 tax treatment) and the IHT change from 6 April 2027 are explained. The product is a personal pension consolidating three existing arrangements into the Aviva Pension Portfolio with a total transfer value of £110,185, invested in multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) at an agreed risk level of 5.

    File:     The risk profile report (P35) records the client's natural ATR questionnaire result as 4 (Cautious Balanced) with an agreed/overridden level of 5. The report describes the Cautious Balanced investor as someone who 'tend[s] to see yourself as quite a cautious person and are inclined to view risk negatively rather than as a source of opportunity' and notes 'If you have limited or moderate experience of investments'. The file notes (P38) evidence the adviser researched alternative options across Zurich, Aviva and Standard Life and considered fund suitability, charges and consolidation rationale. The Key Features document (P20) describes the Aviva Pension Portfolio's aims, flexibility and drawdown options. The suitability report (P31) states 'You understand the pension freedoms and prefer the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown.' The suitability report (P4) provides a detailed explanation of FAD mechanics, lifetime allowance, annual allowance, and IHT treatment. Product literature (P1, P20, P22) is on file covering drawdown risks, investment risks and retirement options. KIIDs (P23, P24, P26) and fund factsheets (P8, P9, P29) for the recommended funds are on file. However, no fact-find or client-facing knowledge-and-experience questionnaire is present in the retrieved passages to independently corroborate the client's stated investment knowledge level or prior experience with pensions/investments beyond what the suitability report itself asserts. The risk profile report's characterisation of the client as a Cautious Balanced investor with potentially 'limited or moderate experience' sits in mild tension with recommending a complex consolidation/drawdown product, though the adviser's override to risk level 5 and the detailed file notes show engagement with suitability. No vulnerability indicators material to this check are identified in the file.

    Discrepancies:

      - The risk profile report describes the Cautious Balanced (ATR 4) profile as potentially having 'limited or moderate experience of investments', yet the suitability report asserts the client understands pension freedoms and FAD without a separate knowledge/experience record in the file to corroborate this.

      - The product complexity is inferred by the extractor (not explicitly stated in the report), meaning the report itself does not formally grade or articulate the complexity level of the recommended product.

      - No standalone fact-find or knowledge-and-experience section from a client questionnaire appears in the retrieved passages to independently verify the client's investment knowledge and experience level beyond the suitability report's own narrative assertions.

    The recommended product — a personal pension with FAD and multi-asset fund investment — is a mainstream personal pension wrapper. Per the guard, a mainstream personal pension in multi-asset funds is not to be escalated on product volume or value alone. However, the specific features present here (FAD flexibility, pension consolidation from multiple arrangements, IHT planning from April 2027, and the override of the ATR questionnaire from 4 to 5) introduce complexity beyond a simple accumulation pension. Product literature is on file (Key Features, T&Cs, KIIDs, fund factsheets), which satisfies the requirement that product literature be present. The suitability report itself provides a reasonably detailed explanation of FAD, IHT implications, death benefits and fund selection rationale (P4, P31). The file notes (P38) show the adviser considered and discounted alternatives. The discrepancy that matters for this check is the absence of an independent knowledge-and-experience record: the risk profile document (P35) characterises the natural ATR-4 profile as one with potentially limited or moderate investment experience, and no separate fact-find passage corroborates the report's assertion that the client understands pension freedoms and FAD. The ATR override itself is out of scope for this check unless relied upon to evidence knowledge — here it is not directly relied upon for that purpose. The complexity classification is inferred rather than stated, and the rationale, while present in the report, rests entirely on the report's own assertions without independent corroboration of knowledge/experience. This is a moderate concern: no record of client questions or explanations specific to the client's knowledge level being graded or verified, and the risk profile's language about 'limited or moderate experience' is not reconciled in the file. No vulnerability factors material to this check are evidenced.
