  [G4.4] Recommended product is too complex for the stated level of knowledge, or the knowledge level has been misinterpreted

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts the client (John) has 'Limited' knowledge and experience, and classifies the recommended product as 'Complex'. The capacity for loss section states 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The fund recommendation section states that knowledge and experience were considered alongside other factors. The client is described as a Balanced investor (risk rating 5, overriding a questionnaire result of 4) who understands pension freedoms and prefers Flexi Access Drawdown flexibility. The report recommends two multi-asset funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 50% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 50%) within an Aviva Pension Portfolio personal pension.

    File:     The risk profile report (category D) records the client's knowledge questionnaire responses as: stocks and shares — 'No understanding / knowledge'; Enterprise Investment Schemes and VCTs — 'No understanding / knowledge'; pension planning and income in retirement — 'No understanding / knowledge'. Investment experience: no investment in unit trusts, GIAs, stocks and shares, EIS/VCTs, or pension products in the last 5 years. Only experience recorded is 'Yes, by myself' for current accounts, savings/deposit accounts, cash ISA, and NS&I. No additional investor experience notes were recorded. The agreed risk level is 5 (Balanced), whose description in the risk profile report states it is suited to someone with 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters'. The capacity for loss section of the suitability report (P7) corroborates limited experience: 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements'. The recommended product is an Aviva Pension Portfolio (personal pension) investing in two multi-asset OEIC funds rated risk 5.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report classifies the recommended product as 'Complex', yet the recommended funds are two standard multi-asset OEICs (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C) within a personal pension wrapper — both are professionally managed, diversified, risk-profiled solutions described in the suitability report itself as designed to 'remain within your agreed level of risk'. No explanation is provided in the evidence for why a personal pension investing in two multi-asset funds is classified as Complex rather than a simpler product type.

      - The client's knowledge questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning and income in retirement, yet the report does not address this direct contradiction when asserting the product is suitable for someone with Limited knowledge — particularly given the product is classified as Complex.

      - The agreed risk level description (Balanced, level 5) carries a precondition of 'some experience of investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters', but the file records no investment experience in any product type in the last 5 years and 'No understanding / knowledge' of pensions, stocks and shares. The report reproduces the Balanced description without reconciling this contradiction.

      - There is no category C record (e.g. client questions asked, explanations given, comprehension check) evidencing that the client understood the Complex product features (e.g. Flexi Access Drawdown, investment pathways, pension freedoms) despite the product being classified as Complex and the client having no recorded knowledge of pension planning.

      - The suitability report states the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms and prefer[s] the flexibility of entering Flexi Access Drawdown' but the knowledge questionnaire records 'No understanding / knowledge' of pension planning. No corroborating evidence of how this understanding was established appears in the file.

    The canonical model records both 'Limited' knowledge and a 'Complex' product classification simultaneously. Under the decision logic this is a Potential Concern at the highest severity: a complex product recommended to a client the file records as having no or limited relevant knowledge. The knowledge questionnaire in the risk profile report (P9) is unambiguous — the client answered 'No understanding / knowledge' to pension planning specifically, and recorded no investment experience in any product in the last five years beyond cash savings. The suitability report acknowledges experience is 'limited to workplace pension arrangements' (P7), which corroborates limited experience but does not bridge the gap to a Complex product classification without a corroborating comprehension record. The guard 'You confirmed you understand' applies here: the report's assertion that the client 'understand[s] the pension freedoms' is an adviser assertion, not evidence of understanding, and no category C record exists in the pack to corroborate it. The second guard also applies: the Balanced risk profile description carries a precondition of 'some experience of investments', which the file directly contradicts; reproducing this description does not establish knowledge. The fund choice itself (two plain multi-asset OEICs in a personal pension) does not obviously warrant a 'Complex' classification — the report does not explain the basis for this classification, so it is possible it is a mis-labelling; but that ambiguity does not resolve the absence of any comprehension record given the product is what it is classified as. On vulnerability: no formal vulnerability assessment outcome is recorded in the file (the extraction flags /vulnerability/C1/assessmentOutcome and /vulnerability/C2/assessmentOutcome as NotPresentInDocument), so there is no identified vulnerability characteristic that would independently affect this finding. The absence of a vulnerability record is assessed under CHK-010. The core finding stands: a client with no recorded knowledge of pensions or investments has been recommended a product classified as Complex, with no category C corroboration of comprehension, and the risk profile preconditions are not met by the file evidence.

  [G4.5] Evidence that the client can evaluate the risks and features of a complex product, based on their questions and comprehension

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report asserts (page 4, Knowledge & Experience section) that the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards.' It further states (page 4, Capacity for Loss section) that 'although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The report therefore characterises the client as having a reasonable but limited-experience understanding of investments, confined to workplace pension arrangements.

    File:     The fact find (category B) and risk profile report (category D) contain the following relevant material. The file notes (category C, [P2]) record, under Capacity for Loss: 'you are aware that investment values can rise as well as fall, and although your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The ATR section of the file notes ([P2]) records that the questionnaire produced a risk rating of 4, that the adviser felt it 'didn't fully reflect your risk appetite', and that the client agreed to proceed at risk rating 5 (Balanced investor), described as someone who 'may have some experience in investments and a degree of understanding of financial matters.' The risk profile report ([P14]) records under 'Investor Experience': 'No additional notes were recorded.' The risk profile report ([P16]) records under 'Agreed risk level': 'No additional notes were recorded.' No record of client questions about the recommended product's features, risks or complexity appears in any document. The recommended product is an Aviva Pension Portfolio invested via a Centralised Investment Proposition (CIP) — a multi-fund, platform-based drawdown-capable pension — which carries meaningful complexity (multiple funds, platform charges, drawdown options, investment pathways consideration, ongoing adviser charge structure).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the client 'feel[s] comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' but the risk profile report (category D, [P14]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience, providing no corroboration for this characterisation.

      - The report's Investor Experience section contains no graded knowledge level (e.g. None / Limited / Experienced), only a narrative assertion; the risk profile report's Investor Experience section is blank, so no graded level appears in the file either.

      - No record of client questions, clarifications sought, or demonstrated comprehension of the recommended product's specific features or risks appears in any document in the pack, despite the product being a complex multi-fund CIP-based pension platform with drawdown capability.

      - The risk profile report ([P16]) records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Agreed risk level, leaving the basis for the knowledge/experience conclusion unsupported in the risk profiling documentation.

    The report's two knowledge and experience assertions are partially corroborated: the statement that experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements and that returns are understood to be non-guaranteed is directly echoed in the file notes ([P2]), so that element is consistent. However, the broader assertion that the client feels 'comfortable with how investments work and have a reasonable understanding of potential risks & rewards' is not corroborated by the risk profile report, which records nothing under Investor Experience ([P14]). The absence of notes is not conclusive proof the client lacks understanding, but per the guard, an absence of recorded questions or notes is an evidence gap that must be reported, weighted by product complexity. The recommended product — an Aviva Pension Portfolio using a CIP of multiple funds, with platform charges, drawdown options, investment pathway considerations and an ongoing adviser charge — is a complex product. The file contains no record of questions asked by the client, no record of explanations given about the product's unusual or complex features (e.g. the multi-fund CIP structure, the platform mechanics, investment pathways being discounted), and no demonstration of comprehension. The risk profile report's 'Investor Experience' section is entirely blank. This creates a material evidence gap: the report asserts a level of comfort and understanding that is not graded, not substantiated in the risk profiling documentation, and not corroborated by any record of client engagement with product complexity. No vulnerability factors in the pack materially alter this conclusion (CHK-010 covers vulnerability separately). The outcome is a Potential Concern at Moderate severity: there is no record of client questions or explanations where the product has unusual features, and the knowledge level is stated in the report but not graded or evidenced in the file.

  [G4.6] Existing holdings evidencing investment and pension experience

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the client has held cash savings products, has not taken out a stocks and shares investment by himself, but has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work, with a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards. Existing arrangements comprise five pensions: People's Pension (OccupationalDC, invested in Global Investments up to 85% shares), Scottish Widows (PersonalPension, SW Pension Portfolio Four CS1 and Three CS1), Aviva (PersonalPension, Aviva pension my future focus consolidation, risk rating 2), Zurich (PersonalPension, Managed AP, risk rating 7), and Standard Life (PersonalPension, SL at retirement universal PP 10 year pension fund, risk rating 4).

    File:     Policy documents confirm the five existing pensions. Aviva (SP50163762): Aviva Pensions My Future Focus Consolidation (Pre-2024) S2, value £3,744.36, paid up, lifestage investment approach [P2]. Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL): Managed AP (accumulation), 492.05 units at £210.221, fund value £103,439.24, no payments received in last ten years [P8]. Standard Life (D2301334000): SL At Retirement Universal (PP 10 Year) Pension Fd, value £2,994.30 per valuation statement [P4][P5][P14]. Scottish Widows (ZU4225575): value £211.32, Master Trust, no guarantees or bonuses, Money4Life plan [P11][P13]. People's Pension: Global Investments (up to 85% shares) fund available in the scheme [P12]. The Zurich plan has had no payments received in the last ten years [P8], suggesting it was set up by a prior employer or adviser. The People's Pension is an occupational/workplace scheme. The Scottish Widows plan is also a Master Trust workplace scheme. No fact find or separate knowledge and experience questionnaire is present in the retrieved passages to independently corroborate the report's characterisation of the client's self-assessed comfort level and understanding.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report records the Standard Life plan value at £3,002.00; the policy valuation statement shows £2,994.30 as at 30 September 2025 (a minor difference, but a difference nonetheless).

      - No independent fact find or knowledge and experience assessment document is present in the file to corroborate the client's stated comfort level and understanding of investments and risks/rewards.

      - The Zurich plan shows no payments received in the last ten years, suggesting it may be an inherited or employer-set-up arrangement rather than one the client actively managed, which limits its weight as evidence of personal investment experience.

      - The People's Pension and Scottish Widows plans are both workplace/Master Trust schemes, consistent with auto-enrolment rather than active investment decision-making by the client.

    The report asserts the client has held group/workplace pensions for some time and feels comfortable with how investments work and has a reasonable understanding of potential risks and rewards, having previously held cash savings but no standalone stocks and shares investment. The five existing pension holdings in the file do corroborate ownership of multiple pension arrangements, and the policy documents confirm the fund names and plan details match those stated in the report for Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows, and People's Pension. However, the guard on auto-enrolment applies here: the People's Pension is an occupational DC scheme (consistent with auto-enrolment) and the Scottish Widows plan is a Money4Life Master Trust workplace plan; holding these evidences ownership, not active investment experience. The Zurich plan, while a personal pension, shows no payments in the last ten years, suggesting it was set up by a prior employer or adviser rather than reflecting active client decision-making. The minor discrepancy in the Standard Life value (£3,002 in the report vs £2,994.30 in the policy document) is noted but is likely attributable to different valuation dates and is not material to the knowledge and experience assessment. The more significant concern is the absence of any independent fact find or knowledge and experience record in the file: the report's claim that the client 'confirmed' comfort with investments and reasonable understanding of risks and rewards is self-referential — it is asserted in the suitability report but not corroborated by any separate category A or G document. While the holdings themselves do support a level of pension ownership experience, the characterisation of the client's understanding and comfort level rests solely on the report's own assertion. Given the recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio — a SIPP with a broad investment range including ETIs, equities, model portfolios and discretionary investment management capability per the key features and T&Cs) is a moderately complex product, the absence of an independent knowledge and experience record to support the report's characterisation is a moderate concern rather than a highest-severity one, since the client does have multiple existing pension arrangements across different providers. No vulnerability factors have been identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment.

  [G4.7] Adequacy of the rationale supporting a complex recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report classifies the recommended product (Aviva Pension Portfolio with consolidation of multiple existing arrangements) as Complex, driven by four stated complexity drivers: (1) pension transfer from multiple existing arrangements; (2) Flexi-Access Drawdown with flexible income withdrawal; (3) investment into multi-asset funds with ongoing management; (4) IHT implications from April 2027. The report provides per-recommendation rationale: People's Pension retained because employer contributions continue and transferring would lose that benefit; Scottish Widows encashed under small pots rules (estimated net £170) as it would not detract from retirement plan; Aviva existing plan transferred because it is 0.06% more expensive than the new solution and invested in lifestyling funds that will eventually move outside the agreed risk range; Zurich transferred for broader investment choice, adviser charge facilitation and alignment with risk profile; Standard Life transferred as 0.08% more expensive. The report also records ATR of 5 (overriding questionnaire result of 4), describes the client as having limited investment experience confined to workplace pension arrangements, and sets out IHT and consolidation context.

    File:     Category C file notes (P8, P10, P11) corroborate that the client's investment experience is limited to workplace pension arrangements. P8 states: 'your experience is limited to your workplace pension arrangements, you understand that returns are not guaranteed.' The risk profile report (P22/P13/P14) records a natural ATR of 4 (Cautious Balanced) with no additional notes on investor experience or agreed risk level discussion beyond what is in the questionnaire appendix. Category D (P14) records no additional notes on investor experience. The file notes (P10) corroborate rationale for discounting alternative options: Aviva alternative discounted as only mixed-asset fund is risk-rated 4; Zurich alternative discounted because no risk-5 multi-asset fund exists and the blend includes interest deposits (single-asset); Standard Life alternatives discounted as more expensive. No file note or meeting record corroborates any explanation given to the client about the complexity drivers themselves — specifically Flexi-Access Drawdown mechanics, IHT implications from April 2027, or the nature of multi-asset ongoing management — nor any record that the client demonstrated comprehension of these features. The risk profile tool section on investor experience (P14) records 'No additional notes were recorded.' The policy research (P3/P4/P12) and product literature (P1/P2/P5) confirm the Aviva Pension Portfolio supports drawdown and multi-asset investment, corroborating the product's complex features. P8 records the client confirmed awareness that funds may go up and down and agreed to risk rating 5, but this addresses ATR rather than comprehension of the complex product features.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report asserts the recommended product is Complex with Flexi-Access Drawdown as a complexity driver, but no file record evidences that the client was given an explanation of FAD mechanics or demonstrated understanding of it — the investor experience section of the risk profile (P14) records no additional notes.

      - The report identifies IHT implications from April 2027 as a complexity driver but no file note, meeting record or client-facing document in the pack records that this was explained to the client or that the client acknowledged understanding it.

      - The report records the client's investment experience as limited to workplace pensions (corroborated by P8), yet the recommended product is classified Complex — no record in the file bridges this gap by documenting client comprehension of the complex features.

      - The report states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as part of the rationale, but the derived figures and the charges table on page 11 show the impact for EA5 is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the 0.26% / 0.08% figures unreliable for Standard Life and therefore the cost-based rationale for that transfer is unsupported by accurate arithmetic.

      - The risk profile report (P14) records 'No additional notes were recorded' under Investor Experience, leaving the report's characterisation of the client's knowledge level as stated rather than graded or evidenced in the fact-find or risk tool.

    The report correctly classifies the recommended product as Complex and provides per-recommendation rationale that is largely client-specific and corroborated at the level of the transfer decisions (why each plan is or is not transferred). The file notes in P10 corroborate the alternative-option analysis. However, the adequacy of the rationale for a complex recommendation requires more than transfer justification: where the product involves Flexi-Access Drawdown, multi-asset ongoing management and IHT considerations — all identified as complexity drivers — there should be evidence in the file that these features were explained to the client and that the client demonstrated comprehension, particularly given that the client's experience is recorded as limited to workplace pensions (P8) and the investor experience section of the risk profile tool records no additional notes (P14). No such record exists in the pack. The absence of any comprehension check or explanation record for FAD mechanics and IHT implications is a genuine gap given the client's limited background. The Standard Life cost rationale is also arithmetically undermined by the derived figures showing EA5 charges were computed on the wrong fund value, though this is a secondary concern relative to the comprehension gap. The vulnerability overlay: P8 notes the client's experience is limited but records no formal vulnerability. The limited experience characteristic does not trigger a separate vulnerability concern here, but it reinforces the need for documented comprehension of complex features. The ATR override (questionnaire 4, agreed 5) is noted but excluded from this check per the instructions. The discrepancy regarding no notes in the investor experience section of the risk tool (P14) is not explained by any guard: it is a genuine absence of corroboration for the knowledge characterisation. The complexity-to-comprehension gap is the primary concern: a complex product recommended to a client with limited, workplace-only experience, with no documented explanation of the complex features and no comprehension record in any supporting document.

CHK-006 — Recommendation Rationale and Suitable Alternatives

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G6.1, G6.2, G6.3, G6.4, G6.5, G6.6, G6.7, G6.8). The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving), but the charges comparison table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost to the client). These are contradictory. The EA5 charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the entire cost comparison for Standard Life is based on the wrong plan value and is arithmetically unreliable. The report's rationale bullets for Aviva switch, Zurich switch and Standard Life switch include several sentences that are verbatim identical across all three recommendations ('The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options...', 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly...', 'The adviser will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required'), which meet the definition of boilerplate under the comparison guard. 12 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G6.1] Client-specific rationale for each recommendation, linked to specified goals

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report provides rationale for five recommendations: (1) Retain People's Pension — employer contributions still active, transferring would lose employer contributions; (2) Encash Scottish Widows — no single multi-asset fund at risk level 5, no adviser management, small pots rules net receipt ~£170; (3) Switch Aviva — lifestyling funds, 0.06% cost saving, wider investment options, adviser charging, active monitoring; (4) Switch Zurich — no risk level 5 funds available, blended risk 7/risk 2 funds underperform, consolidation objective, wider options, flexible drawdown, adviser charging, active monitoring; (5) Switch Standard Life — lifestyling funds, 0.08% cost saving, wider options, flexible drawdown, adviser charging, active monitoring. All rationale items are marked isClientSpecific=true. Recommendations are linked to OBJ2 and OBJ3. The report also notes the ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4 but the agreed rating is 5 on the basis the questionnaire did not fully reflect the client's risk appetite.

    File:     Policy research (P1, P4, P8) records the file strategy as consolidation of pensions the client finds difficult to understand, with investment choice goal of ATR 5 CIP funds. Per-plan research notes: Aviva — Switch, reason: lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing; Zurich — Switch, reason: expensive, no adviser servicing; People's Pension — Retain, reason: active workplace pension; Scottish Widows — Encash (small pots); Standard Life — Switch. The SIPP research report (P5, P7) covers the recommended Aviva platform solution with an investment of £110,185 and provides MiFID II ex-ante cost disclosures for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced and CT Universal MAP Balanced funds. The risk profile report (P11, P12) confirms a natural risk level of 4 (Cautious Balanced) from questionnaire dated 18/09/2025, with the process described as arriving at an agreed risk level through discussion. No additional notes were recorded under 'Investment target' or 'Agreed risk level'. The suitability report itself (P9, P10, P14) confirms the recommendation narrative including objectives and charges. The charges table (internal inconsistency noted in extraction) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26%, contradicting the report's stated 0.08% saving. The EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. No retirement income projection is present. C2's assets, liabilities, ATR and capacity for loss are absent from the report.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving), but the charges comparison table shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase in cost to the client). These are contradictory.

      - The EA5 charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), meaning the entire cost comparison for Standard Life is based on the wrong plan value and is arithmetically unreliable.

      - The report's rationale bullets for Aviva switch, Zurich switch and Standard Life switch include several sentences that are verbatim identical across all three recommendations ('The new scheme offers access to a wider range of investment options...', 'The new scheme allows adviser charges to be deducted directly...', 'The adviser will have the ability to actively monitor and adjust investments as required'), which meet the definition of boilerplate under the comparison guard.

      - The ATR questionnaire produced a score of 4, but the report records an agreed rating of 5. The risk profile document records no additional notes under 'Agreed risk level', providing no documented basis for the override.

      - No retirement income projection is present in the report (flagged as NotPresentInDocument), leaving the link between the consolidation recommendations and OBJ3 (retirement positioning) unquantified.

      - C2's assets, liabilities, ATR and capacity for loss are absent from the report; this is a joint case and the report's suitability conclusions are therefore not demonstrably grounded in C2's individual position.

      - The report asserts all 22 rationale items are client-specific (isClientSpecific=true), but several bullets are repeated verbatim across multiple recommendations and are not individually tailored.

      - The policy research file (P8) records the objective fields for each plan recommendation as blank, offering no corroboration that the linked objectives (OBJ2, OBJ3) were identified at the research stage.

    The report provides a structured rationale for each of the five recommendations and links them to stated objectives. Several elements are genuinely client-specific: the People's Pension retention rationale correctly identifies the active employer contribution; the Scottish Widows encashment rationale identifies the specific fund limitation (no single ATR-5 fund) and the small-pots net receipt of ~£170; the Zurich switch rationale identifies the specific fund blending problem and underperformance. These are not pure boilerplate. However, multiple concerns arise. First, and most seriously, the Standard Life cost rationale is contradicted by the charges table: the report says switching saves 0.08%, but the table shows a +0.26% increase. This is a direct internal contradiction flagged in the extraction's internalInconsistencies. The derived figures confirm that the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (~£3,002), making the cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically wrong. A rationale that claims a cost saving where the evidence shows a cost increase is a material concern. Second, several rationale bullets are repeated verbatim across Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life recommendations ('wider range of investment options', 'adviser charges to be deducted directly', 'actively monitor and adjust investments'). Under the guard, identical bullets repeated verbatim are boilerplate; they should not all be marked isClientSpecific=true. Third, the ATR override from 4 to 5 is not documented in the risk profile file — the 'Agreed risk level' section records no additional notes — leaving the basis for the agreed rating unsupported. Fourth, no retirement income projection exists, weakening the suitability narrative for OBJ3. Fifth, C2's financial position and risk profile are absent, so the report cannot be verified as suitable for the joint client. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability indicators are identified in the available evidence that would materially alter this assessment. On balance, the Standard Life cost contradiction (a rationale claiming a saving where the evidence shows an increase, compounded by the wrong base value being used) is a material concern sufficient to warrant a Potential Concern finding at High severity, reinforced by the boilerplate issue and the undocumented ATR override.

  [G6.2] Justification for the particular product, provider, wrapper and investment

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report recommends the Aviva Pension Portfolio (Personal Pension/SIPP wrapper) for client C1, consolidating three existing pensions (Aviva £3,744.36, Zurich £103,439.24, Standard Life £3,002) with a total transfer value of £110,185. Provider rationale covers: financial strength (S&P A Stable, £407bn AUM); fund choice (6,900+ funds); competitive/transparent charges; online convenience; and governance. Investment rationale covers: risk-profile alignment (funds reflect ATR 5 individually and combined); diversification across asset classes; active/passive blend to keep costs low; client preference for simplicity; competitive charges; peer-relative performance; and CIP governance (quarterly investment committee, external research team). The recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (50%, risk 5, 0.18% p.a.) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (50%, risk 5, 0.29% p.a.). The report states investment pathways were considered and discounted because the client requires a more tailored solution. The report also asserts the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as part of the switch rationale. Provider selection was supported by research. Fund selection is part of the firm's CIP. The wrapper is a SIPP (Pension Portfolio - Choice) on the Aviva Platform.

    File:     The SIPP research report (P5, P7, P9) confirms a whole-of-market product search was conducted in the SIPP research area, producing a shortlist of one product matching filters applied: Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice (Aviva Life & Pensions UK Limited). The platform research (P1) shows a shortlist of four platforms (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) with no DNA filters applied. The fund research (P10, P12) shows both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc and CT Universal MAP Balanced C) with performance data, cost disclosures and risk ratings, consistent with CIP inclusion. Fund performance data (P12) shows positive multi-year returns for both funds. The policy research document (P13) records the rationale fields for each existing arrangement: Aviva — 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich — 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension — 'Retain / Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows — fields partially visible. The charges comparison table (per the canonical model's internal inconsistency flags) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase), contradicting the report's narrative claim of a 0.08% saving. The Aviva Key Features document (P8) confirms the Pension Portfolio is a SIPP on the Aviva Platform with Core and Choice options. The Zurich policy information (P17) confirms drawdown options are not available on the Zurich plan and it can be transferred to another provider. The research report (P7, P9) confirms 118 products were assessed and only Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice matched the filters applied for the product selection.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states (page 8) that the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (page 11, per the canonical model's internal inconsistency record) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — an increase, not a saving. These figures are directly contradictory.

      - The platform research (P1) shows no DNA analysis was carried out and no filters were applied to the platform shortlist of four, yet the report asserts 'transparent, competitive charges with potential tiered pricing' as a provider justification without a comparative quantitative basis from the platform research.

      - The provider rationale points (financial strength, fund choice, value, convenience, governance) are generic and not client-specific — none is linked to the client's individual circumstances or objectives, though the investment rationale does include two client-specific points (risk profile alignment and simplicity preference).

      - The policy research document (P13) leaves the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field blank for both the Aviva and Zurich switch recommendations, meaning the link between the switch rationale and a named client objective is not documented in the research file, even though the suitability report references objectives.

      - The EA5 (Standard Life) charge comparison row in the canonical model is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89 implied by 0.18% / £186.19) rather than Standard Life's own value of £3,002, rendering the Standard Life charge comparison unreliable.

    The comparison can be made from the pack. The most significant finding is the direct contradiction between the report's narrative on page 8 (Standard Life plan '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', i.e. switching saves money) and the charges table on page 11 (showing +0.26% impact, i.e. the recommended solution is more expensive for Standard Life). This is not explained by any guard: it is an internal contradiction within the report itself, and the derived figures confirm the EA5 charge comparison row is computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's £103,439 rather than Standard Life's £3,002), which means the 0.26% figure in the table is arithmetically wrong relative to Standard Life's actual balance — yet the narrative's 0.08% saving is also unsupported. Either way the report's cost-saving claim for the Standard Life switch is unreliable and potentially misleading. The platform research (P1) shows no DNA or filters applied and four platforms in the shortlist with no comparative scoring, so the 'competitive charges' provider claim is an absolute assertion rather than a comparative one — the marketing-claims guard applies, but that guard distinguishes absolute from comparative claims; since no quantitative comparison underpins it, the claim is asserted without evidenced comparison. The product selection (P9) is well-evidenced: 118 SIPPs were assessed, filters applied, and only Aviva Pension Portfolio - Choice matched — this robustly supports the product/wrapper choice. Fund selection is supported by CIP membership, performance data (P12) and cost disclosures (P10). The investment rationale includes two genuinely client-specific points (risk profile alignment, simplicity preference) and the investment pathways alternative was explicitly considered and rejected (P2). The generic provider rationale points do not contradict the file but are not comparative. The blank objective fields in the policy research (P13) are a minor gap — the suitability report does link recommendations to objectives in its narrative. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence as affecting the suitability assessment conclusions for this check. The dominant concern is the Standard Life cost-saving claim contradiction, which falls under the highest-severity band (rationale contradicts case facts — 'lower costs' where charge comparison shows an increase).

  [G6.3] Personalised language linked to individual circumstances

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The canonical model asserts isClientSpecific=true for all 22 recommendation rationale entries. The report presents itself as providing personalised, client-specific justification for each recommendation, linked to Alan's individual circumstances including his retirement planning objective, desire to consolidate pensions, ATR of 5, and need for £1,700 net per month household income in approximately 2 years.

    File:     The fact find [P5] records that the client 'Just needs advice helping him organise his pensions in the best place. Wants to consolidate to help him get his head around what he has. Thinks he'll probably retire in the next 2 years and needs a household income of £1,700 net per month. ATR 5.' The policy research document [P14] records the summary of advice as 'Client has come to us seeking advice because he has a lot of pensions that he doesn't understand how they work and ideally wants to put them all together to make it easier for him to understand and utilise in his retirement. Will be recommending to consolidate where necessary and provide cashflow planning around his retirement.' However, the policy research file [P15] shows that the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for every arrangement (Aviva, Zurich, People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Standard Life). The reasons given for switching or retaining are brief labels only: Aviva — 'Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; Zurich — 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; People's Pension — 'Active workplace pension'; Scottish Widows — omitted/truncated; Standard Life — not shown in available text. The suitability report narrative [P13] opens with general advisory framing and references switching the Aviva pension, but the passage retrieved does not demonstrate individualised per-recommendation rationale beyond the generic consolidation theme. The internal inconsistency already identified shows that the Standard Life recommendation rationale states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving) while the charges table shows '+0.26%' (an increase) — directly contradicting the personalised cost-saving rationale for that recommendation. A retirement income projection is noted as absent from the document (expectedButAbsent path /needsAnalysis/retirementIncomeProjection), meaning the report's claim to have assessed whether consolidation meets the £1,700/month income need is unsupported. C2's ATR and capacity for loss are also absent, though this appears to be a single-client case. The ambiguity over the ATR override (questionnaire result 4, agreed 5) is unresolved in the evidence.

    Discrepancies:

      - Policy research file [P15] shows the 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' field is blank for every arrangement, contradicting the report's assertion that each recommendation rationale is client-specific and linked to a named objective.

      - Standard Life recommendation rationale asserts a 0.08% cost saving, but the charges table shows a +0.26% increase — the personalised cost rationale for that recommendation is factually contradicted by the report's own supporting data.

      - No retirement income projection is present in the file (confirmed absent by extraction metadata), so the report's personalised claim that the recommended strategy meets Alan's stated £1,700/month income need at retirement is unsupported by any quantified analysis.

      - ATR questionnaire result (4) differs from the agreed/override ATR (5) used in recommendations; the report does not contain a fully resolved, documented client-specific justification for the override beyond a brief statement that the questionnaire 'didn't fully reflect' the client's risk appetite.

      - The policy research rationale for each arrangement is limited to brief labels ('Lifestyle, expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Expensive, no adviser servicing'; 'Active workplace pension') with no named client objective linked to any individual recommendation.

      - The report asserts isClientSpecific=true for 22 rationale entries, but the supporting research file fields for linking recommendations to client objectives are uniformly blank.

    The report asserts client-specific personalisation across all 22 recommendation rationale entries. The fact find and policy research file confirm the client's headline objectives (consolidation, retirement in ~2 years, £1,700/month net income, ATR 5), so there is a genuine client profile against which to measure personalisation. However, the evidence reveals several material gaps. First, the policy research document [P15] — which is the adviser's working document linking each recommendation to a client objective — has blank entries for 'Which objective/goal is this achieving?' across every arrangement. This directly undermines the assertion that each recommendation carries its own client-specific rationale linked to a named objective. Second, and most seriously for this check, the Standard Life recommendation rationale claims a cost saving of 0.08% as part of its personalised justification, but the report's own charges table (identified in the internal inconsistency log) shows a +0.26% increase. This is not a minor rounding difference: it is a directional contradiction where the personalised rationale states the client benefits from lower costs when the data shows the opposite. A guard covering standard regulatory wording does not apply here — this is a substantive factual claim within the personalised rationale. Third, the absence of a retirement income projection means the report cannot be said to have demonstrated in a client-specific, quantified way that the recommended strategy will deliver the client's £1,700/month income objective. The consolidation rationale (simplification, single platform) is consistent with the client's stated desire and is genuinely personalised in that respect. The ATR override is documented, if briefly. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability drivers are identified in the evidence beyond the client's monthly cash flow being negative (income £1,430, expenditure £1,718 per [P4]), which is a financial circumstance that the report should have addressed in the context of affordability and any ongoing charges; there is no indication it was factored into the personalised rationale. Overall, while some personalisation is present (consolidation objective, specific fund values, named provider), the blank objective fields in the research file, the contradicted cost-saving rationale for Standard Life, and the absence of a retirement income projection mean the assertion that all 22 rationale entries are client-specific is not fully supported and is in part contradicted by the evidence.

  [G6.4] Alternatives considered and reasons for rejection

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers the following alternatives: (1) Consolidate into People's Pension / Option 4 (employer scheme) — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, £4.50 annual admin charge, no multi-asset funds, limited fund range. (2) Leave Scottish Widows pot to grow until retirement (do-nothing) — rejected in favour of encashment under small pots rules to serve consolidation objective; net receipt ~£170. (3) Option 1 — leave existing pension scheme untouched — rejected because lifestyling funds will move portfolio outside agreed risk range, lower fund choice and no adviser management. (4) Option 2 — leave existing scheme and switch funds internally (in-situ switch) — rejected because existing Aviva plan does not allow adviser management and limited fund range does not meet risk profile. (5) Stakeholder pension — rejected because recommended plan is more cost-effective and stakeholder pensions typically do not allow flexible access and have limited fund ranges. Research options OPT1–OPT5 are listed (Options 1–5) with OPT5 recommended; OPT1 and OPT2 appear in the options list but with no rejection reasons recorded in the /research/optionsConsidered[] array. The report also notes Aviva internal switch was considered (only mixed-asset Aviva fund available is risk-rated 4, does not meet ATR 5) and Zurich internal switch was considered (no risk-level-5 funds; blend of 7 and 2 used; no multi-asset funds; blend performed marginally worse). However, a key internal inconsistency exists: the report (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving argument for switching), while the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — meaning the new plan is actually more expensive for Standard Life on the table, directly contradicting the narrative saving claim.

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) confirms: (a) a four-product shortlist was researched for the SIPP wrapper (Aviva Platform, Elevate/Aberdeen, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime Platform), with Aviva Platform recommended but no additional justification text entered and no DNA analysis carried out. (b) Individual alternative comparisons were run for Aviva-only, Standard Life-only and Zurich-only switches, all showing a decrease in projected maturity value on switching (e.g. Zurich-only mid-growth: £127,000 existing vs £125,000 new; Aviva-only mid-growth: £4,280 existing vs £4,260 new). (c) The combined comparison (all three switched) also shows a decrease at all growth rates (mid: £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new, a £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; with advice charges included the new plan requires 4.24% outperformance / £4,672 p.a.). (d) The pension switch report including advice charges shows a 2.1% reduction in yield on the new solution (vs 0.3% existing) and a 9.4% decrease at mid-growth after advice charges. (e) File notes (category C) record: Aviva alternative discounted (only mixed-asset Aviva fund available is risk-rated 4); Zurich alternative discounted (no risk-level-5 funds; blend of 7 and 2; no multi-asset funds; blend not performed as well); Standard Life alternative discounted (all funds in 20-60% equity range more expensive than Aviva offering). (f) The stakeholder comparison table in the pension switch report includes a generic stakeholder column but no named stakeholder product. (g) OPT1 (leave untouched) and OPT2 (internal fund switch) appear in the options list in the suitability report but have no recorded rejection reasons in the /research/optionsConsidered[] array. (h) The charges table shows EA5 (Standard Life) impact as +0.26%, contradicting the report narrative of a 0.08% saving. (i) Derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed against Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. OPT1 (leave untouched) and OPT2 (in-situ fund switch) are listed as options considered in the report but carry no rejection reasons in the /research/optionsConsidered[] array — partial treatment only. The report narrative provides some reasoning (lifestyling risk misalignment, no adviser management) but it is presented as inferred rather than stated for OPT1/OPT2 specifically.

      - 2. Standard Life cost claim contradicted: report (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying the new plan is cheaper), but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% (meaning the new plan is more expensive relative to Standard Life). These directly contradict each other.

      - 3. The EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the derived figures are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison numerically unreliable. The report nonetheless asserts a specific saving figure for Standard Life.

      - 4. Platform shortlist research (SIPP research report) lists four providers (Aviva, Elevate, Aegon ARC, Wealthtime) but records 'No additional justification text has been entered' and 'No Platform DNA analysis was carried out' — the platform choice rationale is asserted in the report but is unsupported by the research document's own reasoning fields.

      - 5. Product DNA analysis also not carried out ('No Product DNA analysis was carried out'), meaning the product-level selection rationale in the research report is similarly bare.

      - 6. The all-three-combined switch comparison shows a material decrease at all growth rates (e.g. mid-growth: £12,000 / 9.4% decrease after advice charges), and the new plan requires 4.24% outperformance / £4,672 p.a. The report presents this but does not explicitly reconcile why switching is still recommended given this projected loss — i.e. the recommendation is not ranked above the existing arrangement on a pure value projection basis, yet the report claims lower costs as a primary reason.

      - 7. Growth rate label discrepancy: pension switch report uses 2.94% as mid growth rate label in one table (Aviva-only comparison) but 2.95% elsewhere; the suitability report narrative references 2.94% on page 11 but the critical yield table uses 2.95% — minor but a noted inconsistency across documents.

      - 8. The Scottish Widows do-nothing alternative is presented with two possible outcomes (£170 net now vs full amount tax-free at retirement) without a fully stated recommendation in the alternatives section — the ambiguity note in the extraction acknowledges this, though the net-receipt-now appears to be the recommended course.

    The report does present alternatives and reasons for most of them, and the file notes (category C) provide corroborating detail for the internal-switch discounts. However, several material concerns arise after applying the guards. First, the most serious issue is the Standard Life cost claim: the report asserts on page 8 that Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves money), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact as +0.26% — meaning the new plan is more expensive for Standard Life. This is not a document-comparison artefact; it is a direct contradiction within the report itself, confirmed by the derived figures which show the Standard Life rows were computed on Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's, making the stated 0.08% saving unreliable. This meets the 'rationale contradicts the case facts' threshold at the highest severity level. Second, the platform and product selection research records no justification text and no DNA analysis, so the adviser's assertion that Aviva Platform was selected for specific client-appropriate reasons is unsupported by the research document. Third, OPT1 and OPT2 appear in the options list without recorded rejection reasons in the research array; the report provides some narrative reasoning but it is flagged as inferred. Fourth, the combined switch projection shows the new plan produces a lower projected value at all growth rates after advice charges (9.4% decrease at mid-growth, £12,000 reduction), and the new plan is 4.24% more expensive including advice fees — yet the report presents 'lower costs' as a primary switching rationale. While the like-for-like (ex-adviser fee) comparison shows a 0.24% saving, the report does not clearly reconcile the contradiction between the 'lower cost' narrative and the 9.4% projected value decrease. This is a ranked comparison placing the recommendation below the existing arrangement in value terms, which must be flagged. On vulnerability: the file records no vulnerability assessment outcome for either client (noted as absent in the extraction), so no additional overlay applies here beyond the general concern that the Standard Life cost error could mislead a client who relies on the written rationale. Overall, the discrepancy between the Standard Life cost narrative and the charges table, compounded by unreliable charge computation on the wrong fund value, is a material finding that cannot be explained by any guard.

  [G6.5] Every existing arrangement has a stated recommended outcome and a rationale supporting it

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   Five existing arrangements are identified: (1) People's Pension – retain, rationale: active workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions; (2) Scottish Widows – encash under small pots rules (recommended course), net receipt approximately £170; rationale also references risk profile mismatch, no single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, limited withdrawal flexibility, and no adviser management; (3) Aviva – switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: risk profile mismatch (funds do not meet risk profile), cost saving of 0.06% over existing, £4.50 annual admin charge, lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring; (4) Zurich – switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs, lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring; (5) Standard Life – switch to Aviva Platform, rationale: lower costs (stated as 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution i.e. saving), lifestyling, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Each arrangement is linked to client objectives (primarily OBJ3, and OBJ2 for Scottish Widows). All rationales are marked isClientSpecific=true.

    File:     Policy research (P3/P5/P12) confirms five arrangements exist: People's Pension 6197175/PP (occupational, active), Scottish Widows ZU4225575, Aviva SP50163762 (stakeholder, paid-up), Zurich P10026-719-001/DL (personal pension), Standard Life D2301334000. The comparison table in the suitability report (P21) lists retirement options, fund counts and adviser servicing flags: People's Pension – FAD, Full and partial UFPLS, 7 funds, no servicing adviser, current workplace scheme; Scottish Widows – Full and partial UFPLS only, 29 funds, no servicing adviser; Aviva – FAD, Full and partial UFPLS, 80+ funds, no servicing adviser; Zurich – Full and partial UFPLS only, 60+ funds, no servicing adviser; Standard Life – FAD only, 300 funds, yes servicing adviser. The suitability report recommendation section (P23) records the Standard Life switch rationale including 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution.' The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction identifies that the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving). The derived-figures analysis confirms that the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438) not Standard Life's (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. The Scottish Widows policy information (P2) confirms no direct annuity options and limited retirement options. The SIPP research report (P19) shows that agreed risk level is 5 and investments selection is rated 6, supporting the risk-profile mismatch rationale for arrangements not offering a suitable fund. People's Pension policy information (P13) confirms the standard 'balanced' investment profile invests in Global Investments (up to 85% shares) fund, with a suggestion that switching to Global Investments (up to 60% shares) would better align with the client's risk profile. The Scottish Widows ambiguity (extraction note) records two possible outcomes for the encashment – £170 net now or potentially tax-free at retirement – with the £170 net encashment being the recommended course, though both options are presented without a definitive single recommendation statement in the report itself.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life rationale (report page 8/P23) states 0.08% cost saving (existing is 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution); the charges comparison table (page 11, internal inconsistency in extraction) shows +0.26% impact, meaning the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life, directly contradicting the cost-saving rationale.

      - Derived figures confirm the EA5 charge row (£186.19 at 0.18%) implies a fund value of £103,438.89, which is Zurich's current value not Standard Life's (£3,002), meaning the Standard Life cost comparison is computed on the wrong plan value and is arithmetically unreliable, yet the report presents a confident cost-saving figure.

      - Scottish Widows recommendation presents two alternative tax outcomes (£170 net now vs potentially fully tax-free at retirement) without a single unambiguous recommended course of action; the extraction ambiguity confirms this dual-outcome presentation.

      - People's Pension retain rationale references the need to switch funds within the plan to Global Investments (up to 60% shares) to better align with risk rating; however the adviceAction for People's Pension is recorded as 'Retain' without separately noting that a fund switch within the plan is also recommended, creating a potential gap between the stated action and the fuller recommendation.

      - The report's combined pension value of 'approximately £110,000' on page 3 encompasses all five plans but arithmetic sum of all five is £116,998.47; the report's 'approximately £106,000' on page 4 also does not match. Neither matches the sum. This does not directly affect the outcome rationale per arrangement but bears on whether the cost/value context presented to the client is accurate.

    All five existing arrangements have a stated recommended outcome and a rationale. The rationales are client-specific in the sense that they reference the client's risk rating, objective of consolidation/simplification, workplace scheme status, and desire for adviser management and flexibility. The People's Pension retain rationale is adequate and supported by evidence of active employer contributions (P12 notes ongoing contributions). The Scottish Widows encashment rationale is supported by the product comparison table showing limited retirement options (Full and partial UFPLS only, no FAD, no adviser servicing) (P21) and by the risk profile mismatch, but the report presents two possible outcomes without clearly stating which is the single recommended action; the extraction ambiguity flags this and it represents a moderate gap in clarity of recommendation. The Aviva and Zurich switch rationales are internally consistent and supported by the product comparison evidence. The most material concern is the Standard Life switch rationale: the report states a cost saving of 0.08% as a reason to switch, but the charges table on page 11 (captured in the internal inconsistency) shows the impact as +0.26% (an increase). The derived figures further confirm the Standard Life cost rows are computed against Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002 value, making the cost comparison on which the 'lower costs' rationale partly rests demonstrably unreliable and contradicted by the report's own charges table. This is a genuine contradiction between the report's rationale and the report's own supporting data, not explained by any false-positive guard. The 'lower costs' bullet is one of several rationale points for the Standard Life switch (lifestyling, flexibility, adviser charging, monitoring are also cited), so the switch is not without rationale entirely, but a stated cost-saving that is contradicted by the charges table is a material concern. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability flags are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The Scottish Widows dual-outcome presentation is a lower-severity concern. The Standard Life cost contradiction is the primary finding, rated Moderate (rationale present but one stated reason – cost saving – is contradicted by the report's own charges table; the recommendation is not entirely without rationale).

  [G6.6] Where a change is suggested, the report states whether it is recommended, who will implement it, and the intended outcome

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   Five recommendations are made, all marked 'Recommended'. Three involve changes (transfers/switches) and one involves an encashment: (1) Retain People's Pension – continue employer contributions until retirement; (2) Encash Scottish Widows (£211.32) under small pots rules, placing proceeds into client's savings account; (3) Switch Aviva (£3,744.36) into new Aviva pension platform for lower costs, investment flexibility, adviser charging facilitation and ongoing oversight; (4) Switch Zurich (£103,439.24) into new Aviva pension platform for better investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation and ongoing oversight; (5) Switch Standard Life (£3,002) into new Aviva pension platform for lower costs, exit lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation and ongoing oversight. Implementation timescale is 10–15 working days; cancellation rights are stated (30 days). A next step is noted for the client to complete a nomination of beneficiaries form after the transfer process begins. The canonical model records no fields for '/recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement' or '/recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty' — the extraction confirms these are absent from the document itself.

    File:     The retrieved passages are predominantly Aviva platform terms and conditions (categories H) and the risk profile report (category D), plus a SIPP research report (category G) and policy information documents (categories E, A). None of the retrieved passages address who will implement the recommended switches or encashment, nor do they corroborate or contradict the stated intended outcomes. The risk profile report confirms agreed risk level 5 (Balanced) and an investment figure of £106,000, consistent with the Zurich transfer being the dominant value. The SIPP research report confirms fund selection (CT Universal MAP Balanced and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced, 50/50). No passage addresses the implementation question (adviser vs client vs provider) for any of the three switch recommendations or the encashment.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report does not state who will implement the three switches (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) — the responsible party is absent from the document per the extraction's own report. This is a gap in the report itself, not an extraction failure.

      - The report does not state who will implement the Scottish Widows encashment — same absence.

      - The canonical model records an ambiguity (noted in extraction) about the Scottish Widows encashment: two possible outcomes are described (£170 net now under small pots rules vs full amount potentially tax-free at retirement), and while the net £170 route appears to be the recommended course, the report presents both without unambiguously stating only one is recommended.

      - EA5 (Standard Life) cost rationale in the recommendation narrative states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), but the charges comparison table shows the impact as '+0.26%' — an increase. This directly contradicts the stated intended outcome of 'lower costs' for the Standard Life switch. This is an internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction and confirmed by the derived figures (EA5 impact at 0.26% is computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002, making the EA5 charges comparison unreliable).

      - No retirement income projection is present (extraction confirms absent), so the intended outcome of aligning recommendations with 'retirement objectives' cannot be fully assessed against projected figures.

    The requirement has two limbs: (a) whether the change is recommended, and (b) who will implement it and the intended outcome. On limb (a), all five recommendations are explicitly marked 'Recommended' and carry a summary action — this is satisfied. On intended outcomes, four of the five recommendations carry clear, client-specific intended outcomes. The exception is the Scottish Widows encashment (REC2), where the extraction flags an ambiguity: two outcomes are described and while one (£170 net now) appears to be the recommended course, the report does not unambiguously select between them — this is a presentation concern. On limb (b) — who will implement — the extraction explicitly confirms that '/recommendations[]/implementation/willAdviserImplement' and '/recommendations[]/implementation/responsibleParty' are absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument is not recorded for these paths in the expectedButAbsent list, but the canonical model itself notes these paths are absent). The extraction's 'expectedButAbsent' list does not include these paths, meaning the extraction did not find them and did not flag them as expected-but-missing due to a known document gap — the document simply does not state them. The terms and conditions passages note that the adviser is responsible for submitting instructions (e.g. P8: 'Your Adviser is responsible for checking the accuracy of all information, online application and instructions they pass to Us'), which implies adviser involvement in implementation, but this is a generic platform T&C, not a case-specific statement in the suitability report. This cannot substitute for an explicit statement in the report itself. The guard notes that a provider-handled implementation step is still a stated step and the finding is silence — this applies here: regardless of whether the platform or adviser mechanically executes the switches, the report's duty is to state who is responsible, and it does not. Additionally, the Standard Life cost rationale contradiction (0.08% saving stated vs +0.26% increase in the charges table, with the derived figures confirming the EA5 charges row is computed on the wrong fund value) means the stated intended outcome of 'lower costs' for the Standard Life switch is not supported and is in fact contradicted by the report's own charges table. This is a material concern going to the quality of the rationale for that specific recommendation. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment. Overall: the absence of any statement of who will implement the changes across all four non-retain recommendations, the Scottish Widows dual-outcome ambiguity, and the Standard Life cost contradiction together constitute material concerns.

  [G6.7] Recommendations that present multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The report presents five discrete recommendations, each with a single stated action: (1) Retain People's Pension; (2) Encash Scottish Widows under small pots rules; (3) Transfer Aviva into new Aviva platform; (4) Transfer Zurich into new Aviva platform; (5) Transfer Standard Life into new Aviva platform. All five carry adviceStatus 'Recommended'. However, the extraction flags an ambiguity at /recommendations/REC2/taxOutcome: two alternative outcomes are presented for the Scottish Widows encashment — (a) withdraw now under small pots rules receiving approximately £170 net, and (b) leave to grow until retirement and potentially take fully within the personal allowance with no tax due. The extraction notes 'Both are presented but only one is recommended. The net receipt of £170 appears to be the recommended course.'

    File:     File note passage [P1] records 'Scottish widows – alternative option – 30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three', indicating the adviser considered an internal fund switch as the alternative for Scottish Widows before recommending encashment. No file note or other document in the pack explicitly resolves the dual tax-outcome presentation in the report (encash now vs leave to grow) by recording a client decision on that specific point. The suitability report itself states the recommended action is encashment under small pots rules and provides a rationale ('would not detract from your financial retirement plan'), but also presents the leave-to-grow outcome without clearly dismissing it or explaining why encashment now is preferred over deferral.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report presents two tax outcomes for the Scottish Widows encashment (approximately £170 net now under small pots rules vs potentially £0 tax if left to grow and taken within personal allowance at retirement) without explicitly stating which is recommended and providing a reasoned basis for preferring encashment now over deferral — the extraction itself flags this as an ambiguity about which outcome is the recommendation.

      - The canonical model does not capture /recommendations[]/optionsPresented[], /recommendations[]/optionsPresented[]/isRecommended, or /recommendations[]/presentsUnresolvedOptions, so it cannot be confirmed from the model alone whether the report formally resolves the dual-outcome presentation or leaves it open.

      - No file note or supporting document in the pack records a client decision specifically choosing immediate encashment over deferral to retirement, which might otherwise resolve the open option.

    The check method is PresenceOnly: the question is whether any recommendation presents multiple possible outcomes without stating which is recommended and why. The first guard confirms that setting out options and naming one as recommended is good practice, not a finding — the finding is an option left unresolved. The third guard notes that an option presented for information about a future decision is not an unresolved recommendation. For four of the five recommendations (People's Pension retain, Aviva transfer, Zurich transfer, Standard Life transfer) the report states a single clear action with supporting rationale; there is no unresolved option. For the Scottish Widows recommendation, the extraction's own ambiguity note confirms the report presents two tax outcomes. The report does state that encashment under small pots rules is the recommendation ('I would recommend you withdraw the full amount of this pension under the small pots rules') and provides a rationale that it would not detract from the retirement plan. However, the report also presents the leave-to-grow/tax-free-at-retirement scenario without explaining why that option was rejected in favour of immediate encashment. This falls short of the requirement to explain why the recommended option is preferred over the alternative. The leave-to-grow scenario is not a future decision for the client to make later (in which case the third guard would apply) — it is a currently available alternative path that the adviser has implicitly rejected by recommending encashment now, but without reasoning. No file note resolves this by recording a client preference for immediate encashment over deferral. The second guard (client decision in file note resolving an open option) is not satisfied. This is a genuine, if low-severity, concern: the recommended action is stated but the alternative is presented without a reasoned rejection, leaving the client without a clear explanation of why encashing now is better than deferring. The vulnerability overlay does not materially affect this specific finding as no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the pack that would heighten the concern about unclear option presentation.

      · Canonical model /recommendations/REC2/rationale and /ambiguities/recommendations/REC2/taxOutcome [I]

        "I would recommend you withdraw the full amount of this pension under the small pots rules and put the money into your savings account. This would not detract from your financial retirement plan."
