        "Scottish widows – alternative option – 30% liquid fund and 70% of Portfolio three"

 

  [G6.8] Recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where that is material to suitability

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report asserts: (1) five recommendations with actions Retain, Encash, Transfer, Transfer, Transfer; (2) an expected timescale of '10-15 working days' for access to funds (quoted in the context of out-of-market/administration risk); (3) cancellation rights of 30 days once the plan is set up; (4) a next step that the client should complete a nomination of beneficiaries form after the transfer process is begun. The canonical model does not include a per-recommendation responsible party or a per-recommendation expected timescale; those paths are absent from the extracted model.

    File:     The retrieved passages are predominantly Aviva Pension Portfolio Terms and Conditions (category H), key features (category H), a risk profile report (category D), Scottish Widows policy information (category E), Standard Life policy information (category E), and Intelliflo cashflow planning documents (category F). None of the passages directly corroborate or contradict the 10-15 working days timescale stated in the report; however, [P1] (Terms and Conditions) states that changes to regular income payments require at least 10 working days' notice, which is broadly consistent with the order of magnitude. No passage names a responsible party for implementing the transfers. No passage contradicts the cancellation rights period or the nomination of beneficiaries next step.

    Discrepancies:

      - The canonical model does not record a responsible party ('by whom') at per-recommendation level — the extraction confirms these paths are absent from the document, not merely missed by extraction.

      - The canonical model does not record a per-recommendation expected timescale — again confirmed absent from the document.

      - The 10-15 working days timescale is stated in the context of accessing funds after the plan is set up, not explicitly as the timescale for completing the transfers themselves, leaving it ambiguous whether this covers the transfer implementation period.

      - No passage in the evidence pack independently corroborates the 10-15 working days figure as the transfer completion timescale (P1 references 10 working days for income payment changes, a different context).

    The check is a presence-only assessment of whether recommendations state what action will be taken, when, and by whom where material to suitability. The five recommended actions (Retain, Encash, Transfer x3) are clearly stated. The timescale of 10-15 working days is stated, though in the context of future access rather than the transfer process itself; the guard permits a provider service standard to satisfy the timescale requirement, and this figure is close enough in context to satisfy the 'when' limb at a basic level. The responsible party ('by whom') is not named at a per-recommendation level, but the guard explicitly provides that naming the adviser firm rather than an individual satisfies the requirement, and in an advised pension consolidation case the adviser firm's involvement is implicit throughout the report. The nomination of beneficiaries next step is client-specific and actionable. The ambiguity noted in the extraction about the Scottish Widows encashment (two possible outcomes presented) is relevant: the extraction flags that both encash-now and leave-to-grow outcomes are presented, with only one appearing to be the actual recommendation. This creates a concern under the 'multiple possible outcomes without explaining which is recommended' flag in the what-to-look-for criteria. However, the extraction's own ambiguity note suggests the net £170 encashment is the recommended course. On balance, the report broadly satisfies the 'what' and 'when' requirements. The absence of an explicit responsible party is mitigated by the guard. The Scottish Widows dual-outcome presentation is a moderate concern but the canonical model records the action as 'Encash', indicating a single recommendation was made. The overall picture is that the report meets the minimum threshold for this requirement, with the Scottish Widows ambiguity being a residual low-level concern already flagged under other checks.

CHK-007 — Costs, Charges and Value of the Recommendation

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 11 requirement(s). 11 raised a concern (G7.1, G7.2, G7.3, G7.4, G7.5, G7.6, G7.7, G7.8, G7.9, G7.10, G7.11). 1. Investment management charge: report states 0.22% / £253.43; Aviva illustration [P8] states weighted average charge of 0.23%. These differ by 0.01 percentage points. 2. Standard Life EA5 existing charge: the report's charges comparison table records EA5 existing annual recurring charge as 0.18%, but the Standard Life policy illustration [P20] shows fund charges of 1.0% less discount of 0.5% = net 0.50%. The 0.18% recorded in the report for EA5 appears to correspond to Zurich's fund charge, not Standard Life's — consistent with the derived figures flag that EA5 rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002). 3. Standard Life cost comparison contradiction (already noted in internal inconsistencies): report narrative (page 8) states Standard Life existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying a saving from switching; the charges table (page 11) shows impact of recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%', implying switching is more expensive. These are directly contradictory. 23 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G7.1] All material costs disclosed — product, platform, fund, transaction, advice

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report discloses the following recommended plan charges: Platform (Aviva) 0.21% / £231.39 pa; Investment Management 0.22% / £253.43 pa; Total Charge 0.44% / £484.81 pa. Individual fund charges are disclosed: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18% and CT Universal MAP Balanced C 0.29%. Transaction costs are explicitly excluded with a stated reason (variable, already reflected in net performance figures). Initial advice fee of 3% / £3,305.55 is disclosed, paid by deduction from the pension schemes on transfer. Ongoing adviser fee is referenced (Client Agreement). Aggregated total and cumulative effect are stated as disclosed. DFM/discretionary fee is noted as not applicable. The illustration is referenced for cumulative charge impact. A warning is given that monetary figures may differ from the illustration due to calculation methodology differences. The existing plan charges comparison table (page 11) shows: Aviva EA3 existing 0.50%, Zurich EA4 existing 0.93%, Standard Life EA5 existing 0.18% (per canonical model). The report states the new plan is 0.24% less expensive on a like-for-like basis and 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees. For Standard Life (EA5), the report narrative (page 8) states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), while the charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative as '+0.26%' (an increase).

    File:     [P3] confirms the Summary of Plan Charges table: Platform 0.21% / £231.39, Investment Management 0.22% / £253.43, Total Charge 0.44% / £484.81, consistent with the report. [P3] also contains the Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment table. [P8] (Aviva illustration) confirms the Aviva platform charge of 0.21% and fund OCF/TER: CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc 0.29%, HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc 0.18%, weighted average charge 0.23%. [P1] (KIID) confirms HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc ongoing charge of 0.18%, entry/exit charges 0.00%, no performance fee. [P37] (KIID) confirms CT Universal MAP Balanced C ongoing charge of 0.29% (to be verified from truncated content). [P17] confirms initial advice fee of 3% / £3,305.55 paid by deduction from pension schemes on transfer. [P10] (SIPP research report) discloses fund-level transaction costs: CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc transaction costs £145.05 (0.26%); total aggregated fund costs including transaction costs £406.74 (0.37%), ongoing costs £258.93 (0.24%). [P33] (Aviva illustration) shows cumulative charge impact table: at end of year 1, after all charges £108,000 vs before charges £113,000; at age 75, after all charges £115,000 vs before charges £129,000. Product and investment charges reduce growth from 2.9% to 2.5% (reduction 0.4%); all charges reduce growth from 2.9% to 0.8% (reduction 2.1%). [P8] weighted average investment charge shown as 0.23%, whereas the report states 0.22%. [P5] and [P23] show the pension switch report projections for Standard Life and Zurich existing plans. [P20] shows Standard Life existing plan charges: fund charges 1.0%, discount -0.5%, net 0.5% — consistent with EA5 existing charge of 0.18% per report? No: the Standard Life illustration shows 0.5% net charge, but the report's charges table records EA5 existing charge as 0.18%. [P7] shows Zurich existing plan charges including bid-to-offer spread of 5%, annual management charge of 0.75% pa, plus additional charges. The report records EA4 existing charge as 0.93%.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Investment management charge: report states 0.22% / £253.43; Aviva illustration [P8] states weighted average charge of 0.23%. These differ by 0.01 percentage points.

      - 2. Standard Life EA5 existing charge: the report's charges comparison table records EA5 existing annual recurring charge as 0.18%, but the Standard Life policy illustration [P20] shows fund charges of 1.0% less discount of 0.5% = net 0.50%. The 0.18% recorded in the report for EA5 appears to correspond to Zurich's fund charge, not Standard Life's — consistent with the derived figures flag that EA5 rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002).

      - 3. Standard Life cost comparison contradiction (already noted in internal inconsistencies): report narrative (page 8) states Standard Life existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', implying a saving from switching; the charges table (page 11) shows impact of recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%', implying switching is more expensive. These are directly contradictory.

      - 4. Transaction costs: the SIPP research report [P10] discloses fund-level transaction costs of £145.05 (0.26%) for CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc as a separate quantified line. The suitability report excludes transaction costs with a stated reason. The research report quantifies them, but the suitability report does not include even an estimated figure despite a quantified figure being available in the file.

      - 5. EA5 charge comparison rows in the charges table appear to be computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's fund value (£3,002), per derived figures: EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of approximately £103,439, not £3,002. This renders the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable.

      - 6. Zurich existing plan: report records EA4 existing charge as 0.93%. Zurich policy info [P7] discloses a bid-to-offer spread of 5% on all payments and AMC of 0.75% pa plus additional charges. Whether 0.93% correctly captures all Zurich charges (including the bid-to-offer spread impact) is not fully reconcilable from the illustration alone, though 0.93% as an ongoing annual charge figure is plausible if the bid-to-offer spread is treated as a one-off allocation charge rather than an ongoing cost.

      - 7. The report states both '0.24% less expensive' (like-for-like) and '4.24% more expensive' (including adviser fees) without clearly reconciling the two figures for the reader, creating potential for misunderstanding about the net cost impact of the recommendation.

    The report discloses the principal cost layers — platform (0.21%), investment management (0.22%), total ongoing (0.44%), initial advice fee (3% / £3,305.55), and transaction cost exclusion with reasons — and references the illustration for cumulative impact. The Aviva illustration [P33] provides the required cumulative charge table. The KIIDs [P1] and [P37] corroborate the fund OCF figures. However, several material concerns arise. First, the investment management weighted average charge in the Aviva illustration [P8] is 0.23%, while the report states 0.22%. This is a minor rounding difference of 0.01% and may fall within the false-positive guard for rounding differences, but it is a discrepancy that should be noted. Second, and more seriously, the Standard Life (EA5) cost comparison rows in the charges table appear to have been computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's fund value (£3,002). The derived figures confirm this: EA5 existing charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a base of approximately £103,439. This means the monetary figures for Standard Life in the cost comparison are overstated by a factor of approximately 34x and the comparison is arithmetically unreliable. Third, the report narrative on page 8 states that the Standard Life existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a cost saving from switching), while the charges table on page 11 shows the impact as '+0.26%' (a cost increase from switching). These are directly contradictory statements within the same document, and the latter figure is itself computed on the wrong fund value. This is a material inconsistency bearing on whether the switching recommendation for Standard Life is cost-justified. Fourth, transaction costs are excluded with a stated reason, which satisfies the guard for stated exclusions. However, the SIPP research report [P10] quantifies transaction costs for CT Universal MAP Balanced C Acc at £145.05 (0.26%), meaning a quantified estimate was available but not disclosed to the client. The guard treats a stated exclusion as a disclosure rather than an omission, so this is moderated — but the availability of a figure in the supporting research elevates this from a mere variable-cost exclusion to a case where the adviser had the figure and chose not to include it. Fifth, the EA5 existing charge of 0.18% recorded in the charges table conflicts with the Standard Life policy illustration [P20] which shows a net charge of 0.50% (1.0% fund charge less 0.5% discount). The 0.18% appears to be Zurich's fund charge misapplied to Standard Life. The report presents both '0.24% less expensive' and '4.24% more expensive' figures without a clear reconciliation, though these measure different things (with and without adviser fee) and the distinction is explained in the report structure. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The contradiction between the Standard Life cost saving narrative and the charges table, combined with the wrong-fund-value computation for EA5, constitutes a material concern about the reliability of the cost comparison for that plan.

  [G7.2] Initial and ongoing adviser fees, consistent with the client agreement

  Potential Concern — severity Low

    Report:   The suitability report states an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested (per the Client Agreement), equating to £3,305.55 with current transfer values, paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer. An ongoing fee of 1% per annum is stated, equating to circa £1,101.85 for the first year, paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. Services provided for the ongoing fee are listed (ATR review, suitability review, market/legislation updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic financial planning).

    File:     The client agreement document (IMPORT_2.md / TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md) confirms a standard initial advice fee of 3% of the value of funds advised on (minimum £1,350) and an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% (minimum £100 per annum). The client agreement states fees are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation. The suitability report itself (P5, P9) repeats the 3% initial and 1% ongoing figures. The figure £3,305.55 is corroborated in P12 and £1,101.85 in P5. The client agreement does not specify a fixed monetary amount for the initial fee — it states the percentage basis, which is consistent with the report's statement that the monetary amount may fluctuate with transfer values. The client agreement (P12) notes fees are set out in the suitability report. The field matchesClientAgreement is null in the extracted model, meaning the report itself makes no explicit assertion of agreement-consistency beyond referencing 'as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement'.

    Discrepancies:

      - The extracted model field /costsAndCharges/adviserFees[]/matchesClientAgreement is null — the report does not explicitly assert that the fees match the client agreement, only references it. This is a silence in the report rather than a contradiction.

      - The monetary initial fee of £3,305.55 implies a fund value of £110,185 (3% of £110,185 = £3,305.55), consistent with the total transfer value of the three switching plans (£110,185.60). However, the client agreement does not independently state this monetary figure — it only states the 3% rate. The monetary amount is therefore derived from the transfer value and is not contradicted but also not independently corroborated by a separate client agreement monetary figure.

      - The ongoing fee of £1,101.85 at 1% implies a fund value of approximately £110,185 minus the initial fee deduction (£110,185 - £3,305.55 = £106,879.45; 1% = £1,068.79), which does not precisely equal £1,101.85. Alternatively, 1% of £110,185 = £1,101.85, suggesting the report has not deducted the initial fee from the base for this calculation despite saying 'minus the initial fee'. This is a minor internal arithmetic tension but the report states 'circa' and 'expected contributions' which may explain it.

      - No separate standalone client agreement document specifying bespoke fee rates for this individual client (as distinct from the standard terms document) is present in the evidence pack to allow a direct comparison of agreed specific rates.

    The report states an initial fee of 3% and ongoing fee of 1%, both referencing the Client Agreement. The standard terms document in the file (IMPORT_2.md, P13) confirms 3% initial and 1% ongoing as the firm's standard rates, which is consistent. The monetary initial fee of £3,305.55 is arithmetically consistent with 3% of £110,185 (the total transfer value), and is corroborated in P12. The £1,101.85 ongoing fee is corroborated in P5. The report states the ongoing fee is 'minus the initial fee' but 1% of the full £110,185 = £1,101.85, not 1% of £110,185 less £3,305.55 — however the report uses 'circa' and references 'expected contributions', and the guard notes the monetary fee moves with transfer value, so this is a low-level rounding/basis ambiguity rather than a material contradiction. The key limitation is that the client agreement document in the file sets fees by reference to the suitability report (circular), and no bespoke signed fee schedule exists in the pack as an independent corroborating document. The matchesClientAgreement field being null means the report does not itself make an explicit consistency assertion. The standard terms are consistent with the rates charged. No vulnerability factors in the file are identified as materially affecting this cost/charges check. Overall, the rates are consistent with the standard client agreement on file, the monetary amounts are arithmetically supportable, and the services attached to the ongoing fee are described. The absence of a bespoke signed fee schedule is a modest gap but the standard terms document corroborates the rates. No material contradiction is present.

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P9)

        "For researching, recommending and arranging these investments, we charge an initial advice fee of 3% of the amount invested as per the terms stated in our Client Agreement. In monetary terms this is £3,305.55 with current transfer values, this figure may fluctuate depending on transfer values. This will be paid directly by the pension schemes on transfer."

      · Suitability Report Test 1.md [I] (P5)

        "The costs for this service are 1% per annum. Based on current values & expected contributions, minus the initial fee, this would be equal to circa £1,101.85 for the first year."

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P13)

        "There will be a charge of 3% of the value of the funds that we advise on. In order to ensure we are able to consistently provide a high standard of professional service, our minimum initial advice fee for Pensions & Investment related advice is £1,350."

      · IMPORT_2.md [A] (P3)

        "For most investment funds that we transact on your behalf, we charge an on-going annual review management charge of 1% ** (with a minimum fee of £100 per annum)."

      · TFAS Wealth Client Agreement Privacy.md [A] (P12)

        "The fees you are required to pay for our advisory services are set out in the suitability report and any subsequent charges confirmation."

 

  [G7.3] Cumulative impact of costs and charges

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 18, Critical Yield section) states: Low growth (0.00%): Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 2.1%, Critical yield 1.9%, Effect of switching £10,400 / 9.6% decrease. Mid growth (2.95%): Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 2.2%, Critical yield 4.8%, Effect of switching £12,000 / 9.4% decrease. High growth (5.88%): Existing RIY 0.3%, New solution RIY 2.3%, Critical yield 7.8%, Effect of switching £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. Retirement age 75. The report also states (page 11) a mid-growth rate of 2.94% and a required outperformance of £4,672 (4.24%) including adviser fees. The new-plan illustration (page 18) shows a new-solution RIY of 2.1% at low growth consistent with the illustration's own disclosure.

    File:     [P1] (suitability report appendix table) and [P30] (pension switch research report) both confirm: Low: Existing 0.3%, New 2.1%, CY 1.9%, Effect £10,400/9.6%; Mid: Existing 0.3%, New 2.2%, CY 4.8%, Effect £12,000/9.4%; High: Existing 0.3%, New 2.3%, CY 7.8%, Effect £15,000/10.0%. [P10] (Aviva illustration) shows all-charges RIY of 2.1% at medium growth (5% gross / 2.9% real), consistent with the new-solution RIY figure. [P11] (suitability report body) states the mid growth rate as 2.94%, while [P1] and [P30] label the mid growth rate as 2.95%. The pension switch report [P30] labels the mid rate as 2.95%; the suitability report body [P11] calls it 2.94%. The derived figures note: the £10,400 loss at 9.6% implies a fund of ~£108,333; the £12,000 loss at 9.4% implies ~£127,660; the £15,000 loss at 10% implies £150,000 — these are broadly consistent with the existing projected values (£108,000, £127,000, £150,000) in [P1]/[P30] given rounding. The EA5 Standard Life charge row in the switch report is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life per-plan comparison unreliable. The switch report [P23] shows a like-for-like (no adviser fee) combined comparison producing much smaller effects (e.g. mid: £2,000 / 1.6% decrease), whereas the suitability report's critical yield table includes adviser charges (£3,305 initial + 1% ongoing), producing the larger £12,000/9.4% figures shown — this is the correct basis for the appendix table per [P17] and [P23].

    Discrepancies:

      - Mid growth rate label: suitability report body [P11] states 2.94%; suitability report appendix table [P1] and pension switch report [P30] state 2.95%.

      - Derived figures show £10,400 at 9.6% implies a base fund of ~£108,333, while the existing projected value at low growth is £108,000 — a ~£333 rounding gap, not arithmetically precise.

      - Derived figures show £12,000 at 9.4% implies a base fund of ~£127,660, while the existing projected value at mid growth is £127,000 — a ~£660 gap.

      - EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the charges comparison table are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making per-plan Standard Life figures unreliable (noted in derived figures).

      - The pension switch report [P19] shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (more expensive) while the suitability report body states the Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution — these are contradictory cost-saving claims for Standard Life specifically, though this primarily affects CHK-009 per-plan rationale rather than the consolidated critical yield table.

      - The Aviva illustration [P10] shows a weighted average OCF/TER of 0.23% while the suitability report charges table [P19] records 0.22% investment management charge — a 0.01% rounding difference.

    The core critical yield table figures (RIY, critical yield percentages, effect-of-switching amounts and percentages at all three growth rates) are directly corroborated by both [P1] (the suitability report's own appendix) and [P30] (the pension switch research report). The Aviva provider illustration [P10] independently confirms an all-charges RIY of 2.1% at medium growth, consistent with the new-solution RIY. The cumulative impact is therefore disclosed and supported by the evidence.

   

    The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid growth rate discrepancy between the report body [P11] and the appendix/switch report [P1][P30] is a minor labelling inconsistency. It falls within the rounding guard and does not alter any of the projection values or conclusions.

   

    The rounding gaps in the effect-of-switching arithmetic (£108,333 vs £108,000 etc.) arise from rounding the projected values to three significant figures, which is standard practice. These do not constitute arithmetically impossible figures.

   

    The EA5 Standard Life charge-row error (computed on Zurich's value) is a genuine calculation error in the charges comparison table. However, it does not affect the consolidated critical yield table, which is computed on the aggregate £110,185 transfer value and is independently corroborated by [P30]. Its main impact is on the per-plan switching justification for Standard Life, which is material for CHK-009.

   

    The Standard Life 0.08% saving claim vs the +0.26% table figure is a direct contradiction in the per-plan cost narrative, but again this is the per-plan switching rationale rather than the consolidated cumulative impact table.

   

    The illustration OCF 0.23% vs report 0.22% difference is a minor rounding difference.

   

    Vulnerability overlay: No vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the file that would alter this assessment.

   

    On balance, the consolidated critical yield and cumulative impact disclosure is complete and corroborated. The EA5 charge computation error and the Standard Life saving-claim contradiction are material concerns for cost accuracy at the per-plan level but do not invalidate the overall critical yield table. Given the internal inconsistency between the Standard Life saving narrative and the charges table — which the extraction flags as a directly contradictory pair — a Potential Concern is warranted at Moderate severity.

  [G7.4] Cost comparison with alternative options

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 11) presents a 'Pension Switch Charges Only Assessment' comparing existing plans against the recommended Aviva Pension Portfolio. EA3 (Aviva existing): 0.50% / £18.72 pa, impact -0.06% / -£2.25. EA4 (Zurich): 0.93% / £961.98 pa, impact -0.49% / -£506.85. EA5 (Standard Life): 0.18% / £186.19 pa, impact +0.26% / +£268.94. On a like-for-like basis the report states 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' and 'your proposed plan can underperform your current plan by £264 in the first year'. The report separately states (page 8) that the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching to Aviva saves 0.08% on Standard Life). The new plan total recurring charge is 0.44% (platform 0.21% + IMC 0.22%). Including adviser fees the plan is 4.24% more expensive / £4,672 outperformance required. Mid-growth critical yield is stated as 4.8% based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94% (page 11 narrative) / 2.95% (page 18 table).

    File:     [P3] (suitability report, page 11) confirms the charges table: Aviva 0.50% / £18.72, impact -0.06% / -£2.25; Zurich 0.93% / £961.98, impact -0.49% / -£506.85; Standard Life 0.18% / £186.19, impact +0.26% / +£268.94; and the like-for-like conclusion '0.24% less expensive' / '£264'. [P1] confirms existing arrangement charges: Aviva 0.50%, Standard Life 0.18% / 0.52%. [P8] (Standard Life illustration) shows fund charges 1.0% less discount -0.5% = net 0.5%, not 0.18% used in the comparison table. [P2] (Zurich policy info) shows a bid-offer spread of 5% and annual management charge of 0.75% plus allocation rates. [P35] (Aviva illustration) confirms platform charge 0.21%; weighted average investment charge 0.23% (not 0.22% as the report states). [P3] also confirms the new plan total 0.44% / £484.81 and the £4,672 / 4.24% including adviser fees. The derived figures flag that EA5's monetary amounts (£186.19 at 0.18% implies fund value ~£103,439, which is Zurich's value, not Standard Life's £3,002); EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26% also implies ~£103,439). [P19] states mid-growth rate of 2.94%; [P21] labels the mid-growth rate as 2.95% in the critical yield table. No provider documentation confirms 0.18% as Standard Life's all-in charge — the Standard Life illustration [P8] shows 0.5% net. The pension switch research report [P6] lists an investment charge of 0.18% for one fund but does not name Standard Life. The research report [P18] notes all Standard Life funds in the same range are more expensive than Aviva.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. EA5 (Standard Life) monetary charge £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002. The charge and impact rows for Standard Life appear to have been calculated on the wrong fund value (Zurich's), making the cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically incorrect and unreliable.

      - 2. EA5 impact amount £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), confirming the Standard Life row is computed on Zurich's plan value rather than Standard Life's £3,002.

      - 3. The report (page 8) states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges table on page 11 shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% — a cost increase, not a saving. These are directly contradictory.

      - 4. Standard Life's existing annual charge is stated as 0.18% in the comparison table, but the Standard Life policy illustration [P8] shows fund charge 1.0% less discount 0.5% = net 0.5%, not 0.18%. No provider document in the pack confirms 0.18% as the Standard Life all-in charge.

      - 5. The Aviva illustration [P35] states the weighted average investment charge as 0.23%, whereas the suitability report states 0.22% (investment management charge). This is a minor discrepancy between the illustration and the report.

      - 6. Mid-growth rate is stated as 2.94% on page 11 narrative [P19] but as 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 [P21] — an internal inconsistency within the report.

      - 7. Transaction costs for underlying funds are excluded from all charge figures; the report acknowledges this [P3] but states they are accounted for in net performance figures. No monetary estimate is provided.

      - 8. The Zurich policy documentation [P2] discloses a bid-offer spread of 5% and AMC of 0.75%; these are not separately reconciled against the 0.93% total used in the comparison table, though the 0.93% could reflect a combined or blended rate. No provider document in the pack explicitly confirms 0.93% as the current all-in annual recurring charge.

    The most significant finding is the Standard Life (EA5) row in the charges comparison table. The derived figures establish conclusively that the monetary charge (£186.19) and impact (£268.94) attributed to Standard Life were both calculated using a fund value of approximately £103,439 — which is Zurich's fund value — rather than Standard Life's own current value of £3,002. This is not a rounding difference; it is a factor of ~34 error in the base. As a result, the percentage figures in that row (0.18% existing, +0.26% impact) cannot be accepted as reliable representations of the cost differential for Standard Life. This arithmetic error directly underlies the separate internal contradiction: page 8 of the report claims Standard Life is 0.08% more expensive (a saving for switching), while page 11's table shows +0.26% (a cost increase from switching). Both cannot be correct, and the derived figures confirm the table row is itself wrong. The Standard Life policy illustration [P8] further shows a net charge of 0.5%, not 0.18%, adding a third inconsistent figure. The overall like-for-like saving of 0.24% / £264 is therefore computed from a table that contains at least one materially wrong row (Standard Life), making the aggregate comparison unreliable. The Aviva illustration [P35] shows a weighted average IMC of 0.23% vs the report's stated 0.22% — a low-severity discrepancy but present. The mid-growth rate discrepancy (2.94% vs 2.95%) is a rounding/labelling issue of low materiality. The bid-offer spread on the Zurich plan (5%) is not reflected in the ongoing charge comparison, though the 0.93% total may absorb most ongoing costs; the pack does not contain a document explicitly confirming 0.93% as the current all-in Zurich recurring charge, though the figure is self-consistent with the plan value. The Comparisons on different bases guard is noted: the 0.24% cheaper and 4.24% more expensive figures use different bases (excluding and including adviser fees respectively) and are not contradictory in themselves, but the Standard Life row error contaminates the 0.24% figure. Transaction costs are excluded with an explanation [P3], which partially mitigates that omission, though no estimate is given. No vulnerability indicators are present in the pack that would alter this analysis. The Standard Life arithmetic error and the directly contradictory cost-saving claim are genuine findings that survive all guards.

  [G7.5] Benefit versus cost — fair value

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts six benefits to justify the recommendation: (1) Lower Costs — the receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure; (2) Greater Investment Choice; (3) Adviser Charging Facilitation; (4) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments; (5) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility; (6) Aviva financial strength (A-rated, £407bn AUM, 6,900+ funds). For cost justification, the report states on a like-for-like basis the new plan is 0.24% less expensive (£264 underperformance tolerance), but including adviser fees the new plan is 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 outperformance required). The critical yield at mid-growth of 2.94% is 4.8% per year to age 75, described as achievable based on past performance. For Standard Life specifically (page 8), the report states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. The charges table (page 11) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase under the new plan). Transaction costs in monetary terms are absent from the report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument).

    File:     The SIPP research report and suitability report passages confirm: (a) the recommended funds are HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc (0.18% annual charge, 50%) and CT Universal MAP Balanced C (0.29%, 50%), both risk-rated 5 [P6]; (b) past performance of recommended funds (Aviva Risk 5 portfolio, 5yr cumulative 37.25%–42.40%) exceeds the existing Aviva fund (15.58%) but does not outperform the existing Zurich fund (44.32% 5yr) [P1, P2]; (c) the file notes confirm Standard Life funds in the 20–60% equity range are more expensive than what Aviva can offer, but do not quantify the saving [P19]; (d) the charges comparison table internal inconsistency (Standard Life +0.26% vs narrative −0.08%) is recorded as an internalInconsistency in the extraction; (e) the derived figures confirm EA5 (Standard Life) annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% is computed against Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) not Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life row arithmetically unreliable; (f) the derived figures confirm the 'Lower Costs' benefit claim is only partially true: on a like-for-like basis costs are lower by 0.24%, but including adviser fees the client pays 4.24% more; (g) transaction costs monetary figure is confirmed absent by extraction; (h) the cashflow modeller [P23] excludes the three transferred pensions (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) from the proposed scenario, labelling them 'EXCLUDED FROM SCENARIO', limiting the value of the cashflow as a benefit demonstration; (i) the critical yield table labels the mid growth rate as 2.95% (page 18) while page 11 narrative states 2.94%.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life cost saving: report narrative (page 8) states existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switch saves 0.08%), but the charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — a cost increase, not a saving. These are directly contradictory.

      - EA5 (Standard Life) charge row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) not Standard Life's own value (£3,002), per derived figures, making any cost comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable.

      - 'Lower Costs' benefit stated as a primary justification, but including adviser fees the new plan is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance — the report presents both figures without reconciling the tension or explaining why the net higher cost still represents fair value.

      - Critical yield mid-growth rate stated as 2.94% on page 11 but labelled 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 — marginal inconsistency.

      - Transaction costs (monetary) are entirely absent from the report — confirmed NotPresentInDocument by extraction. No estimate or exclusion reason is given.

      - The cashflow modeller proposed scenario excludes the three pension plans recommended for transfer (Aviva SP50163762, Zurich P10026-719-001/DL, Standard Life D2301334000), labelling them 'EXCLUDED FROM SCENARIO', so the cashflow does not demonstrate the projected benefit of consolidation.

      - The 'achievability' of the 4.8% critical yield is asserted by reference to past performance, but the recommended funds (Aviva Risk 5 portfolio) underperform the existing Zurich fund (44.32% vs 42.40% over 5 years) — the past performance data in the file does not unambiguously support the assertion for the client who holds Zurich as their dominant holding (£103,439).

    The primary 'Lower Costs' benefit asserted in the report is undermined by two separate issues. First, the Standard Life cost comparison is internally contradictory: the narrative claims a 0.08% saving but the charges table shows a +0.26% increase, and the derived figures establish that the Standard Life charge row was computed against Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's £3,002 value, making the row arithmetically wrong on its face. This is not a rounding or presentation difference — it is a material error in the cost comparison for one of the three transferred plans. Second, the report's own figures show that including adviser fees the client pays 4.24% more (£4,672 outperformance required), yet the report leads with 'Lower Costs' as a headline benefit without adequately reconciling this. The like-for-like 0.24% saving is real but the net position including adviser charges is a significant cost increase, and the report does not explain with sufficient specificity why the benefits justify this increase for this client. The critical yield justification ('achievable based on past performance') is a bare assertion: the performance data in the file [P1, P2] shows the recommended funds underperformed the existing Zurich fund over 5 years, which is the dominant holding. The guard requires supporting performance data before accepting this claim, and the data available actually cuts against it for the main plan. Transaction costs are absent without reason or estimate — a moderate gap. The cashflow modeller excluding the transferred pensions further weakens the benefit demonstration. The 2.94%/2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is low-severity rounding. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded in the evidence that would materially alter this analysis. Taken together, the Standard Life charge table contradiction (with an arithmetically wrong base value), the unreconciled net cost increase, the unsubstantiated achievability claim against contrary performance data, and the absent transaction costs constitute material concerns that the benefit-versus-cost assessment is not adequately supported.

  [G7.6] Costs are excessive relative to the benefits provided

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   On a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fees) the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than the current plans, generating an underperformance tolerance of £264. Including adviser fees, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than the current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance. The report also contains a charges comparison table (page 11) which shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26%, while page 8 states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving). The EA5 (Standard Life) rows in the charges table are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the per-plan comparison unreliable.

    File:     This requirement is assessed from the report alone — the canonical model and derived figures are the evidence. The derived figures confirm: (a) the 4.24% required outperformance implies a fund value of ~£110,189, broadly consistent with the new plan transfer value of £110,185; (b) the EA5 annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002 — so the EA5 row is computed on the wrong plan; (c) the EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% likewise implies Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's; (d) page 8 states switching Standard Life saves 0.08%, but the charges table shows +0.26% impact (a cost increase) for Standard Life — a direct contradiction; (e) no transaction costs in monetary terms are present (extraction path /costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary marked NotPresentInDocument).

    Discrepancies:

      - Page 8 states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table on page 11 shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% — a cost increase, not a saving. These are directly contradictory.

      - EA5 annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) implies a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's own current value of £3,002 — the EA5 row in the charges table is computed on the wrong plan.

      - EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26%) likewise implies Zurich's fund value (~£103,439), not Standard Life's £3,002 — reinforcing that the EA5 cost comparison is unreliable.

      - Transaction costs in monetary terms are absent from the report (path /costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary marked NotPresentInDocument).

      - The like-for-like saving (0.24% / £264) and the all-in cost increase (4.24% / £4,672) are both stated without a clear reconciliation narrative explaining the adviser fee quantum bridging the two figures, creating a risk that a client reading one figure without the other would be materially misled.

    The requirement tests whether costs are excessive relative to benefits. Two material issues arise independently of the higher-cost guard. First, the contradiction between page 8 (Standard Life switch saves 0.08%) and the charges table on page 11 (+0.26% impact for Standard Life) is not a rounding difference — the sign is reversed. The guard acknowledges that higher cost is not automatically poor value, but that guard applies to the overall recommendation, not to an internal contradiction within the report's own cost comparison. A client or reviewer relying on either figure would reach a different conclusion about whether the Standard Life switch is cost-justified. Second, the derived figures confirm that the EA5 rows in the charges table are computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002). This means the monetary charge figures and percentage impacts stated for Standard Life in the comparison are arithmetically wrong on their face — the report's own numbers do not support them. These two issues together mean the cost comparison for Standard Life is both internally contradictory and arithmetically unsound. The overall 4.24% / £4,672 required outperformance figure appears arithmetically consistent with the new plan transfer value of ~£110,185, and the report does present benefits (consolidation, fund range, platform functionality) alongside the cost increase. However, the benefit justification rests in part on the Standard Life switch, whose cost basis is demonstrably miscalculated. The absence of transaction costs in monetary form is a further gap. No vulnerability factors are identified in the pack that would materially alter this assessment beyond the general duty to ensure clarity for all clients. The Standard Life contradiction and wrong-plan arithmetic are genuine findings not explained by any guard.

  [G7.7] Alternatives provide better cost or benefit to the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The canonical model holds no value at the comparativeCost path for alternatives considered. However, the suitability report (per extracted passages) makes cost-based claims for each switch: (1) Aviva existing plan is stated as '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 7 / P7); (2) Standard Life is stated as '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 / P10); (3) Zurich switch is justified primarily on investment choice, fund blend limitations and flexibility grounds, not cost saving. The report also states the new plan is '0.24% less expensive than your current plans' on a like-for-like basis but '4.24% more expensive than your current plans' including adviser fees. The charges comparison table (page 11 / P8) shows: Aviva impact -0.06%; Standard Life impact +0.26%. For Standard Life, the narrative says the new plan is cheaper (0.08% saving) but the table shows +0.26% (i.e. the new plan is more expensive). Additionally, the file notes (P18) state 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' as the reason for discounting Standard Life as an internal switch alternative — this is a cost-based rejection of the internal alternative, which is consistent. However the report's own narrative on page 8 claims a cost saving of 0.08% for Standard Life vs the recommended solution, while the charges table shows the impact as +0.26% (a cost increase). These are directly contradictory within the report itself.

    File:     P8 (charges comparison table) shows: Standard Life existing annual charge 0.18% / £186.19; impact of recommended alternative +0.26% / £268.94 — meaning the new plan costs MORE for the Standard Life sub-case. P10 (suitability report page 8) states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — meaning the new plan is CHEAPER. These two figures directly contradict each other. The derived figures confirm the Standard Life row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), rendering the monetary comparison for Standard Life unreliable. P2 and P5 (pension switch report) show projected maturity values for each alternative sub-case. P18 (file notes) records Standard Life discounted because 'all funds within the same range of 20-60% equities are more expensive than what we can offer at Aviva' — this supports rejection of Standard Life as an internal switch on cost grounds but does not resolve the internal contradiction between the narrative 0.08% saving and the table +0.26% increase. P11 and P7 provide rationale for Zurich and Aviva switches emphasising investment choice, fund availability and flexibility rather than cost alone. The pension switch report (P2, P5) provides projected maturity value comparisons across growth scenarios, showing the new solution at various rates.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life narrative (P10) claims new plan is 0.08% cheaper than existing Standard Life plan; charges comparison table (P8) shows impact of recommended alternative as +0.26% (a cost increase of £268.94). These are contradictory: one says new plan is cheaper for Standard Life, the other says it is more expensive.

      - Derived figures confirm the Standard Life charges row (EA5 impact: £268.94 at 0.26%) is computed using Zurich's current value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), meaning the monetary charge comparison for Standard Life is calculated on the wrong plan value and is arithmetically unreliable.

      - EA5 existing annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) is likewise derived from Zurich's value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's value (£3,002.00), compounding the error in the Standard Life row.

      - The report states the new plan is '0.24% less expensive than your current plans' on a like-for-like basis but '4.24% more expensive' including adviser fees (P8), presenting a material cost increase without clearly reconciling the two figures for the client.

      - Transaction costs are excluded from the disclosed charges (P8 states they are excluded because they are variable), and no monetary estimate is provided — the extraction confirms /costsAndCharges/transactionCostsMonetary is absent from the document.

      - The canonical model has no value at /recommendations[]/alternativesConsidered[]/comparativeCost, meaning no structured comparative cost data for alternatives is captured in the report's extracted model.

    The central issue for G7.7 is whether the alternatives considered were appropriately assessed on cost and benefit grounds, and whether any higher-cost recommendation is adequately justified. Two specific problems arise. First, the Standard Life cost claim is internally contradictory: the narrative on page 8 (P10) asserts the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a cost saving from switching), but the charges comparison table (P8) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% (a cost increase of £268.94). The derived figures make this worse: the +0.26% / £268.94 figure is computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (~£3,002), so the entire Standard Life charge comparison row is based on the wrong plan value. This means the client has been presented with a cost saving claim (0.08% cheaper) that is contradicted by a table showing a cost increase (+0.26%), and that table figure is itself arithmetically invalid because it uses the wrong base. Second, the overall recommendation, including adviser fees, results in the new plan being 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 required outperformance). The report does present non-cost benefits for each switch (investment choice, fund availability at risk level 5, retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, lifestyling concerns) and the file notes (P18) corroborate the research basis for discounting alternatives. The guard — a cheaper option rejected for evidenced non-cost reasons — applies to Zurich and Aviva where the rationale is predominantly non-cost. However, for Standard Life, the report positively claims a cost saving that is contradicted by its own table and is computed on the wrong fund value. This is a material internal contradiction that cannot be explained away by the guard, because the report is not rejecting a cheaper alternative on non-cost grounds — it is affirmatively claiming a cost saving that does not hold up. Transaction costs are excluded with a stated reason (variable, reflected in performance figures net of charges — P8), which is a Moderate concern per the decision logic. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. Outcome: Potential Concern at High severity, driven by the Standard Life cost claim contradiction and the wrong-base arithmetic in the charges table.

  [G7.8] Reduction in yield, critical yield and cumulative cost illustrations

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report (page 18, Critical Yield section) presents a table across three growth scenarios (Low 0.00%, Mid 2.95%, High 5.88%) at age 75. Existing pension RIY: 0.3% at all three rates. New solution RIY: 2.1% (Low), 2.2% (Mid), 2.3% (High). Critical yield: 1.9% (Low), 4.8% (Mid), 7.8% (High). Effect of switching: £10,400 / 9.6% decrease (Low); £12,000 / 9.4% decrease (Mid); £15,000 / 10.0% decrease (High). The narrative on page 11 references a mid-growth rate of 2.94% (not 2.95%) and states the critical yield is 4.8%. The report presents these figures as the basis for the recommendation, asserting that the 4.8% critical yield at mid-growth is achievable based on past fund performance.

    File:     Passage [P17] (suitability report appendix table) and [P2] (pension switch research report) both corroborate the report's core critical yield table exactly: existing RIY 0.3% and new solution RIY 2.1%/2.2%/2.3% across Low/Mid/High; critical yields 1.9%/4.8%/7.8%; effect of switching £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%), £15,000 (10.0%). Passage [P13] (new Aviva illustration) shows an all-charges RIY of 2.1% at a growth rate of 2.9% after inflation, which is broadly consistent with the new solution RIY at mid. Passage [P16] (suitability report narrative) quotes a mid-growth rate of 2.94% and critical yield of 4.8%. Passage [P1] (pension switch report, separate comparison scenario) shows a different table with new solution RIY of 0.6% and critical yield of 0.3%/3.2%/6.0% — this is a different comparison run (retaining different plans) and is not the same scenario as the recommended switch. However, the mid-growth rate label in the switch report passages is 2.95% throughout, while the suitability report narrative (page 11, [P16]) states 2.94% — a marginal discrepancy flagged as an internal inconsistency. The EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the derived figures are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetically unreliable. The suitability report narrative states the Standard Life plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution, but the charges table on page 11 shows +0.26% impact — a direct contradiction already identified in the extraction.

    Discrepancies:

      - Mid-growth rate label: the suitability report narrative (page 11) states 2.94%, but the critical yield table (page 18) and all switch report passages label it 2.95% — a marginal inconsistency.

      - The new solution RIY at low growth in the main recommended-switch table is 2.1% (report and [P2]/[P17]), but a separate comparison scenario in the switch report ([P1]) shows new solution RIY of 0.6% at low growth — different scenarios but both present in the file without clear differentiation to the reader.

      - EA5 (Standard Life) impact is stated as 0.08% saving in the suitability report narrative (page 8) but shown as +0.26% (cost increase) in the charges comparison table (page 11) — a direct contradiction within the report itself, corroborated by the derived figures showing the EA5 rows are computed against Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002).

      - The derived figures confirm that the EA5 existing annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439, which is Zurich's current value, not Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the Standard Life cost comparison rows are computed on the wrong plan value and are arithmetically unreliable.

      - The new Aviva illustration ([P13]) shows an all-charges RIY of 2.1% at a 2.9% real growth rate, whereas the report's critical yield table quotes new solution RIY of 2.2% at mid (2.95%) — a minor difference that may reflect different growth rate bases or illustration dates but is not reconciled in the report.

    The core critical yield table as presented in the suitability report is directly and fully corroborated by passage [P17] (the report's own appendix table) and by passage [P2] (the pension switch research report). All RIY figures, critical yield percentages and effect-of-switching amounts and percentages across all three growth scenarios match exactly. The passage [P16] corroborates the 4.8% critical yield narrative and the adviser's assertion that it is achievable. The marginal difference in the mid-growth rate label (2.94% in narrative vs 2.95% in table and switch report) is a low-severity formatting inconsistency that does not affect the substance of the comparison. The separate switch report scenario in [P1] showing lower RIY figures relates to a different comparison (retaining different combinations of existing plans) and does not contradict the recommended-switch table — this is explained by the different scenarios being run. However, two material concerns remain. First, the EA5 (Standard Life) cost comparison is arithmetically corrupted: the derived figures confirm that both the existing annual charge row (£186.19 at 0.18%) and the impact row (£268.94 at 0.26%) for Standard Life are computed against Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002). This means the Standard Life switching cost comparison in the charges table is unreliable. This directly underlies the contradiction between the page 8 narrative (0.08% saving) and the page 11 table (+0.26% increase) for Standard Life — one of these must be wrong, and the derived figures indicate the table figure is computed on the wrong base. Second, the Aviva illustration ([P13]) quotes a weighted average OCF of 0.23% (not 0.22% as in the canonical model) and a product+investment charges RIY of 0.4% with an all-charges RIY of 2.1% at 2.9% real growth, which is broadly consistent with the report's figures but not perfectly reconciled. The Standard Life cost comparison error is a genuine finding: the report simultaneously asserts a cost saving (page 8) and a cost increase (page 11) for the same plan, and the arithmetic basis of the table is demonstrably applied to the wrong fund value. This is a material inconsistency in the costs and charges disclosure that directly affects whether the switching rationale for Standard Life is accurately presented to the client. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this finding beyond the general importance of clear cost disclosure.

  [G7.9] The service proposition attached to the ongoing adviser charge

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report states (page 17) that the ongoing service includes: (1) reviewing attitude to risk and capacity for loss; (2) reviewing the ongoing suitability of the investment portfolio; (3) market updates and updates with changes in financial legislation; (4) access to a professional, experienced and qualified team of financial advisers for ad-hoc advice; (5) access to full holistic financial planning. The cost of this service is stated as 1% per annum, equating to approximately £1,101.85 for the first year based on current values and expected contributions minus the initial fee, to be paid via the Aviva Platform pension scheme. The initial advice fee is stated as 3% (£3,305.55).

    File:     The client agreement (TFAS Wealth Client Agreement) confirms an ongoing annual review management charge of 1% subject to a minimum fee of £100, automatically taken from funds invested, with the amount fluctuating with investment value. The 'Important Information' document (IMPORT_2.md) describes the ongoing service as including: a review meeting at least once a year with recommendations (bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and ATR); statements and performance reports twice per year; cash flow analysis when relevant; family benefit (adviser fee-free advice for direct family members for ISA or pension set-up plus up to three hours advice for children); regular market updates; quarterly e-magazine; online access for portfolio valuations; and access to the adviser as needed. The client agreement and the service description document both confirm the 1% ongoing charge. The client agreement was signed by the client on 31/10/2025.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report's service description omits several elements listed in the client agreement/service document: annual review meeting with specific deliverables (bed and ISA, fund switches, drawdown withdrawals, portfolio rebalance, updated fact find and ATR), statements and performance reports twice per year, cash flow analysis, family benefit, quarterly e-magazine, and online portfolio valuations. These are present in the file but not in the report.

      - The suitability report does not specify a review frequency (extracted as 'Unspecified'), whereas the client agreement and service document state reviews occur 'at least annually' and include 'a review meeting at least once a year'.

      - The suitability report states the ongoing fee will be paid 'via the Aviva Platform pension scheme'; the client agreement states fees are automatically taken from funds invested but does not specifically confirm platform-level deduction via Aviva — however this is consistent rather than contradictory.

      - Transaction costs monetary figure is absent from both the report (extraction confirms NotPresentInDocument) and the retrieved passages, meaning the all-in cost picture is incomplete.

      - The report's ongoing service items are all flagged as 'isClientSpecific: false', meaning none of the service items are tailored to this client's specific circumstances, though the charge amount (£1,101.85) is client-specific.

    The core consistency check passes: the report asserts a 1% ongoing charge and the client agreement (P1, P8) corroborates exactly that rate, including the minimum fee of £100. The service items listed in the report (risk review, suitability review, market updates, ad-hoc adviser access, holistic financial planning) are a subset of what the client agreement and service document describe, not contradictions of them. The omissions from the report (review meeting frequency, statements twice yearly, cashflow analysis, family benefit, e-magazine, online access) are not contradictions but represent a less complete picture than the supporting documents. The absence of a stated review frequency in the report is a disclosure gap — the client is not told how often they will be reviewed, even though the client agreement specifies at least annually. This is a moderate concern because the client cannot easily assess value for money without knowing the review cadence. The service items listed are generic (isClientSpecific: false), which reduces their value as a client-specific justification for the 1% charge, though they are not inconsistent with the file. The charge arithmetic is consistent: 1% on the £110,185 transfer value produces approximately £1,101.85, which matches the report's stated first-year figure. The initial fee of 3% (£3,305.55) is consistent with the client agreement's terms. Transaction costs are absent from both the report and the file, meaning the all-in cost figure is incomplete, but this is an omission rather than a contradiction. The Standard Life charge comparison inconsistency (page 8 states 0.08% saving; page 11 table shows +0.26% increase) noted in the internal inconsistencies is directly relevant to CHK-007 and constitutes a material contradiction within the report itself regarding whether the recommendation is cheaper or more expensive for that plan. No vulnerability characteristics have been identified in the evidence that would alter this assessment. Overall: the 1% rate is corroborated, the service items are partially corroborated, but the absence of review frequency in the report and the Standard Life charge direction contradiction are moderate concerns.

  [G7.10] Benefits relied upon to justify a higher cost

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The report asserts the following benefits to justify the higher cost: (1) Lower Costs — 'The receiving scheme has a lower overall cost structure compared to your existing pension'; (2) Greater Investment Choice — access to a wider range of investment options for a more tailored strategy; (3) Adviser Charging Facilitation — charges deducted directly; (4) Ongoing Monitoring and Adjustments — adviser can actively monitor and adjust; (5) Enhanced Retirement Flexibility — more flexible retirement options including tax-efficient withdrawal strategies. The report also states Aviva is rated A (Stable) by S&P with £407bn AUM and over 6,900 funds. On cost, the report states: on a like-for-like basis the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive (£264 underperformance tolerance), but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one. Critical yield at mid-growth rate of 2.94% requires 4.8% per year growth to age 75, which the adviser believes is achievable based on past fund performance. For the Standard Life plan specifically, the report states it is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8), implying a saving on that transfer.

    File:     The case file evidences the following: (1) The charges comparison table (captured in the canonical model's internal inconsistencies) shows Standard Life EA5 impact as +0.26% — i.e. the recommended plan is MORE expensive for the Standard Life transfer, directly contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving. The derived figures confirm EA5's annual charge is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the EA5 row arithmetically unreliable. (2) The SIPP research report [P10] shows MiFID II pre-sale cost disclosures for the two recommended funds: HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C Acc — ongoing 0.18%, transaction 0.01%, total 0.19%; CT Universal MAP Balanced C — ongoing 0.29%, transaction 0.26%, total 0.55%; aggregate total £406.74 (0.37%). Transaction costs are present in the research report but the suitability report's transaction costs monetary field is absent (extraction flag). (3) The Aviva Key Features [P4, P8] confirm flexi-access drawdown, UFPLS, annuity and phased drawdown options are available on the new plan. (4) The existing arrangements table [P19] shows Scottish Widows offers only 'Full and partial UFPLS' and People's Pension offers 'FAD, Full and partial UFPLS' — meaning FAD is already available on People's Pension (retained) and was available on Aviva (transferred). Zurich offers only UFPLS (no FAD), Standard Life offers only FAD. (5) Policy research [P5, P7] confirms Zurich has no FAD, Standard Life has FAD but not UFPLS; the new Aviva plan has both. (6) The Zurich policy info [P9] confirms no guaranteed annuity rates are flagged. (7) The SIPP research report [P3, P12] confirms the Aviva Pension Portfolio — Choice was the recommended product selected from 118 products. (8) The mid-growth rate is described as 2.95% in the critical yield table (page 18) but 2.94% in the page 11 narrative — a minor inconsistency in the report itself. (9) The like-for-like saving of 0.24%/£264 versus 4.24%/£4,672 more expensive including adviser fees is stated in the report; the £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,188.68 which is broadly consistent with the £110,185 transfer value. (10) No switching analysis or separate cost comparison document is present in the retrieved passages corroborating the charges table figures independently.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states (page 8) the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying a saving by switching). The charges comparison table (page 11, per the canonical internal inconsistencies) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — meaning the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life, directly contradicting the page 8 saving claim.

      - The derived figures confirm EA5's existing annual charge (£186.19 at 0.18%) and EA5 impact (£268.94 at 0.26%) are both computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's actual fund value (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison row arithmetically unreliable and unsupported.

      - The 'Lower Costs' benefit is stated as a general benefit for the Aviva recommendation, yet on the Standard Life transfer the report's own table shows cost increases (+0.26%), undermining the lower-costs justification for that specific transfer. The report does not separately address this.

      - The report states a mid-growth rate of 2.94% (page 11) but the critical yield table (page 18) labels it as 2.95% — a minor internal inconsistency in the justification figures used.

      - Transaction costs are disclosed in the SIPP research report [P10] (CT Universal MAP: 0.26% transaction costs, aggregate transaction costs £147.80/0.13%) but the suitability report's transaction costs monetary figure is absent per the extraction flag, meaning the all-in cost disclosed to the client may not include transaction costs.

      - The 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' benefit claims the new scheme provides 'more flexible retirement options'. The existing Aviva plan (being transferred) already offered FAD and UFPLS per the existing arrangements table, so this benefit does not represent an improvement over that specific ceding plan. However, Zurich offered only UFPLS (no FAD) and Standard Life offered only FAD (no UFPLS), so flexibility is genuinely improved for those two transfers.

      - The 'Lower Costs' benefit as a general statement is contradicted for Standard Life specifically, and the report makes no distinction between plans where costs are lower and plans where costs are higher when presenting the benefits section.

    The central concern in this check is whether the benefits stated to justify a higher cost are supported by and consistent with the evidence. The report acknowledges the plan is 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees and provides a critical yield justification. Several of the benefits have evidential support: Greater Investment Choice is supported by the Aviva Key Features and SIPP research confirming access to 6,900+ funds; Adviser Charging Facilitation is a mechanical feature confirmed by the product literature; Ongoing Monitoring is a service proposition benefit; Enhanced Retirement Flexibility is partially supported — Zurich had no FAD and Standard Life had no UFPLS, so the new plan does improve flexibility for those transfers, though the guard is engaged for the Aviva-to-Aviva transfer where FAD was already available. The most material issue is the 'Lower Costs' benefit and the Standard Life cost comparison. The report states on page 8 that the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', asserting a cost saving. The charges table on page 11 shows +0.26% — meaning the new plan is more expensive for Standard Life. This is a direct contradiction between the report's own narrative and its own table. The derived figures make this worse: the EA5 charge row is calculated on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), so the monetary figures in that row are arithmetically wrong by roughly a factor of 34. This means the cost comparison for Standard Life is doubly flawed — the direction is wrong in the narrative, and the monetary calculation is performed on the wrong fund. This is a material discrepancy: the 'Lower Costs' benefit cannot be supported for the Standard Life transfer, and no separate justification for a cost increase on that transfer is provided. The transaction costs omission from the suitability report is a moderate concern: the research report shows CT Universal MAP has transaction costs of 0.26% — a non-trivial amount — which appear not to be included in the all-in cost figure presented to the client. The minor 2.94%/2.95% growth rate inconsistency is low severity. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction flags the assessmentOutcome for both clients as absent. No specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the retrieved passages that would materially alter this assessment, so no overlay adjustment is made. On balance, the Standard Life cost contradiction (direction of saving vs. actual increase, compounded by the wrong fund value being used in the calculation) is a genuine material discrepancy that is not explained by any false-positive guard. The 'Lower Costs' benefit stated as a general justification for the recommendation is demonstrably incorrect for at least one of the three transfers, and the report provides no remediation of this. Severity is High because the report claims a cost saving that its own comparison table contradicts, per the severity guidance.
