  [G7.11] Higher charges producing lower projected outcomes or an increased yield requirement

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts (page 11) that on a like-for-like basis the Aviva plan is 0.24% LESS expensive than the current plans (i.e. recommended plan is cheaper). However, including adviser fees, the Aviva plan is 4.24% MORE expensive than current plans, requiring the new solution to outperform by £4,672 in the first year. The critical yield section (page 18) states: low growth (0.00%) — existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%; mid growth (2.95%) — existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%; high growth (5.88%) — existing RIY 0.3%, new solution RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%. Projected values at age 75: low £97,600, mid £115,000, high £135,000. The report states (page 11, narrative) that based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94% the plan must grow at 4.8% to match existing plans, and that this is considered achievable based on past fund performance. The Standard Life rationale (page 8) states that plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08% for Standard Life). The charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as '+0.26%' (an increase).

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto, [P10]) confirms all three growth scenarios and their figures exactly: low (0.00%) — existing projected value £108,000, new solution £97,600, existing RIY 0.3%, new RIY 2.1%, critical yield 1.9%, effect £10,400 / 9.6% decrease; mid (2.95%) — existing £127,000, new £115,000, existing RIY 0.3%, new RIY 2.2%, critical yield 4.8%, effect £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; high (5.88%) — existing £150,000, new £135,000, existing RIY 0.3%, new RIY 2.3%, critical yield 7.8%, effect £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. The report narrative in [P16] also states a mid-growth rate of 2.94% and a critical yield of 4.8%. The Defaqto switch report also contains per-plan alternative comparisons: for Zurich-only ([P5]) RIY new solution 0.6% at low growth, critical yield 0.3%; for Aviva-only ([P3]) and Standard Life-only, RIY figures differ materially from the combined analysis (new solution RIY 0.6% in alternative comparisons vs 2.1–2.3% in the combined analysis). The switch report ([P3]) for the combined analysis at mid growth shows existing projected value £127,000 and new solution £125,000 in one section, but [P10] shows £127,000 vs £115,000 — these appear to relate to different sub-analyses (per-plan vs combined). The derived figures flag that EA5 (Standard Life, current value £3,002) charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439), making the Standard Life charge comparison unreliable. The switch report shows the new solution RIY is 2.1–2.3% across all growth rates, materially higher than the existing pension RIY of 0.3%, meaning the recommended solution has significantly higher charges including adviser fees, producing lower projected outcomes across all growth scenarios.

    Discrepancies:

      - The suitability report states the mid-growth rate as 2.94% (page 11 narrative, [P16]) but the critical yield table labels it as 2.95% (page 18, canonical model). The switch report [P10] uses 2.95%.

      - The suitability report's per-plan rationale for Standard Life (page 8) states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (page 11, identified in the internalInconsistencies) shows the impact for Standard Life as '+0.26%' — an increase, not a saving. These are directly contradictory.

      - The derived figures confirm that the EA5 (Standard Life, £3,002) charge comparison rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,439), not Standard Life's own value of £3,002. This means any cost-saving claim for the Standard Life switch rests on arithmetic using the wrong fund value, making the comparison unreliable and potentially misleading.

      - The suitability report presents a like-for-like comparison showing the recommended plan is 0.24% cheaper, but the corroborating passages do not confirm this figure — the nearest value in the switch report is 0.29% (Zurich retained scenario, [P5]), and the -0.24% figure is unmatched in any passage.

      - The 4.24% more expensive (including adviser fees) figure is not directly corroborated by any passage — the nearest is 4% in [P10] (5.7% away), though £4,672 and 4.24% as figures appear in the canonical model extracted from the report itself ([P16] confirms £4,672 in narrative).

      - The new solution RIY is 2.1–2.3% across growth scenarios vs existing 0.3% — the report discloses this but the like-for-like claim of 0.24% cheaper is inconsistent with the materially higher RIY on the new solution, which is not reconciled in the report.

      - The switch report at [P3] (appearing to be the Aviva-only or combined comparison) shows mid-growth new solution projected value of £125,000, whereas [P10] (the combined overall analysis) shows £115,000 at mid growth for the new solution. These differ by £10,000 and relate to different sub-analyses, but the suitability report relies on £115,000 without clearly distinguishing which comparison is operative.

    The suitability report discloses that the recommended solution is more expensive than the existing arrangements once adviser fees are included (4.24% more expensive, critical yield 4.8% at mid growth). It presents this transparently and provides a justification (past fund performance considered sufficient to achieve the 4.8% critical yield). The critical yield figures in the report are fully corroborated by passage [P10] from the Defaqto pension switch report, which confirms all three growth scenario projections, RIY figures and critical yields precisely. The projected values at age 75 are all lower for the new solution than the existing pension across every growth scenario, confirming the report correctly characterises the position (higher charges, lower projected outcomes, with adviser fees being the driver). This is the core of requirement G7.11: the report does disclose higher charges producing lower projected outcomes and does state a critical yield. However, several concerns remain. First, the Standard Life cost-saving claim (page 8: '0.08% more expensive than recommended') is directly contradicted by the charges table (page 11: '+0.26%' impact), as identified in the internalInconsistencies. The derived figures further confirm that this Standard Life row was computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own £3,002 — meaning the charge comparison for Standard Life is arithmetically unreliable. This is a material finding: a cost-saving claim is made for one of the three switching plans that is contradicted by the report's own table and computed on the wrong base value. Second, the like-for-like 0.24% saving figure is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack. The nearest comparable figure (0.29% for a Zurich-retained scenario in [P5]) is from a different sub-analysis. Third, the mid-growth rate label discrepancy (2.94% in the narrative vs 2.95% in the table and switch report) is a minor inconsistency but low severity. The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the passages that would materially affect this charge disclosure assessment. The guard on required outperformance does not apply here — at 4.24%/4.8% critical yield this is well above the 0.5% noise threshold and is a genuine concern requiring justification. The justification offered (past performance) is present but the underlying arithmetic for Standard Life is unreliable. Overall: the report does disclose the higher charge impact and critical yield (no issue on that disclosure), but the Standard Life cost-saving claim contradicted by the report's own table and computed on the wrong fund value is a material concern that cannot be explained by any guard.

CHK-008 — Disadvantages, Risks and Customer Understanding

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 8 requirement(s). 8 raised a concern (G8.1, G8.2, G8.3, G8.4, G8.5, G8.6, G8.7, G8.8). The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative), implying switching saves 0.08% for Standard Life. However, the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — an increase, not a saving. These figures are contradictory. The EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the canonical model are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439.24) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), meaning the Standard Life charge comparison figures are calculated on the wrong plan and are arithmetically unreliable. The report states mid growth rate as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative but 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 — a minor inconsistency in the same document. 9 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G8.1] Material disadvantages, risk factors and limitations of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report discloses multiple material disadvantages and limitations: (1) including adviser fees, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one; (2) at mid growth rate of 2.94%, the plan must grow at 4.8% p.a. to age 75 to match current plan value; (3) switching effect at low growth = £10,400 / 9.6% decrease; at mid growth = £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; at high growth = £15,000 / 10.0% decrease in projected value at age 75; (4) loss of employer contributions if People's Pension transferred; (5) recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed Zurich funds over five years; (6) Zurich funds allocated to higher-risk funds more susceptible to market volatility; (7) market movement risk during transfer pending period; (8) no guarantee new investment will outperform existing; (9) liquidity delay of 10-15 working days; (10) Standard Life plan 0.08% more expensive than recommended solution (per recommendation rationale); (11) tax on encashment of Scottish Widows small pot (25% TFC, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net £170); (12) alternative: if left to grow, full amount could be taken within personal allowance tax-free; (13) transaction costs excluded from charges; (14) charge figure disclaimer — monetary terms may differ from illustration, annual recurring charges assume no growth; (15) Standard Life performance data unavailable; (16) cashflow assumes all surplus income spent. On a like-for-like basis (excluding adviser fee), the Aviva plan is stated as 0.24% less expensive / £264 underperformance tolerance.

    File:     The Defaqto pension switching report (P1/P2) provides switching effect figures for the three constituent plans. For the Aviva plan alone: at low growth, effect of switching is £10 or 0.3% decrease; at high growth, £10 or 0.2% decrease. The Zurich Policy Info (P39/P40/P42) flags that some plans have guaranteed annuity rates, exit charges, and that life cover/waiver benefits are no longer available on new plans — the transfer checklist prompts consideration of features that may be lost. P42 explicitly confirms for the Zurich plan: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' The Aviva Key Features (P4/P7) disclose standard pension risks: value can go down as well as up, client could get back less than invested, income drawdown risks including depletion of fund if high income taken. Aviva T&Cs (P9) confirm switching may expose client to adverse market movements during disinvestment period. The policy research (P10/P23/P27) shows transfer values and plan features. The fact find (P35/P36) shows client objective is to consolidate pensions and retire in approximately 2 years with £1,700 net/month household income. The internal inconsistencies noted in the extraction indicate: (a) the Standard Life charges table (page 11) shows +0.26% impact (an increase) while page 8 narrative says the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — these are contradictory; (b) the EA5 charge rows in the canonical model are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life comparison unreliable; (c) the critical yield table labels mid growth rate as 2.95% while page 11 narrative says 2.94%.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative), implying switching saves 0.08% for Standard Life. However, the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — an increase, not a saving. These figures are contradictory.

      - The EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the canonical model are computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439.24) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), meaning the Standard Life charge comparison figures are calculated on the wrong plan and are arithmetically unreliable.

      - The report states mid growth rate as 2.94% in the page 11 narrative but 2.95% in the critical yield table on page 18 — a minor inconsistency in the same document.

      - The switching effect figures in the report (£10,400/9.6% low; £12,000/9.4% mid; £15,000/10.0% high) relate to the combined three-plan switch. The Defaqto report (P1/P2) provides per-plan switching effects (e.g. Aviva alone: £10/0.3% decrease at low growth) but the aggregate figures are not directly verifiable from the passages retrieved — the report presents combined figures without clearly reconciling them against the per-plan research tool outputs.

      - The Zurich transfer checklist (P39/P40) raises the question of features that may be lost on transfer (life cover, waiver, guaranteed annuity rates). P42 confirms these do not apply to the Zurich plan. However, the extraction notes that featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent from the report for all five existing arrangements (EA1–EA5) — the report does not contain a section explicitly addressing features or guarantees lost for each plan being switched, even though the Zurich transfer documentation prompts this consideration.

      - The report presents both a like-for-like cost saving (0.24% less expensive / £264) and an including-adviser-fee cost increase (4.24% more expensive / £4,672) without clearly reconciling them for the reader, creating a risk that the materially adverse adviser-fee impact is not sufficiently prominent relative to the headline cost saving.

      - The derived figures show the required outperformance of £4,672 at 4.24% implies a fund value of £110,188.68, which does not match any stated arrangement value or sum — a minor arithmetic inconsistency in the report's own figures, though immaterial in magnitude.

      - The loss scenarios (£10,400/9.6%; £12,000/9.4%; £15,000/10.0%) imply fund values (£108,333; £127,659; £150,000 respectively) that match no stated arrangement value — the base figures used for these projections are not fully transparent in the report.

    The report contains a substantial and largely client-specific risk and disadvantage disclosure. Multiple quantified loss scenarios at three growth rates are presented, the cost disadvantage including adviser fees is clearly stated with a monetary figure, the critical yield requirement is given, performance shortfall versus Zurich is acknowledged, tax consequences of the Scottish Widows encashment are explained with a net receipt estimate, and the People's Pension employer contribution risk is identified. These are material disclosures that meet a significant part of the requirement.

   

    However, several concerns remain after applying the false-positive guards:

   

    1. STANDARD LIFE CHARGE CONTRADICTION: The report simultaneously states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8, implying switching saves cost) and the charges table shows +0.26% impact for Standard Life (implying switching costs more). The derived figures confirm the EA5 rows are computed on Zurich's fund value, not Standard Life's £3,002. This means the cost justification for the Standard Life switch — one of three plans being consolidated — is internally contradicted and arithmetically based on the wrong fund. This is a material accuracy failure for that specific recommendation limb, directly relevant to the disadvantages disclosure.

   

    2. FEATURES/GUARANTEES LOST: The extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is absent (NotPresentInDocument) for all five existing arrangements. The Zurich transfer documentation (P39/P40) explicitly prompts consideration of whether features such as guaranteed annuity rates, life cover, and waiver benefits are present and would be lost. P42 confirms these do not apply to the Zurich plan specifically. However, the report does not contain an explicit features-lost analysis for any plan. The guard notes that disadvantages may be spread across sections — the report does address lifestyling funds being left behind (for Standard Life), employer contributions (for People's Pension), and higher-risk fund exposure (for Zurich) — so this is partially mitigated. But a structured features-lost assessment is absent from the report in the way the Zurich provider documentation contemplates.

   

    3. PROMINENCE OF ADVERSE COST IMPACT: The like-for-like saving (0.24%/£264) and the adviser-fee-inclusive cost increase (4.24%/£4,672) are both stated but the difference between £264 and £4,672 is stark. The adviser-fee-inclusive figure is disclosed in the disadvantages section, which is appropriate, but the presentation of both figures without clear reconciliation carries a risk of consumer confusion. This is a moderate presentation concern rather than an omission.

   

    4. VULNERABILITY OVERLAY: The fact find (P35) records the client as a private tenant, aged approximately 69 (date of birth context from P42 suggests mid-50s but the cashflow references ages 69/65 for male/female life expectancy — P26). The client has a monthly deficit of £288 (P32: expenditure £1,718 vs income £1,430) and is expecting to retire in approximately 2 years. This financial fragility makes the accuracy of the cost comparison for the Standard Life switch particularly material — a client in this position needs reliable cost information to make an informed decision about each consolidation. The contradiction in the Standard Life charges is therefore of heightened concern given the client's circumstances.

   

    5. The minor growth rate inconsistency (2.94% vs 2.95%) and the base-fund-value transparency issues in the loss scenarios are low-severity presentation matters.

   

    Overall: the report makes a genuine and substantial attempt at disadvantage disclosure, but the Standard Life charge contradiction (0.08% saving stated vs +0.26% increase shown in the table, computed on the wrong fund value) is a material accuracy failure in the disadvantages section for that recommendation. Combined with the absence of an explicit features-lost analysis and the prominence concern around the adviser-fee cost impact, the outcome is Potential Concern at Moderate severity — the Standard Life issue alone would justify this; the other matters reinforce it.

  [G8.2] Product-specific and investment-specific risks explained

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the following product-specific and investment-specific risks: (1) Liquidity delay of 10–15 working days for access to funds (LiquidityDelay, page 8); (2) Cancellation notice — previous provider not obliged to take the plan back (CancellationNoReinstatementRight, page 16); (3) Cancellation — if investment falls in value before cancellation notice received, amount equal to fall deducted (CancellationValueDeduction, page 16); (4) IHT legislation change from 6 April 2027 — unused DC pensions included in taxable estate (IHTLegislationChange, page 3); (5) Lifestyle risk drift — existing funds in lifestyling will eventually move outside agreed risk range (LifestyleRiskDrift, page 7); (6) Performance comparison — recommended Aviva advised funds stronger than existing Aviva holdings but have not outperformed existing Zurich funds (PerformanceComparison, page 13); (7) Cashflow projection uncertainty — small variance in assumptions can cause marked difference in outcomes (CashflowProjectionUncertainty, page 15); (8) Long-term care costs not modelled in cashflow (LongTermCareNotModelled, page 15); (9) Market risk — unit prices and pension fund value can fall as well as rise, no guarantee of return of original capital (MarketRisk, page 12). No explicit mention of counterparty risk, credit/default risk on underlying bonds, derivatives risk, currency/FX risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk at fund level, leverage risk, or income drawdown-specific risks (means-tested benefits impact, depletion of fund from high income withdrawals, less money for annuity/dependants). No mention of property fund dealing delays, SDRT/dilution levy, or the risk that the recommended funds are rated risk level 3–4 by the fund managers themselves (HSBC GSB Portfolio rated 3; CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund is rated 4 per KIID), while the client's agreed ATR is 5.

    File:     The product literature on file (Aviva Key Features, KIIDs for HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund, Fund Factsheet, Terms and Conditions, Zurich Policy Info) discloses the following material risks not fully replicated in the report: (a) HSBC KIID: counterparty risk, credit risk, default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, liquidity risk at fund level, emerging markets risk (P3, P11); fund rated 3 on risk/reward scale (P1, P5); investment not appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years (P3, P9); (b) CT Universal MAP Balanced KIID: derivative risk (losses can be greater than initial investment), fixed interest securities risk, credit risk, smaller companies risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk, currency risk, leverage risk (P11); fund rated 4 on risk/reward scale (P9); (c) Aviva Key Features: income drawdown risks — impact on means-tested benefits, fund depletion from high income withdrawals, less money for annuity/dependants, tax position affecting income drawdown payments (P4); pension pot value and retirement benefits may go down as well as up, could get back less than invested (P14); property fund dealing delays (P4); (d) Zurich Policy Info: property fund dealing delays up to 12 months, derivatives risk, OEIC cross-liability risk, inflation erosion of fixed-interest income (P17); transfer checklist noting features or benefits that may have been forgotten (P16); (e) Terms and Conditions: dilution levy, SDRT, ETI trading charges (P10, P13); (f) Risk Profile Report: natural ATR questionnaire score was 4 (Cautious Balanced), overridden to agreed level 5 (P18); HSBC fund is risk level 3, CT fund is risk level 4 — both below the agreed ATR of 5, a mismatch not discussed in the report as a product-specific risk.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report discloses a general capital-at-risk/market risk warning but does not disclose counterparty risk, credit/default risk, derivatives risk, currency/FX risk, emerging markets risk, leverage risk, or liquidity risk at fund level — all of which appear in the KIIDs for the two recommended funds (HSBC GSB Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund).

      - The report does not disclose that HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio is rated risk level 3 (out of 5) and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund is rated risk level 4, while the client's agreed ATR is 5; no product-specific discussion of this fund-level risk rating mismatch appears in the report.

      - The report does not address income drawdown-specific risks identified in the Aviva Key Features: impact on means-tested benefits, fund depletion from high income withdrawals, reduced provision for dependants or future annuity purchase, or tax-position-related variation in income drawdown payments.

      - The report does not mention the 5-year minimum holding period recommendation stated in the KIIDs (HSBC and CT funds both state the fund may not be appropriate for investors planning to withdraw within 5 years).

      - The report does not disclose the Zurich property fund dealing delay risk (up to 12 months) or OEIC cross-liability risk from the existing Zurich policy literature.

      - The report does not mention dilution levy, SDRT or ETI trading charges disclosed in the Aviva Terms and Conditions.

      - The performance comparison risk (report states recommended funds have not outperformed existing Zurich funds) is disclosed, but no quantified or illustrated loss scenario flowing from this underperformance relative to the existing Zurich holding is provided in the context of why switching is still recommended.

      - The report's lifestyle risk drift warning relates to the existing funds being switched away from, not to the recommended funds — no equivalent forward-looking risk about the recommended fund's own risk profile trajectory is given.

      - No vulnerability-specific communication adjustment or additional risk explanation is recorded in the report, and no formal vulnerability assessment outcome is present in the canonical model (both /vulnerability/C1/assessmentOutcome and /vulnerability/C2/assessmentOutcome are absent), meaning any heightened need for prominent or simplified risk disclosure cannot be confirmed as addressed.

    The report contains a general capital-at-risk warning, a lifestyle drift risk, a performance comparison note, cashflow projection caveats, IHT legislative risk, cancellation risks and a liquidity access delay. These are meaningful disclosures. However, the KIIDs for both recommended funds (HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio and CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund) disclose a material set of product-specific and investment-specific risks — counterparty risk, credit and default risk, derivatives risk, currency risk, emerging markets risk, liquidity risk at fund level, leverage risk — none of which appear in the suitability report. The guard confirms that a general capital-at-risk warning does not satisfy the requirement to disclose product-specific risks; these must be tested separately. The fund-level risk ratings (HSBC rated 3, CT rated 4) sit below the client's agreed ATR of 5, and the report nowhere discusses this as a product-specific risk or limitation. The Aviva Key Features document identifies income drawdown-specific risks (means-tested benefits, fund depletion, annuity provision) that are absent from the report. The KIIDs also flag that neither fund is appropriate for investors intending to withdraw within 5 years — a material product-specific limitation that is not mentioned in the report. Taken together, the omission of these KIID-identified risks means the report has not fairly and prominently explained all material product-specific and investment-specific risks. The guard against deferring risk disclosure to product literature is engaged: there is no statement in the report that all other risks are in the literature provided, so the risks are simply absent rather than delegated, which is the more serious position. On vulnerability: the canonical model records no formal vulnerability assessment outcome for either client, so it cannot be confirmed that any heightened disclosure needs have been addressed. This adds a further layer of concern but is primarily assessed under CHK-010. Severity: the omission of KIID-identified product-specific risks (counterparty, derivatives, currency, credit, leverage, emerging markets) from a recommendation involving two OEIC funds is a material gap — assessed as Moderate to High. The income drawdown risk omission and the fund risk-rating mismatch with agreed ATR push this toward High.

  [G8.3] Tax consequences of the recommendation

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states the following tax consequences: (1) Pension tax relief: a net contribution of £80 becomes £100 gross. (2) Death benefits before age 75 paid free of tax; after age 75 taxed at beneficiary's marginal rate. (3) From 6 April 2027, most unused DC pensions and death benefits will be included in the taxable estate for IHT; exceptions include spouse/civil partner pensions, dependants' scheme pensions and death-in-service benefits. (4) Scottish Widows small pot encashment: 25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate, estimated net receipt £170 from a £200 fund withdrawal. (5) Alternative for Scottish Widows: if left to grow until retirement, full amount could potentially be taken within personal allowance with no tax due. (6) Flexi-access drawdown: up to 25% tax-free lump sum at crystallisation (subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275); remainder taxed at marginal rate. (7) Annual allowance excess charge of up to 45%; tax relief limited to £3,600 p.a. or 100% of salary. (8) Benefits can be drawn from age 55 (rising to 57 from 6 April 2028) and advice should be taken to draw benefits tax-efficiently. (9) IHT currently £0 for the client; pensions currently outside estate.

    File:     The Key Features document [P2] confirms 25% tax-free lump sum entitlement, income tax payable on income and lump sum payments, and references the lump sum allowance and lump sum and death benefit allowance. [P4] confirms death before 75: benefits normally paid tax free; death after 75: benefits taxed at beneficiaries' marginal rate; lump sum to trust or personal representatives: 45% charge. [P6] confirms FAD 25% tax-free lump sum subject to Lump Sum Allowance of £268,275; remainder taxed at marginal rate; and the replacement of the lifetime allowance with new allowances. [P3] and [P5] confirm IHT liability currently £0 and the April 2027 IHT change for DC pensions. [P10] confirms the small pot encashment tax treatment verbatim: 25% tax-free, 75% at marginal rate, estimated £170 net. The pension switch research report [P23] shows the death benefit shortfall immediately after switch: existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.00, a shortfall of £3,305.00 — this is a material tax/financial consequence of switching (loss of immediate death benefit value equal to the advice charge) that is present in the research file. The suitability report does not explicitly explain that the immediate death benefit will fall by £3,305 on day one due to the initial advice charge being deducted from the fund on transfer. The report's IHT treatment of death benefits pre-/post-75 is consistent with [P4]. The report's Scottish Widows small pot tax treatment is consistent with [P10]. The report's FAD tax disclosure is consistent with [P6].

    Discrepancies:

      - The pension switch research report [P23] discloses a day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £106,880.60) arising from the initial advice charge. The suitability report does not explain this immediate reduction in death benefit value or its tax/financial consequence for beneficiaries, representing a material omission of a consequence identified in the supporting evidence.

      - The report's small pot tax calculation states 25% of a £200 fund = £50 tax-free, 75% = £150 taxed at marginal rate, net receipt estimated at £170. If the client is a basic rate taxpayer (20%), tax on £150 would be £30, giving £170 net — this arithmetic is consistent. However, the report does not state the client's marginal tax rate being applied to confirm the £170 estimate, making this client-specific calculation unverifiable from the report alone (though the guard acknowledges this is unverifiable rather than wrong).

      - The report states the Scottish Widows fund value as £211.32 in the existing arrangements table but refers to it as a '£200 pension fund withdrawal' in the tax consequence explanation — a rounding/approximation that is not explicitly flagged as an approximation.

      - The report describes the IHT change from 6 April 2027 in generic legislative terms without quantifying the potential IHT exposure on the client's pension estate (currently ~£110,185), though the report does confirm current IHT liability is £0.

    The report covers the principal tax consequences of the recommendations competently: pension tax relief, FAD crystallisation tax treatment, death benefit tax treatment pre- and post-75, the IHT legislative change from April 2027, the small pot encashment tax outcome, and the annual allowance. These are corroborated by [P2], [P4], [P6], [P3], [P5] and [P10]. The FAD disclosure in [P6] matches the report's assertion verbatim. The death benefit tax treatment in [P4] is consistent with the report's assertion. The small pot tax treatment in [P10] matches the report exactly. The guard on generic legislative explanation vs client-specific consequence is relevant to the IHT disclosure, which is legislative in nature; however, the report does confirm the client's current IHT liability is £0 and that pensions are currently outside the estate, which provides some personalisation. The more significant gap is the day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 disclosed in [P23]. This is a direct financial consequence of the initial advice charge being extracted from the fund at the point of transfer, reducing the death benefit available to beneficiaries immediately. The suitability report discloses the initial advice fee of £3,305.55 [P7] but does not connect this to its effect on the death benefit value on day one. This is a material disadvantage/risk identified in the supporting evidence that is not explained in the report. The omission is particularly relevant given the client's age (69) and the short window before the April 2027 IHT change. The small pot rounding (£211.32 described as '£200') is a minor presentation issue. The marginal rate gap is noted but the guard correctly identifies this as unverifiable rather than wrong. On balance, the main substantive gap is the death benefit shortfall consequence.

  [G8.4] Loss scenarios illustrated

  Potential Concern — severity Low — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report illustrates five loss scenarios: (1) stress test — immediate market crash of -30% combined with high inflation of 6%, no shortfall projected; (2) stress test — high inflation of 6% sustained, no shortfall projected; (3) low growth rate (0.00%): new solution £97,600 vs existing £108,000, decrease of £10,400 (9.6%); (4) mid growth rate (2.95%): new solution £115,000 vs existing £127,000, decrease of £12,000 (9.4%); (5) high growth rate (5.88%): new solution £135,000 vs existing £150,000, decrease of £15,000 (10.0%). All three critical yield rows appear in the Critical Yield section on page 18 of the report.

    File:     The critical yield table in the suitability report (P3) confirms: low growth (0.00%) existing £108,000 / new £97,600 / effect £10,400 or 9.6% decrease; mid growth (2.95%) existing £127,000 / new £115,000 / effect £12,000 or 9.4% decrease; high growth (5.88%) existing £150,000 / new £135,000 / effect £15,000 or 10.0% decrease. The stress test passages (P22) confirm both the -30% market crash combined with 6% inflation scenario and the 6% inflation-only scenario, each concluding no shortfall. The Aviva illustration (P15) corroborates the new solution projected values: lower 1.9% → £97,500; medium 5% → £115,000; higher 8% → £135,000 (note the illustration uses different growth rate labels from the report's critical yield table). The pension switch report (P25, P26, P27) provides per-plan comparison tables and overall comparison summary. However, the derived figures note that the EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value rather than Standard Life's own £3,002 value, making that comparison unreliable. The internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction also notes that the mid growth rate label is 2.94% in one part of the report (page 11) but 2.95% in the critical yield table (page 18).

    Discrepancies:

      - The report labels the mid growth rate as '2.95%' in the Critical Yield section (page 18), but page 11 of the report refers to 'a mid-growth rate of 2.94%' — a marginal inconsistency between two parts of the same document.

      - The Aviva product illustration (P15) uses growth rate labels of 1.9% (lower), 5% (medium) and 8% (higher) with real-terms values of £97,500, £115,000 and £135,000 — the report's critical yield table uses 0.00%, 2.95% and 5.88% as its growth rates. The projected values for the new solution broadly align (£97,600 vs £97,500 at low; £115,000 matches; £135,000 matches) but the growth rate labels differ materially between the illustration and the critical yield table, meaning the two documents are using different growth rate frameworks.

      - The derived figures confirm that the EA5 (Standard Life) charge impact row of +0.26% is computed on a fund value matching Zurich's £103,438 rather than Standard Life's own £3,002, making the Standard Life-specific loss comparison in the charges table unreliable. The report's page 8 narrative states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive' (a saving) while the charges table on page 11 shows '+0.26%' (an increase) — a direct contradiction already flagged in the extraction.

      - The stress tests produce a 'no shortfall' conclusion rather than quantifying a monetary loss — this is a qualitative outcome, not a monetary loss figure, though it does constitute an illustrated scenario.

    The report contains multiple, substantive loss scenario illustrations: two cashflow stress tests (market crash -30% with 6% inflation; 6% inflation sustained) and three critical yield comparison rows at low, mid and high growth rates showing monetary decreases of £10,400, £12,000 and £15,000 respectively. These are well-supported by the case file. P3 reproduces the critical yield table verbatim, confirming all three monetary loss figures and percentages. P22 confirms both stress test narratives word-for-word. The Aviva illustration (P15) corroborates the projected values for the new solution. The minor discrepancy between the 2.94% and 2.95% mid growth rate labels is a marginal rounding inconsistency within the report itself and does not affect the loss scenario figures materially. The difference in growth rate labelling between the product illustration (1.9%/5%/8% gross) and the critical yield table (0.00%/2.95%/5.88% real/net) reflects the different purposes of the two documents — the illustration uses gross example rates then deducts inflation, while the critical yield table presents post-inflation net rates. The projected values align closely (£97,500 vs £97,600 at low; £115,000 matches exactly; £135,000 matches exactly), confirming consistency of substance despite the different labelling conventions. The false-positive guard — that a stress test in a cashflow model is a loss scenario even if not labelled as one in the risk section — applies here and confirms both stress tests qualify. The EA5 charge table unreliability (derived figures) is a concern for CHK-007 and CHK-009 but does not undermine the five loss scenarios illustrated, which rest on the overall consolidated fund comparison rather than the per-plan Standard Life row. No material loss scenario identifiable in the file is absent from the report. No vulnerability factors are recorded in the evidence that would alter this assessment — the extraction notes vulnerability assessment outcomes were not present in the document.

  [G8.5] Disadvantages and risks are fairly and prominently presented

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report has a dedicated section headed 'Other Considerations of Switching' appearing on page 8 within the main body of the report (not confined to an appendix). Risk warnings include: stock-market movements while transactions are pending; no guarantee the new provider's funds will outperform existing funds; the value of the pension can fall, especially in early years or on income withdrawal; past performance is no guarantee of future returns. A critical yield appendix on page 18 provides projected loss scenarios at three growth rates (low: £10,400 / 9.6% decrease; mid: £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; high: £15,000 / 10.0% decrease). Costs are disclosed including that — on an all-in basis including adviser fees — the new plan requires £4,672 outperformance in the first year and a 4.8% annual growth rate to match the existing plan at the mid-growth rate. The like-for-like charge saving is stated as 0.24% / £264. The Standard Life recommendation narrative states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%). The report notes an immediate shortfall in death benefit of £3,305 following the switch. No features or guarantees lost are described for any of the five existing arrangements. No tax consequences are addressed beyond an implication in the Scottish Widows encashment discussion.

    File:     The pension switch report (category G) confirms the combined critical yield table figures exactly matching the suitability report's appendix: low growth £10,400 / 9.6% decrease; mid £12,000 / 9.4% decrease; high £15,000 / 10.0% decrease. The switch report also confirms an immediate death-benefit shortfall of £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day-one £106,880.60). The switch report contains per-plan critical yield analyses for Zurich-only and Aviva+Zurich+Standard Life combined, showing materially different loss scenarios from those in the suitability report's appendix (e.g. Zurich-only mid-growth effect: £3,000 / 2.4% decrease vs the combined figure of £12,000 / 9.4% used in the report). The charges comparison table in the suitability report shows Standard Life EA5 impact as +0.26%, contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving. Derived figures confirm the EA5 charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life charge comparison unreliable. The switch report shows no information about features or guarantees lost on any existing arrangement. The suitability report extraction confirms featuresOrGuaranteesLost is NotPresentInDocument for all five existing arrangements (EA1–EA5). No tax analysis beyond the Scottish Widows small-pots discussion is evidenced in the file.

    Discrepancies:

      - The charges table on page 11 shows Standard Life (EA5) impact as +0.26% (an increase in cost), but the page 8 recommendation narrative states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a cost saving of 0.08%). These are contradictory.

      - Derived figures confirm the EA5 charges row is computed using Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (~£3,002), so the monetary and percentage impact figures for Standard Life in the charges table are unreliable and misleading.

      - The suitability report's critical yield appendix presents a single combined table for all three switching pensions; the switch report (G) contains separate per-plan tables. The combined presentation may obscure the relatively small loss scenario for the individual Standard Life and Aviva plans.

      - No features, guarantees or protections being given up are described for any of the five existing arrangements (confirmed NotPresentInDocument by extraction for EA1–EA5). The report does not address what, if any, safeguarded benefits or contractual protections attach to any plan being switched.

      - No tax consequences of the pension consolidation (beyond the Scottish Widows small-pots encashment discussion) are addressed in the report, notwithstanding that the initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted from the pension fund, reducing the tax-free cash entitlement at retirement.

      - The report presents a like-for-like charge saving (0.24% / £264) and an all-in cost increase (4.24% / £4,672) without clearly reconciling them for the client; the juxtaposition could mislead a client reading only the like-for-like figure.

      - The mid-growth rate used in the critical yield table is labelled '2.95%' on page 18 but the page 11 narrative refers to '2.94%' — a minor internal inconsistency.

      - The loss scenarios in the critical yield appendix are presented only in the appendix; the body of the report (page 8 'Other Considerations of Switching') does not quantify any monetary loss scenario — it refers the reader to the appendix for critical yield detail.

    The report does have a dedicated risk section ('Other Considerations of Switching', page 8) in the main body and is not confined to the appendix, which satisfies the basic prominence test. Standard investment risk warnings, the no-guarantee statement and the death-benefit shortfall (£3,305) are stated. The critical yield loss scenarios are quantified and consistent with the supporting switch report evidence at the combined level.

   

    However, several material gaps remain:

   

    1. STANDARD LIFE CHARGE DISCREPANCY: The page 8 narrative claims Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a saving). The charges table on page 11 shows +0.26% (an increase). Derived figures confirm the EA5 charges row uses Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (~£3,002) — meaning the charges table figure itself is computed on the wrong plan. This is a material error: the client is told switching Standard Life saves money (page 8 narrative) when the table shows the opposite, and neither figure is reliable. This is not explained in the guard for false positives; it is a genuine internal contradiction in the report.

   

    2. FEATURES/GUARANTEES LOST: No features or guarantees lost are described for any existing arrangement. The extraction confirms this is absent from the document (NotPresentInDocument for EA1–EA5). Where existing pensions may carry any contractual protections, the absence of any disclosure is a material omission under COBS 9.4.7R.

   

    3. TAX CONSEQUENCES: The initial adviser charge of £3,305 is deducted from the pension fund on day one, reducing the capital available to generate tax-free cash at retirement. This tax consequence is not addressed in the report. The Scottish Widows encashment is discussed separately but the broader tax impact of the consolidation is not.

   

    4. LOSS SCENARIO PROMINENCE: The monetary loss scenarios (£10,400–£15,000) appear only in the appendix (page 18). The page 8 risk section refers to the critical yield appendix but does not itself quantify any monetary loss. Benefits (lower costs, greater investment choice, flexibility) are stated prominently in the body of the report, while the quantified downside appears only in the appendix — this is a prominence concern, albeit moderated by the fact that the referral to the appendix is explicit.

   

    5. CHARGE PRESENTATION: The simultaneous presentation of a 0.24%/£264 like-for-like saving and a 4.24%/£4,672 all-in cost increase without clear reconciliation may mislead. The guards note this is not strictly contradictory but the report's failure to clearly explain the relationship is a fair presentation concern.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: the file records John as a risk-4 questionnaire result overridden to risk-5, with the client confirming awareness. No specific vulnerability characteristics are recorded that would heighten the concern beyond what is already identified above, but the complexity of the charge presentation and the absence of clear features-lost disclosure would apply equally to any client.

   

    Overall: the most material concern is the Standard Life charge contradiction (page 8 narrative vs page 11 table, compounded by the wrong fund value used in the table) and the absence of any features/guarantees-lost disclosure. These are High-severity gaps. The tax consequence of the initial charge reducing future tax-free cash is also unaddressed. Severity: High.

  [G8.6] Benefits, features or protections the client is giving up

  Potential Concern — severity High

    Report:   The suitability report asserts for each of the four existing arrangements being replaced (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, and one further plan) that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits', with no safeguarded benefits being given up (hasSafeguardedBenefits: false, isBeingGivenUp: false for all five arrangements). The People's Pension is noted as the current workplace scheme (retained). The Scottish Widows arrangement is noted as 'Retain and Encash'. The report records retirement options available per arrangement: EA1 (FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS); EA2 (UFPLS, PartialUFPLS); EA3 (FlexiAccessDrawdown, UFPLS, PartialUFPLS); EA4 (UFPLS, PartialUFPLS); EA5 (FlexiAccessDrawdown only). The extraction notes that featuresOrGuaranteesLost fields are absent from the report for all five replacement arrangements (NotPresentInDocument).

    File:     Policy research and provider documentation collectively confirm: (1) No GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates on People's Pension, Scottish Widows, Aviva, Zurich, or Standard Life. (2) Standard Life policy letter explicitly states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', 'No Reference Scheme Test', 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' (3) Standard Life policy letter also notes the plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% on the SL At Retirement Universal fund — a charge discount that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' (4) Aviva policy info shows a 0.50% annual fund charge on the existing plan; no exit charges; no life cover; no guaranteed rates. The Aviva policy also notes the plan does not allow adviser charging and cash-only transfers. (5) People's Pension policy info confirms no annuity purchase option available within the plan itself. (6) Scottish Widows policy info confirms no GMP, no guaranteed minimum fund, no reference scheme test, no waiver of premium, no life cover, no fund rebates. (7) Policy research table confirms: FAD available on Aviva (yes) and People's Pension (no unless fund value increases to £10k); UFPLS available on Aviva (yes), Standard Life (no), People's Pension (yes), Zurich (yes); annuity available on Aviva (yes), Standard Life (no), Scottish Widows (yes), People's Pension (only via OMO). (8) The Pension Switch Report (Defaqto) shows the Standard Life existing plan has a reduction in yield of 0.6% and a projected value of £141,000 vs new solution £138,000 — an effect of switching of -£3,000 or -2.1% for Standard Life alone. (9) The new Aviva platform illustration confirms the value of the pension fund can go up or down.

    Discrepancies:

      - The Standard Life policy letter explicitly discloses a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This constitutes a benefit or feature being given up. The suitability report makes no mention of this discount or its potential loss on transfer — the featuresOrGuaranteesLost field is absent (NotPresentInDocument) for the Standard Life arrangement.

      - The policy research table indicates that Standard Life does not offer FAD or full/partial UFPLS, whereas the new Aviva Pension Portfolio does. However, the existing Standard Life plan offers FAD (per the policy research 'yes' column for the existing plan). The report records EA5 (Standard Life) as having only FlexiAccessDrawdown available, while the policy research shows Standard Life existing plan as having FAD. The report's retirement options for EA4 (UFPLS, PartialUFPLS only) and EA5 (FlexiAccessDrawdown only) appear to partially align with the research but the report does not discuss any retirement option features the client is giving up or gaining.

      - The report records no featuresOrGuaranteesLost for any of the five arrangements — this field is absent (NotPresentInDocument) for all five. While safeguarded benefits are confirmed absent, the Standard Life Leaver Discount is a non-safeguarded but material feature that policy evidence identifies as potentially lost on transfer, and the report is silent on it.

      - The report asserts no safeguarded benefits for all five arrangements, which is corroborated by provider documentation. However, the absence of any discussion of non-safeguarded features being given up (such as the Standard Life charge discount) means the report's disclosure of what is being given up is materially incomplete relative to what the file evidences.

    The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits are held across all five arrangements is fully corroborated by provider documentation (Standard Life, Scottish Widows, Aviva, People's Pension all explicitly confirm no GAR, no GMP, no guaranteed growth rates, no safeguarded benefits). On that narrow point, the report is accurate and consistent with the evidence. However, the check requires disclosure of all benefits, features or protections being given up — not only safeguarded benefits. The Standard Life policy letter (P10, P17) is explicit that the plan carries a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a material non-safeguarded feature — a charging benefit — that the client loses on transfer. The suitability report contains no mention of this discount, no explanation of what it means for the client's ongoing charges, and no featuresOrGuaranteesLost entry for Standard Life. This is a material omission: the file evidences a benefit being given up that the report does not disclose. The guard 'A guarantee recorded in provider literature but never mentioned in the report is the finding' applies here, extended to the charge discount which is a material feature of the Standard Life plan. Separately, the Defaqto switch report shows that switching Standard Life alone results in a projected shortfall of -£3,000 (-2.1%) and the report's charges comparison table already shows an anomaly (Standard Life shown as +0.26% impact in the charges table, contradicting the page 8 narrative of a 0.08% saving). The charge discount, if lost, would worsen this position further and its omission is compounded by the existing inconsistency in the charges narrative. No vulnerability overlay factors are identified in the evidence that would specifically aggravate this gap, though the absence of a formal vulnerability assessment outcome (noted as NotPresentInDocument) means any vulnerability-related need for clearer disclosure cannot be confirmed or excluded. The overall finding is a Potential Concern of Moderate-to-High severity: a material product feature (Standard Life charge discount) is evidenced in the file but entirely absent from the suitability report's disclosure of what is being given up.

      · Standard_Life_Policy_Info.md [E] (P17)

        "As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan."

      · Standard_Life_Policy_Info.md [E] (P10)

  [G8.7] Justification for increased costs or reduced projected outcomes

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report states two cost justifications: (1) on a like-for-like basis the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than current plans; (2) including adviser fees, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive, requiring the plan to outperform by £4,672 in the first year. The report justifies this by stating that based on a mid-growth rate of 2.94%, the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year to age 75 to match the existing plan's value, and that 'based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' Projections at three growth rates are provided (low 0.00%: £97,600; mid 2.95%: £115,000; high 5.88%: £135,000). The report also acknowledges recommended funds have not outperformed current Zurich funds over five years. The Standard Life plan is described as '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' as part of rationale for switching it.

    File:     The pension switch research report (G) shows: existing pension projected mid-growth value £127,000 vs new solution £125,000 — an effect of switching of -£2,000 or -1.6% decrease at mid growth. The existing solution maturity values at low/mid/high are £102,000/£127,000/£150,000 respectively. The charges table in the suitability report (I, P19) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving), contradicting the page 8 narrative of '0.08% more expensive than recommended'. The switch report shows day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 due to the initial adviser charge. The SIPP research report (G, P11) shows the new solution ranks 5th out of compared products at mid-growth £125,000, behind the existing solution at £127,000. The existing arrangement projected maturity values from the switch report (P4) show mid-growth £120,000 for the existing solution (all pensions combined basis), and the critical yield table in the switch report uses 2.94% as mid-growth rate label, while the suitability report states 2.95% in projections. EA5 (Standard Life) charges row is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's value (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison unreliable per derived figures.

    Discrepancies:

      - Report states mid-growth rate of 2.94% in cost justification text (page 11) but projections table uses 2.95% — marginal inconsistency between the same document's two references to the mid-growth rate.

      - Report states Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (page 8 narrative) but the charges comparison table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — directionally opposite to the narrative (narrative implies saving, table shows increase).

      - Derived figures confirm EA5 charges row is calculated on Zurich's fund value (£103,439) not Standard Life's (£3,002), making the Standard Life cost comparison arithmetic unreliable — any figure derived from it cannot be trusted.

      - Switch research report projects new solution at mid-growth as £125,000 vs existing solution £127,000 (effect of switching -£2,000), yet the report's own critical yield section shows new solution mid-growth projection of £115,000 — a £10,000 discrepancy between the two documents for the new solution mid-growth projected value.

      - Report projects new solution low-growth value at £97,600 (page 18 critical yield table) but switch report shows existing solution low-growth £102,000 and new solution £100,000 — the report's own low-growth figure of £97,600 is lower than the switch report's new solution figure of £100,000.

      - Cost justification states the plan 'would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75' but the critical yield table (page 18) and switch report show critical yield at mid-growth as 3.2% — not 4.8%. The 4.8% figure appears inconsistent with the documented critical yields.

      - Report does not address the immediate death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (day-one reduction from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 per switch report P17) as a discrete disadvantage or risk in the cost justification section.

      - Report states 'the Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive than your current plans' on like-for-like basis alongside '4.24% more expensive including adviser fees' without reconciling or clearly flagging the £4,408 difference in monetary impact terms (£264 vs £4,672) in a way that makes the net consumer harm clear.

    The report provides cost justifications as required: it states the 4.24% additional cost including adviser fees, quantifies it at £4,672, provides a critical yield justification referencing past performance of the recommended funds, and includes three-scenario projections. On the face of it, the minimum disclosure requirement for justifying increased costs is met. However, several material concerns arise from comparing the report against the case file evidence. First, the Standard Life cost comparison is double-compromised: the narrative says '0.08% more expensive' (implying saving) but the charges table shows +0.26% (an increase), and the derived figures confirm the table row was calculated on Zurich's fund value not Standard Life's — meaning neither figure is reliable and the client receives a materially misleading picture of the Standard Life switch economics. This is a genuine discrepancy not explained by any false-positive guard. Second, the critical yield figure of 4.8% stated in the cost justification text does not match the switch report's critical yield of 3.2% at mid-growth — a material numerical inconsistency in the core justification figure the client is shown. Third, the day-one death benefit shortfall of £3,305 (immediately post-transfer, before any growth) is documented in the switch report but absent from the report's disadvantage or cost justification disclosures — this is a material consumer harm (immediate capital reduction) that the report does not address. The like-for-like vs including-adviser-fee comparison is presented but the difference in monetary outcome (£264 vs £4,672) is not clearly reconciled for the client. The 2.94% vs 2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor and unlikely to be material. The vulnerability overlay: the extraction confirms vulnerability assessment outcomes are not present in the document, and CHK-010 covers this separately; there is no specific vulnerability factor in the available evidence that materially changes the cost disclosure obligation here beyond the general duty of clarity. The Standard Life charges error and the 4.8% critical yield figure inconsistency are the most serious issues — both relate directly to the accuracy of the justification for increased costs and reduced projected outcomes. The death benefit shortfall omission is also material. Overall, while the report attempts justification, the justification contains material inaccuracies and omissions that constitute a Potential Concern at Moderate-to-High severity.

  [G8.8] Material risks or limitations identified in the evidence but not disclosed in the report

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report discloses the following material disadvantages and risks: (1) Including adviser fees, the Aviva plan is 4.24% more expensive than current plans, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one. (2) At mid growth rate of 2.94%, the new plan must grow at 4.8% per year to age 75 to match the existing plan. (3) Loss scenarios at three growth rates: low = £10,400/9.6% decrease; mid = £12,000/9.4% decrease; high = £15,000/10.0% decrease. (4) Retaining People's Pension because transferring would lose employer contributions. (5) Recommended Aviva funds have not outperformed current Zurich funds, which are in higher-risk funds more susceptible to market volatility. (6) Limitations: transaction costs excluded from charge quotes; charge monetary figures may differ from illustration; Standard Life performance data unavailable; cashflow assumes surplus income is spent. (7) Product-specific risks: 10-15 working day liquidity delay; cancellation notice — previous provider not obliged to take plan back; investment fall during cancellation deducted; IHT legislation change from 6 April 2027 on unused DC pensions. The report does not disclose any features or guarantees lost on transfer (featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths all marked NotPresentInDocument).

    File:     The pension switch report (Category G) corroborates the three-growth-rate switching effect figures (£10,400/9.6%, £12,000/9.4%, £15,000/10.0%) and critical yield of 4.8% at mid rate. It also shows a mid growth rate of 2.95% (not 2.94% as stated in the report's narrative). The Standard Life policy info (Category E) discloses that the Standard Life plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.48% which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — a plan-specific benefit the client would lose on transfer. The Zurich policy info (Category E) discloses potential exit charges on capital units reducing by ~0.3%–0.4% per month until SRA, and that Zurich does not currently offer partial UFPLS or phased PCLS without drawdown — features that may not be replicated. The Zurich transfer checklist (Category E) prompts consideration of life cover, waiver benefits, guaranteed annuity rates, and fund choices. The Aviva policy info (Category E) notes the existing plan does not allow adviser charging and that any changes to the plan (such as transferring out) won't affect tax-free lump sum entitlement on any other Aviva plan. The pension switch report shows no benefits listed as lost for any existing product. The SIPP research report and KIIDs (Category H) contain generic market risk, counterparty, credit, default and derivatives risks for the recommended funds. The Aviva Key Features (Category H) discloses income drawdown risks including means-tested benefits impact, fund depletion from high income withdrawals, and tax risks.

    Discrepancies:

      - The Standard Life policy info discloses a Differential Fund Discount / Leaver Discount of 0.4800% that 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer' — this is a material benefit the client loses on switching Standard Life, but the report makes no mention of this discount or its loss anywhere in the risk/disadvantage disclosures.

      - The report states mid growth rate as 2.94% in the narrative; the pension switch report (P10, P2, P3) uses 2.95% — a minor but present numeric discrepancy.

      - The Zurich policy info discloses a potential exit charge on capital units if the fund is transferred, capped at 1% from age 55, reducing by ~0.3–0.4% per month until SRA. The report does not explicitly disclose this potential Zurich exit charge as a risk/disadvantage to the client.

      - The Aviva Key Features document (P11) discloses income drawdown risks (means-tested benefits, fund depletion, tax risks) which are not referenced in the report — though this may be because the recommendation is accumulation rather than drawdown withdrawal at this stage.

      - The pension switch report records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' (P3), yet the Standard Life policy info identifies the Leaver Discount as a benefit specific to the plan. This creates a gap between the switch report and the underlying policy information.

      - The EA5 (Standard Life) charge computation uses Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the Standard Life charges comparison unreliable — a material limitation not disclosed to the client in the report.

      - The internal inconsistency noted in the extraction — Standard Life recommendation on page 8 states '0.08% more expensive' while the charges table on page 11 shows '+0.26%' — means the report gives the client contradictory information about whether switching Standard Life saves or costs money, which affects the client's ability to assess the disadvantage fairly.

    The report provides a reasonably comprehensive risk and disadvantage section, with client-specific switching loss scenarios at three growth rates corroborated by the pension switch report (P10). The critical yield and cost premium figures are present. The employer contribution loss for People's Pension is explicitly stated. IHT legislative risk is flagged. Cancellation risks and liquidity delay are stated. Generic limitations on charges and cashflow assumptions are included.

   

    However, two material items identified in the supporting evidence are absent from the report's risk disclosures:

   

    1. The Standard Life Leaver Discount of 0.4800% (P15): The Standard Life policy information explicitly states the plan benefits from a Differential Fund Discount and that 'These low charges may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a plan-specific benefit the client is giving up by transferring Standard Life. The report never mentions this discount or its loss. Under the 'benefits, features or protections the client is giving up' limb of this check, this is a material omission. The pension switch report recording 'No benefits were listed' does not override the underlying policy document's explicit disclosure — and the guards note that a research finding undercutting the recommendation is material information even if not a formal risk warning.

   

    2. The Standard Life charges comparison inconsistency (page 8 states 0.08% saving; page 11 table shows +0.26% increase, computed on the wrong fund value per the derived figures): The report presents contradictory information to the client on whether switching Standard Life is cheaper or more expensive. This undermines the client's ability to assess the disadvantage fairly, and is not resolved or disclosed as a limitation in the report.

   

    The Zurich potential exit charge on capital units is noted in the Zurich policy information (P25) but the pension switch report's comparison summary (P26/P6) incorporates transfer penalties into the calculation ('The cost of any transfer penalties applied by the existing providers are included in the calculation'), which partially addresses this — though the report itself does not explicitly name the Zurich capital unit exit charge as a risk the client should understand before deciding.

   

    The mid growth rate discrepancy (2.94% report narrative vs 2.95% switch report) is minor and may reflect rounding; it is noted but not itself a material concern.

   

    The income drawdown risks from the Key Features are not relevant at this stage as the recommendation is accumulation.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: the extraction notes vulnerability assessment outcomes are not present in the document. No vulnerability-specific risk disclosures are required that are absent beyond what is already identified.

   

    On balance, the omission of the Standard Life Leaver Discount as a benefit being surrendered on transfer is a material disadvantage present in the product documentation but absent from the suitability report. This meets the Moderate/High severity threshold (a benefit or feature the client is giving up that appears in product literature but is omitted from the report). The charges table inconsistency for Standard Life also means the client is given contradictory signals about cost impact.
