CHK-009 — Replacement or Switch Justification

Outcome: Potential Concern

 

  Potential Concern across 10 requirement(s). 10 raised a concern (G9.1, G9.2, G9.3, G9.4, G9.5, G9.6, G9.7, G9.8, G9.9, G9.10). 1. Standard Life charges contradiction: the report narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table in the report/switching report shows the impact as +0.26% — meaning the new solution is MORE expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. These are directly contradictory figures within the report itself, corroborated by the derived figures confirming the EA5 charge row is computed on Zurich's fund value not Standard Life's. 2. Zurich lifestyling claim: the report states Zurich funds are 'currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement', but the policy research [P35] records the Zurich plan as 'Invested in lifestyle strategy: No' and the fund held is 'Managed AP (accumulation)', which is not a lifestyling fund. 3. Zurich exit charge: the Zurich policy information [P34] confirms that if the plan has capital units, an exit charge applies on transfer. The report does not disclose or address this potential exit charge. 19 cited quote(s) could not be found in the evidence supplied and were rejected.

 

  [G9.1] Comparison of existing versus recommended product and investments

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report recommends switching Aviva (SP50163762), Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL) and Standard Life (D2301334000) into a new Aviva Pension Portfolio on the platform, encashing Scottish Widows (ZU4225575) and retaining People's Pension (6197175/PP). For each switching plan the report provides a comparison across dimensions including fund range, risk alignment, retirement options, adviser servicing, charges and performance. For Scottish Widows (EA2) the report states 29 funds available, no single multi-asset fund aligned to risk 5, does not allow adviser management, and limits retirement options to full and partial UFPLS only. For Aviva (EA3) the report states existing 0.50% fund charge / £18.72 total annual recurring, recommended 0.44% total / 0.06% cheaper; lifestyling fund (risk 2) moving outside agreed risk range; no adviser servicing; 80+ funds vs 6,900+ recommended. For Zurich (EA4) the report states existing 0.93% / £961.98; lifestyling concern; no adviser servicing; full/partial UFPLS only vs FAD on new plan; 60+ funds. For Standard Life (EA5) the report states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'; lifestyling; 300 funds; FAD only. Charges comparison table on page 11 shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving). The report states no safeguarded features exist for any plan. A pension switching report (Defaqto) is referenced showing cost/benefit analysis, critical yield and alternative comparisons. The report acknowledges that including adviser fees the new plan needs to outperform by £4,672 (4.24%) in year one, and at mid-growth rate of 2.94% the recommended plan must grow at 4.8% p.a. to age 75 to match the current plan.

    File:     Provider documentation (Aviva [P18/P40], Scottish Widows [P25/P33/P46], Zurich [P30/P31/P34/P48]) and policy research [P1/P8/P13/P35] confirm: (1) Aviva SP50163762 has no safeguarded benefits, no GAR, no GMP, no exit charge, no adviser charging, auto-switching lifestage approach, 0.50% annual fund charge, retirement options FAD and UFPLS [P18/P40]; (2) Scottish Widows ZU4225575 has no safeguarded benefits, no GAR, no GMP, no exit penalties, does not support ongoing adviser charging or transfer of servicing rights, retirement options full and partial UFPLS only, 29 funds [P25/P33/P46]; (3) Zurich P10026-719-001/DL has no safeguarded benefits, no GAR, no GMP confirmed [P48]; drawdown not available on this plan [P48/P34]; potential exit charge on capital units [P34]; (4) Standard Life D2301334000 allows adviser charging [P8/P23]; retirement options FAD only [P23]. The Defaqto pension switching report [P55/P3/P4/P9/P10/P17/P20] covers all three switching plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) with projected maturity values, reduction in yield, critical yield, effect of switching and alternative comparisons. It confirms initial adviser charge of £3,305 reduces day-one fund from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60 [P12]. Benefits lost section for the overall switch states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' [P17]. The new solution investment details show CT Universal MAP Balanced Fund C Acc and HSBC Global Strategy Balanced Portfolio C, each 50% [P12]. Performance data in the suitability report [P14] shows Aviva My Future Focus Consolidation 5yr 15.58%, Aviva Risk 5 portfolio 5yr 40.02% [P14], and Zurich Managed Pn AP 5yr 44.32% [P6/P32] — the report acknowledges the recommended funds have not outperformed Zurich. Policy research [P35] confirms Zurich is invested in lifestyle strategy 'No' (Managed AP accumulation), contradicting the report's lifestyling claim for Zurich. Charges table in the switching report [P55] and the internal inconsistency noted in extraction shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% while the suitability report narrative states 0.08% saving — these are contradictory. The Defaqto new-solution selection ranking [P9] shows the new solution (rank 5, £125,000) ranks below the existing solution (rank 2, £127,000), Zurich-only (rank 1, £128,000), Aviva-only (rank 3, £126,000) and Standard Life-only (rank 4, £126,000) on mid-growth maturity value. Exit charge on Zurich capital units is confirmed possible [P34] but the report does not address this. People's Pension is a live workplace scheme with active employer contributions (£897.13 p.a.) and is retained [P16]; no employer contribution loss issue arises for the switched plans.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Standard Life charges contradiction: the report narrative (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table in the report/switching report shows the impact as +0.26% — meaning the new solution is MORE expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. These are directly contradictory figures within the report itself, corroborated by the derived figures confirming the EA5 charge row is computed on Zurich's fund value not Standard Life's.

      - 2. Zurich lifestyling claim: the report states Zurich funds are 'currently invested in lifestyling funds, which automatically reduce investment risk as you approach retirement', but the policy research [P35] records the Zurich plan as 'Invested in lifestyle strategy: No' and the fund held is 'Managed AP (accumulation)', which is not a lifestyling fund.

      - 3. Zurich exit charge: the Zurich policy information [P34] confirms that if the plan has capital units, an exit charge applies on transfer. The report does not disclose or address this potential exit charge.

      - 4. New solution maturity value ranking: the Defaqto switching report [P9] ranks the recommended new solution 5th out of 9 options by mid-growth maturity value (£125,000), below the existing solution (£127,000) and several alternatives including Zurich-only and Aviva-only. The report does not explain why a solution ranking below the existing arrangement and several alternatives was selected, beyond non-cost reasons.

      - 5. EA5 (Standard Life) charge row computed on wrong fund value: derived figures confirm that the EA5 ongoing charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of approximately £103,439 — Zurich's value — rather than Standard Life's £3,002. This means the entire cost comparison for Standard Life is unreliable.

      - 6. Scottish Widows fund range: the report states 29 funds available for Scottish Widows; this is consistent with policy research [P23] and provider documentation.

      - 7. Performance chart label swap: the suitability report's two charts on page 13 swap the A/D labels between Aviva and Zurich, creating confusion about which fund's performance is being shown, though the underlying performance figures (15.58% Aviva, 44.32% Zurich) are each corroborated individually in the passages.

      - 8. The report states the recommended funds have 'not outperformed your current Zurich funds' and acknowledges this, but presents this as acceptable on risk/volatility grounds. This is consistent with the evidence but represents a known disadvantage of the switch for Zurich that is addressed only briefly.

      - 9. Critical yield mid-growth rate stated as 2.94% in the report narrative [P29] but the switching report table labels it as 2.95% [P17] — marginal inconsistency.

    The comparison is substantially evidenced. Provider documentation confirms the absence of safeguarded benefits across all five plans, corroborating the report's statements on that point. The Defaqto switching report provides plan-by-plan cost/benefit analysis, critical yield tables, alternative comparisons and a stakeholder comparison, satisfying the structural requirements for a switch analysis. The People's Pension is retained and has active employer contributions; since it is not being switched, no employer contribution loss issue arises for the switched plans.

   

    However, there are three material concerns:

   

    (A) The Standard Life cost claim is internally contradictory and arithmetically unsound. The report narrative states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (a cost saving argument), but the charges comparison table shows +0.26% impact (the new solution is more expensive). The derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed using Zurich's fund value (c.£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own fund value (£3,002), rendering the entire Standard Life cost comparison unreliable. Since cost saving is a stated justification for the Standard Life switch, this is a material finding that goes to the heart of one switching rationale. This satisfies the 'High' severity threshold: a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts.

   

    (B) The report's lifestyling justification for the Zurich switch is contradicted by the policy research, which records the Zurich plan as not invested in a lifestyle strategy. The fund held (Managed AP accumulation) is not a lifestyling fund. This undermines one of the stated rationales for switching the Zurich plan, though the remaining rationales (no FAD, no adviser servicing, no multi-asset risk-5 fund, blended fund approach) are individually supported by evidence.

   

    (C) The potential Zurich exit charge on capital units is disclosed in provider documentation but not addressed in the report. This is a switching cost that should have been disclosed to the client.

   

    The performance label swap between the two charts is a presentation issue rather than a substantive error, as the underlying figures are corroborated. The 2.94%/2.95% mid-growth rate discrepancy is minor. The new solution ranking below alternatives is acknowledged implicitly through the non-cost benefit comparison but not explicitly addressed; however, the switching report does include this comparison so the adviser had sight of it.

   

    Vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability indicators are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment.

   

    Overall: the Standard Life cost contradiction and the Standard Life charge row being computed on the wrong fund value are material; the Zurich lifestyling misstatement and the undisclosed Zurich exit charge are additional concerns. Outcome: Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G9.2] Benefits and costs of switching, including where benefits outweigh higher costs

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the following for the three switching plans: (1) EA3 Aviva — 'Lower Costs' benefit, plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution; charges table shows impact of -0.06% (saving of £2.25); no exit penalty. (2) EA4 Zurich — Lower Costs benefit; charges table shows impact of -0.49% (saving of £506.85); no exit penalty. (3) EA5 Standard Life — recommendation narrative on p8 states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving from switching); charges table on p11 shows impact of +0.26% (an increase of £268.94), meaning the recommended plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life. On aggregate/like-for-like the new Aviva plan is stated as 0.24% less expensive, but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive (requiring £4,672 outperformance). The report claims performance data and a critical yield analysis justify the higher all-in cost. No exit penalties are stated for any plan. Benefits cited include lower costs, lifestyling avoidance, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund alignment. Scottish Widows (EA2) encashment is presented as a separate small-pots exercise with an estimated net receipt of £170 (25% TFC, 75% taxed at marginal rate, on a fund stated as £200 in the quote but £211.32 in the data).

    File:     The pension switch report (Defaqto) [P6] confirms three existing plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) are compared against the new Aviva Pension Portfolio Choice. The combined existing vs new solution comparison at mid growth (2.95%) shows existing projected value £127,000 vs new solution £125,000, an effect of switching of -£2,000 or -1.6%, with a critical yield of 3.2% [P6]. The report including adviser charges shows the new solution requires 4.8% growth p.a. to match the existing plan value at mid growth [P1]. The immediate day-one shortfall after switch is £3,305 (existing £110,185.60 vs new solution day one £110,880.60 — but the table shows £106,880.60) [P7]. Alternative comparisons for each plan individually are provided [P2][P3][P4]. For Standard Life only, at mid growth the new solution projected value is £2,900 vs existing £2,930, effect of switching is a decrease [P2]. The Zurich policy information confirms no transfer value/early retirement charge deduction [P9]. The Standard Life policy info confirms the plan is a SIPP with employer payments in the contribution history [P16]. No explicit corroboration in the evidence for the £170 net receipt figure, the £211.32/£200 fund value breakdown, the 25%/75% tax split calculation, or the per-plan charge differentials of -0.06%, -0.49%, and +0.26% as stated in the report narrative.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Standard Life cost claim contradiction: the report narrative (p8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (p11) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% — meaning the recommended plan is MORE expensive than Standard Life by 0.26%, not cheaper. These are directly contradictory on the most material question for this plan.

      - 2. EA5 charge arithmetic is computed on wrong fund value: the derived figures confirm that the EA5 ongoing charge of £186.19 at 0.18% and EA5 impact of £268.94 at 0.26% both imply a fund value of ~£103,439 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002. The comparison row for Standard Life in the charges table is therefore based on the wrong plan value and is unreliable.

      - 3. Scottish Widows encashment figure inconsistency: the report narrative quotes '£200 pension fund withdrawal' but the canonical data records the fund value as £211.32. The £170 net receipt figure is not corroborated by any passage in the evidence pack.

      - 4. Aggregate combined pension value inconsistency: the report states 'approximately £110,000' on p3 for all five plans, but the arithmetic sum of the five individual values is £116,998.47; the figure £110,185 represents only the three switching plans, not all five.

      - 5. Mid growth rate label inconsistency: p11 narrative states 2.94% mid growth rate; the pension switch report [P6] labels mid growth rate as 2.95%. These differ by 0.01%.

      - 6. The like-for-like aggregate saving of 0.24% (£264) versus the all-in cost increase of 4.24% (£4,672) are both stated in the report without clear reconciliation, creating a materially misleading impression if only one figure is read.

      - 7. No per-plan cost/benefit analysis is evidenced for EA5 (Standard Life) that is computed on Standard Life's own £3,002 fund value — the charge comparison row for EA5 uses Zurich's value throughout.

      - 8. No features-or-guarantees analysis is present in the report for any of the five plans (confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by the extraction metadata for all five paths).

      - 9. Standard Life contribution history [P16] shows employer payments, raising a question whether ongoing employer contributions could be lost on transfer; the report does not address this.

    The central and most serious issue is the Standard Life cost claim. The report narrative asserts on p8 that Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', presenting this as a cost-saving rationale for switching. However, the charges table on p11 (as extracted in the canonical model) shows the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% — meaning the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. This is a direct internal contradiction within the report itself, confirmed by the extraction's own internalInconsistencies record and by the derived figures, which further show that the £268.94/0.26% figures are calculated on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002. This means the Standard Life charges row in the table is entirely unreliable: it uses the wrong denominator, and the narrative figure contradicts the table figure. No guard explains this away — it is not a timing difference, rounding or aggregation issue; it is a fundamental inconsistency that undermines the switching rationale for EA5 (Standard Life). For EA3 (Aviva), the -0.06% saving is directionally consistent between the narrative (p7) and the table (p11), and the derived figure confirms the £3,744 fund value is used correctly. For EA4 (Zurich), the -0.49% saving and £506.85 amount are internally consistent and the derived figure confirms use of the correct ~£103,439 fund value. The aggregate position — 0.24% cheaper like-for-like but 4.24% more expensive including adviser fees — is disclosed, and the report argues that fund performance (critical yield 3.2% at mid growth vs a required 4.8% growth rate) justifies the higher all-in cost. The pension switch report [P6][P12] corroborates the critical yield and effect-of-switching figures used in this argument, including that the new solution projects lower maturity values across all growth scenarios before adviser charges. The report's assertion that performance justifies the higher cost is therefore identifiable as a claim, but the reliance on it is weakened by the Standard Life charge error. The absence of any features-or-guarantees analysis for all five plans (confirmed absent by extraction metadata) is a separate concern under the highest-severity guidance. The Standard Life SIPP payment history [P16] shows employer contribution payments; the report does not address whether active employer contributions would be lost on transfer, which is a highest-severity trigger under the decision logic. The Scottish Widows encashment net receipt of £170 is presented as a benefit of the recommended action but is not corroborated by any passage in the pack. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this finding beyond what is already noted. On balance, the Standard Life charge contradiction (narrative says saving, table says cost increase, and the table is computed on the wrong fund value) is a material concern that is not explained by any guard. The absence of features/guarantees analysis across all plans and the unaddressed employer contribution question for Standard Life compound the finding.

  [G9.3] Details of lost features and guarantees

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The suitability report states, for each of the four arrangements being switched or encashed (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life, Scottish Widows), that 'This pension arrangement does not contain any safeguarded benefits.' All five existing arrangements are recorded as hasSafeguardedBenefits=false and isBeingGivenUp=false. Employer contributions lost is recorded as false for all four replacement analyses. No features or guarantees are described as being lost for any plan.

    File:     Aviva (SP50163762): Policy info states 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension.' No protected tax-free lump sum. No life cover or waiver. Zurich (P10026-719-001/DL): Policy info states 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Standard Life (D2301334000): Policy info states 'No Guaranteed Annuity Rates (GAR's)', 'No Guaranteed Minimum Pension (GMP)', 'No Guaranteed Fund at retirement', 'No Reference Scheme Test', 'As confirmed above there are no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' It also notes a Differential Fund Discount (leaver discount of 0.4800%) which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' Scottish Widows (ZU4225575): Provider confirms 'there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' Policy research table (P3) shows No for GAR and GMP across all five plans. People's Pension (retained): No switch occurring; policy info does not identify any safeguarded benefits. Employer contributions: all plans confirmed as paid-up with no current employer contributions (Scottish Widows: Employer N/A; Aviva: paid up; Standard Life: paid up; Zurich: paid up; People's Pension: no current contributions).

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life policy info discloses a Differential Fund Discount / leaver discount of 0.4800% which 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' The suitability report records featuresOrGuaranteesLost as not present in document and does not mention this discount or its potential loss anywhere in the replacement analysis. This is a feature that may be lost on transfer that the report does not address.

      - The extraction metadata confirms that featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths for EA1 through EA5 are all NotPresentInDocument — the report is silent on any features or guarantees lost beyond the bare statement that no safeguarded benefits exist, with no discussion of the Standard Life leaver discount.

      - costComparison paths for EA1 and EA2 are also NotPresentInDocument per extraction metadata, meaning no cost comparison is present in the report for the Aviva and Zurich plans individually, though a combined comparison table exists.

      - The internal inconsistency already noted in the extraction metadata: Standard Life page 8 states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' while the charges table on page 11 shows '+0.26%' (an increase for the new plan relative to Standard Life), which directly contradicts the cost-saving rationale for the Standard Life switch and is relevant to whether higher costs are justified.

    The report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits exist across all ceding plans is well-corroborated by provider documentation. Aviva explicitly confirms no guaranteed rates and no GMP. Zurich explicitly confirms no guarantees, GMP, safeguarding, protected tax-free cash or market value reduction apply. Standard Life explicitly confirms no GARs, no GMP, no guaranteed fund and 'no Safeguarded benefits attached to this member's plan.' Scottish Widows confirms 'no guarantees, discounts or bonuses.' The policy research table (P3) confirms No for GAR and GMP across all plans. The assertion on safeguarded benefits is therefore well-supported. Employer contributions: all ceding plans are paid-up with no current employer or employee contributions, confirmed by provider documentation, so no employer contributions are at risk of being lost. The guard that an explicit 'no safeguarded benefits' assertion must still be corroborated is satisfied here — corroboration exists from multiple category E documents and one category G document. However, there is one unaddressed concern: the Standard Life policy info explicitly warns that the Differential Fund Discount / leaver discount of 0.4800% 'may not be available from other pension providers upon transfer.' This is a potentially valuable feature that could be lost on transfer. The report's replacement analysis for Standard Life (EA5) contains no mention of this discount or its potential loss — the featuresOrGuaranteesLost path is NotPresentInDocument. This is a feature that falls within the scope of 'safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features that may be lost' under the check's what-to-look-for criteria. The omission is compounded by the internal inconsistency in the Standard Life cost comparison (page 8 claims 0.08% saving; table on page 11 shows +0.26% cost increase), which means the rationale for switching Standard Life rests on a contradicted cost claim, and the potentially valuable discount is not addressed. This meets the threshold for a Potential Concern at moderate severity: a feature that may be lost is disclosed in provider documentation but not addressed in the report, and the cost rationale for that specific plan is internally contradicted. Vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability indicators in the evidence have been identified as bearing on this specific requirement.

  [G9.4] Alternatives to the switch considered

  Potential Concern — severity Moderate

    Report:   The report considers five alternatives: (1) Leave Scottish Widows in place — rejected because no single multi-asset fund aligns with risk rating 5 (combining two funds leaves 20-30% in money market/cash), does not allow adviser management, and limits retirement flexibility. (2) Leave Aviva untouched — rejected because lifestyling funds will automatically reduce risk as retirement approaches, moving portfolio outside agreed risk range. (3) Internal fund switch within Aviva — rejected because the plan does not allow adviser management. (4) Consolidate into People's Pension — rejected because funds do not meet risk profile, plan is 0.06% more expensive than recommended solution, carries £4.50 annual admin charge, no multi-asset funds, and only limited fund range. (5) Consolidate into Scottish Widows (implied) — also considered and rejected. The report does not record a 'leave Zurich in place' or 'internal switch within Zurich' alternative in a distinct section, though the Zurich recommendation section notes no risk-level-5 multi-asset funds are available within Zurich and that a blend of risk-7 and risk-2 funds would be required, and that an internal fund switch is not recommended. The report does not record a 'leave Standard Life in place' alternative in a distinct section, though the Standard Life recommendation section notes lifestyling funds and cost disadvantage. No alternatives-considered section is recorded for Standard Life or Zurich as standalone 'alternative' entries.

    File:     [P1] confirms the Scottish Widows rejection reasoning verbatim: no single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, two-fund blend leaves 20-30% in money market, does not allow adviser management, limits flexible retirement access. [P3] (comparison table) shows People's Pension has 7 funds, no adviser servicing, current workplace scheme; Scottish Widows has 29 funds, no adviser servicing. [P4] confirms the Zurich recommendation section states no risk-level-5 funds, blend of risk-7 and risk-2 required, internal fund switch not recommended, and that the client's main priority is consolidation with full drawdown options. [P2] confirms the Standard Life section states lifestyling funds move portfolio outside agreed risk range and the plan is 0.08% more expensive. The pension switch report [P5/P9] provides individual plan comparisons for Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life against the new solution, supporting the plan-by-plan analysis. [P10] confirms Aviva plan does not allow adviser charging. However, no passage explicitly documents a standalone 'do nothing with Zurich' or 'do nothing with Standard Life' alternative section — those plan-specific reasons are embedded in the recommendation rationale rather than in a separate alternatives section.

    Discrepancies:

      - The report's canonical model does not record a standalone 'leave Zurich in place' alternative entry with explicit rejection reasons, though some rejection reasoning is embedded in the Zurich recommendation narrative in [P4].

      - The report's canonical model does not record a standalone 'leave Standard Life in place' alternative entry; rejection reasoning for Standard Life is embedded in its recommendation narrative in [P2] rather than a dedicated alternatives section.

      - The Standard Life recommendation narrative [P2] states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', but the internal inconsistency flagged in the extraction notes shows the charges comparison table on page 11 records the impact for Standard Life as +0.26% — these figures contradict each other, undermining the cost-based rejection of the Standard Life alternative.

      - The EA5 (Standard Life) charge rows in the derived figures are computed on Zurich's current value (£103,438.89 implied) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002.00), making the charge comparison for Standard Life arithmetically unreliable.

      - The report asserts the People's Pension alternative is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution, but no passage in the evidence pack independently corroborates this specific figure with a supporting calculation; [P3] shows the People's Pension fund range and charges context but no explicit charge percentage comparison.

    The report addresses alternatives at a case-wide level and also embeds plan-specific reasoning within each recommendation section. For Scottish Widows and Aviva, the alternatives analysis is explicit and well-supported by the evidence in [P1], [P3], [P4] and [P10]. The People's Pension alternative is addressed with stated reasons corroborated by [P3]. For Zurich and Standard Life, the 'do nothing' alternative is not recorded as a standalone alternatives entry but the rejection reasoning is present within the recommendation narrative sections ([P4] for Zurich, [P2] for Standard Life) — the SetCoverage guard permits case-wide or embedded reasoning to cover multiple plans, but only where the reasoning is plan-specific and the plans do not differ materially in a way that demands separate treatment. Zurich and Standard Life are both relatively small plans (£3,744 and £3,002 respectively versus Zurich at £103,439) and the reasons given — lifestyling risk for Standard Life and fund-range/diversification issues for Zurich — are plan-specific. This is acceptable under the guard. However, a material concern remains: the Standard Life cost-saving justification ('0.08% more expensive') directly contradicts the charges table figure of '+0.26%' (an increase, not a saving). The derived figures confirm the EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's value rather than Standard Life's own £3,002 value, making the entire cost-based rejection of the Standard Life alternative arithmetically unreliable. This is not a minor omission — the cost advantage is one of the stated rejection reasons for remaining in Standard Life, and it is contradicted by the report's own comparison table. The vulnerability overlay does not appear to raise additional concerns specific to this check based on the evidence provided. Overall, the alternatives analysis is substantively present and plan-specific, but the Standard Life cost comparison contradiction is a genuine unresolved inconsistency that undermines the completeness and reliability of the switching justification for that plan.

  [G9.5] Switching costs exceed the benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts: (1) No exit penalties on any of the three ceding plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). (2) For Aviva (EA3): switching saves 0.06% pa (−£2.25 pa). (3) For Zurich (EA4): switching saves 0.49% pa (−£506.85 pa). (4) For Standard Life (EA5): the report narrative on p8 states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving of 0.08%), yet the charges comparison table on p11 records the impact as +0.26% / +£268.94 pa — meaning the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life. (5) On a like-for-like basis across all three, the new Aviva plan is 0.24% less expensive (saving £264 pa); including adviser fees, the new plan is 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 additional cost pa). (6) Benefits cited: lower costs, avoidance of lifestyling funds, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring, and multi-asset fund selection aligned to agreed risk level 5. (7) Critical yields stated as 1.9% (low), 4.8% (mid), 7.8% (high) — these are corroborated by the switch report. (8) Features/guarantees lost: the report does not address this (confirmed absent by extraction metadata for all five paths). (9) Out-of-market risk disclosed. (10) Scottish Widows (EA2) encashment: net receipt ~£170 under small pots rules (25% TFC, 75% at marginal rate); or full amount potentially tax-free if left to retirement. No exit penalty stated.

    File:     The Defaqto Pension Switch Report (passages P1–P8) shows: (1) No exit penalties recorded for the three ceding plans (consistent with report). (2) For the overall switch, the existing pension day-one value is £110,185.60 and the new solution day-one value is £106,880.60 — an immediate fund value reduction of £3,305.00 due to switching costs/adviser charges. (3) Critical yield figures corroborated: low 1.9% [P7], mid 4.8% [P7], high 7.8% [P7]. (4) The Zurich-only alternative comparison [P1] shows the new solution has a reduction in yield of 0.6% vs the existing 0.3%, i.e. the new solution is MORE expensive on a reduction-in-yield basis for Zurich, with effects of switching being £10,400 (9.6%), £12,000 (9.4%) and £15,000 (10.0%) decreases at low/mid/high growth respectively [P7]. (5) The Standard Life-only comparison [P6] shows new solution reduction in yield of 0.6% vs existing 0.6% — same cost, with a very small negative effect of switching (£10–£30). (6) The Aviva-only comparison [P8] shows new solution reduction in yield of 0.6% vs existing 0.6%, with minimal switching effect. (7) The switch report does not contain any analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features that might be lost. (8) Plan-specific annual charge figures for EA3 (−0.06%, −£2.25), EA4 (−0.49%, −£506.85), EA5 (+0.26%, +£268.94) are present in the report's charges comparison table but not independently corroborated by retrieved passages — the switch report shows charge impacts via reduction-in-yield methodology which directionally contradicts the per-plan savings claims for Zurich and Standard Life. (9) No plan-specific features/guarantees analysis appears in any retrieved passage.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. Standard Life (EA5) cost claim contradiction: the report narrative (p8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table (p11) records the impact as +0.26% / +£268.94 — the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life on a like-for-like switch basis. These are internally contradictory and the +0.26% figure is also computed on the wrong fund value (Zurich's £103,439 rather than Standard Life's £3,002), making the comparison unreliable.

      - 2. EA5 charge arithmetic error: the derived figures confirm that the EA5 impact of +0.26% / £268.94 implies a fund value of ~£103,438 — Zurich's current value — rather than Standard Life's £3,002. The Standard Life cost comparison in the charges table is therefore computed on the wrong plan's value.

      - 3. Zurich reduction-in-yield contradiction: the report claims switching from Zurich saves 0.49% pa. However, the switch report [P7] shows the new solution has a reduction in yield of 2.1–2.3% vs the existing Zurich plan's 0.3%, and the effect of switching is a decrease of £10,400–£15,000 (9.4–10.0%). The direction of the charge impact is contradicted by the switch report's own reduction-in-yield analysis.

      - 4. Immediate capital loss on switching: the switch report [P2/P7] shows an immediate fund value reduction from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60, a day-one loss of £3,305.00. The suitability report does not disclose this immediate capital impact explicitly in the switching costs section.

      - 5. Features/guarantees lost: the report contains no analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or other valuable features that might be lost on switching any of the three ceding plans. This absence is confirmed by the extraction metadata (all five featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths marked NotPresentInDocument). No retrieved passage addresses this either.

      - 6. Mid growth rate label discrepancy: the report (p11) refers to a mid-growth rate of 2.94%; the critical yield table (p18) and the switch report [P7] label it as 2.95%. Minor but noted.

      - 7. Scottish Widows small pots: the report states '£200 pension fund withdrawal' but the actual fund value is £211.32 — the report uses a rounded figure in the tax calculation, potentially understating the gross withdrawal amount on which the tax calculation is based.

      - 8. The plan-specific annual charge difference figures (−0.06%, −£2.25 for Aviva; −0.49%, −£506.85 for Zurich; +0.26%, +£268.94 for Standard Life) do not appear in the retrieved switch report passages and could not be independently corroborated from the evidence pack.

    Several material concerns arise. First, the Standard Life cost claim is contradicted internally: the recommendation narrative states switching from Standard Life saves 0.08%, while the charges table shows +0.26% — the new plan is more expensive. The derived figures further reveal the +0.26%/£268.94 figure is computed on ~£103,438 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002, so the comparison for EA5 is built on the wrong base and is unreliable. Second, the Zurich reduction-in-yield analysis in the switch report [P7] shows the new solution's reduction in yield (2.1–2.3%) is materially higher than the existing plan (0.3%), producing projected decreases of £10,400–£15,000 at all growth rates. This contradicts the report's claim that switching from Zurich saves 0.49% pa. While the per-plan charges table and the reduction-in-yield methodology may measure different things (the former being a like-for-like charge comparison, the latter incorporating all costs including the new platform), the directional contradiction — the switch report showing a significant negative effect of switching from Zurich at every growth rate — is a material concern that the suitability report does not address or reconcile. Third, the immediate day-one capital reduction of £3,305.00 (from £110,185.60 to £106,880.60) shown in the switch report [P2/P7] is not disclosed in the suitability report's switching costs section, though out-of-market risk is noted. Fourth, no analysis of safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features that might be lost on any of the three ceding plans is present in the report or the evidence pack. The non-financial benefits (avoidance of lifestyling, risk alignment, greater investment choice, ongoing monitoring) could in principle justify a net cost increase under the applicable guard, but this guard only operates where the report argues explicitly that non-financial benefits justify a cost increase — and the report does not do so for Standard Life or Zurich, where it affirmatively claims cost savings that are contradicted by the switch report's own analysis. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the available evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The severity is High: the largest ceding plan (Zurich, £103,439) is the subject of a directional contradiction between the report's cost-saving claim and the switch report's reduction-in-yield analysis showing material projected losses, and the Standard Life charge comparison is computed on the wrong fund value.

  [G9.6] Lost guarantees or benefits disadvantage the client

  Potential Concern — citations partly verified

    Report:   The suitability report asserts that no safeguarded benefits are being given up across all five existing arrangements (EA1–EA5, isBeingGivenUp = false for each). For all five arrangements, the report states 'No charges for transfer and/or switching', indicating no exit penalties apply. The featuresOrGuaranteesLost field is absent from all five replacement analysis entries (confirmed as NotPresentInDocument by extraction metadata), meaning the report does not identify any features or guarantees being lost on any transfer.

    File:     [P13] (Scottish Widows / People's Pension correspondence) explicitly confirms: 'Market Value Adjustment/Reduction are both not applicable' and 'I can also confirm that there are no guarantees, discounts or bonuses attached to this member's plan.' [P12] (Policy Research file) shows Market Value Reduction = No for all five arrangements. [P14] (Fact Find) shows GMP Amount, Enhanced Tax Free Cash, Guaranteed Annuity Rate/Pension, EFI/Loyalty Bonus/Terminal Bonus, and Guaranteed Growth Rates fields all blank/empty for the plans reviewed. [P6] confirms transfer values equal current values across all arrangements (no MVR or penalty deduction evident). The Pension Switch Report [P2, P4, P5] does not flag any guaranteed benefits or protected features for the three switching plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life). No passage in the pack identifies any safeguarded benefit, guaranteed annuity rate, protected tax-free cash, GMP, loyalty bonus or other valuable feature attached to any of the five plans.

    The guard for G9.6 states it only applies where G9.3 identified a benefit actually being given up. The evidence across the pack consistently supports the report's assertion that no safeguarded benefits or guarantees exist on any of the five plans. [P13] provides explicit provider confirmation for the Scottish Widows/People's Pension plan that no guarantees, discounts or bonuses are attached. [P12] shows no MVR applicable across all plans. [P14] shows all protected-benefit fields (GMP, enhanced TFC, GAR, loyalty bonuses, guaranteed growth rates) are blank in the fact find, consistent with none existing. The Pension Switch Report does not surface any such features for the three switching plans. There is no passage in the pack that contradicts the report's assertion of no safeguarded benefits. The exit-penalty assertion ('No charges for transfer and/or switching') is stated for all five plans and is not contradicted by any evidence — [P6] confirms transfer values equal current values. The absence of featuresOrGuaranteesLost in the extraction is therefore explained by there being nothing to record, not by an omission. No vulnerability factors have been identified in the evidence pack that would materially affect this specific finding. Overall, the report's position is fully corroborated: no safeguarded benefits, guarantees or valuable features are being given up, and no exit penalties apply.

  [G9.7] Other alternatives provide better outcomes for the client

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report considers five alternatives to the recommended new Aviva Platform pension (Option 5): Option 1 – leave existing pensions untouched; Option 2 – internal fund switch; Option 3 – consolidate into an existing arrangement (Scottish Widows); Option 4 – consolidate into the employer's People's Pension (rejected because funds do not meet risk profile, the plan is 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution, carries a £4.50 annual admin charge, and there are no Multi-Asset funds); Option 6 – retain People's Pension (recommended, as employer contributions are still being made). The Scottish Widows plan is to be encashed under small pots rules (net ~£170). The three plans being switched are Aviva (EA3), Zurich (EA4) and Standard Life (EA5). For Standard Life the report states the plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution', citing lower costs as a reason to switch. The report states that no features or guarantees are lost on any ceding plan. A charges comparison table (page 11) and a pension switching report are referenced. The report does not address features/guarantees lost for any of the five existing arrangements (extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument). Cost comparison paths for EA1–EA5 are also absent from the extraction. The report's own charges table (page 11) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase in cost under the new solution), while the page 8 narrative states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying the new plan is cheaper). These are contradictory within the report itself.

    File:     The pension switching report (category G) provides individual plan-by-plan comparisons for all three ceding plans (Aviva, Zurich, Standard Life) against the new Aviva Platform solution across low/mid/high growth scenarios [P5, P8, P3, P14]. For the overall consolidated position, the new solution projects £115,000 at mid growth vs £127,000 for existing pensions — a £12,000 (9.4%) decrease at mid growth [P13]. At low growth the new solution projects £97,600 vs £108,000 existing — a £10,400 (9.6%) decrease [P13]. High growth: £135,000 vs £150,000 — £15,000 (10.0%) decrease [P13]. The reduction in yield under the new solution (including adviser charges) rises to 2.1–2.3% vs 0.3% for existing pensions [P13]. For each individual plan the switching report shows the effect of switching is a small decrease (e.g. Aviva: £20 or 0.5% at mid growth [P5]; Standard Life: comparable small decrease [P8]; Zurich: £2,000 or 1.6% at mid growth [P14]). The immediate death benefit shortfall post-switch is £3,305 [P13, P4]. The switching report confirms 'No benefits were listed for the existing products' under benefits lost [P14]. The Zurich policy information [P9] explicitly states: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' The Scottish Widows policy information [P24] confirms no GMP, no guaranteed minimum fund, no protected rights. The Aviva policy information [P22] confirms standard retirement options including FAD and UFPLS. Standard Life policy info [P20] confirms no UFPLS available on that plan. The policy research [P18] and existing arrangements table [P19] confirm People's Pension allows FAD and UFPLS; Standard Life (EA5) offers only FAD. The switching report's mid-growth rate is stated as 2.95% in the comparison table [P5] but 2.94% in the Aviva-only alternative comparison [P5]. The switching report overall position shows the new solution produces lower projected values than retaining existing pensions at all growth rates [P13]. No passage evidences the People's Pension being 0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution with a specific charge comparison figure.

    Discrepancies:

      - 1. The report (page 8) states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying the switch saves 0.08%). The report's own charges comparison table (page 11) shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26% — meaning the new solution is 0.26% MORE expensive for Standard Life, not cheaper. These are directly contradictory within the report.

      - 2. The report asserts no features or guarantees are lost on any ceding plan, but does not address this at all for EA1 (People's Pension — retained so not a ceding plan), EA2 (Scottish Widows — encashed), EA3 (Aviva), EA4 (Zurich), or EA5 (Standard Life). The extraction confirms these paths are NotPresentInDocument. However, the switching report [P14] states 'No benefits were listed for the existing products', and the Zurich policy letter [P9] confirms no safeguarded benefits, so this is partially corroborated for Zurich and Aviva. Standard Life's policy info [P20] does not explicitly confirm absence of safeguarded benefits beyond confirming no UFPLS; no passage positively confirms the absence of safeguarded/enhanced benefits for Standard Life.

      - 3. The report claims the overall new solution is cost-effective and justifies the switch on lower costs. However, the switching report's overall position shows the new solution projects £115,000 at mid growth vs £127,000 for existing pensions — a £12,000 decrease — with a reduction in yield rising from 0.3% (existing) to 2.2% (new solution including adviser charges) [P13]. The report acknowledges this (£4,672 outperformance required including adviser fees) but the cost-saving rationale for Standard Life specifically is undermined by the +0.26% charge impact figure.

      - 4. The report states the People's Pension is '0.06% more expensive than the recommended solution' as a reason to discount Option 4. No passage in the file provides a charges comparison table or figure that corroborates the 0.06% figure specifically for the People's Pension vs the new solution.

      - 5. The mid-growth rate is stated as 2.94% in one part of the switching report [P5, Aviva alternative comparison] and 2.95% in the overall comparison table [P5, P13]. The report's own narrative (page 11) refers to 2.94% while the critical yield table (page 18) uses 2.95%. This is a minor inconsistency within the evidence.

      - 6. The EA5 (Standard Life) charge computation in the derived figures is calculated on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.89) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), making the charge impact row for Standard Life unreliable. This is consistent with the +0.26% figure in the charges table being computed on the wrong fund value, which would explain why the Standard Life cost comparison is erroneous.

      - 7. The report does not provide plan-specific cost/benefit analysis for the Scottish Widows encashment (EA2, £211.32) beyond the small pots rules rationale. However, given the negligible value this is a low-materiality omission.

      - 8. Standard Life (EA5) does not facilitate UFPLS [P20], whereas the new Aviva Platform does. This retirement flexibility difference is not explicitly addressed as a plan-specific benefit of switching in the report beyond the general 'Enhanced Retirement Flexibility' point.

    The report contains a genuine and material internal contradiction regarding the Standard Life cost comparison. Page 8 of the suitability report states the Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' — i.e. switching saves costs. However, the charges comparison table at page 11 shows the Standard Life impact as +0.26%, meaning the new solution is more expensive for Standard Life. This is corroborated by the derived figures analysis, which shows that the EA5 charge impact row (£268.94 at 0.26%) is computed on Zurich's fund value (£103,438.46) rather than Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the comparison is computed on the wrong plan's fund value and is therefore unreliable. The report's cost-saving rationale for switching Standard Life is therefore not supported by, and is directly contradicted by, its own charges table. This satisfies the 'High' severity criterion: a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts. On safeguarded benefits: Zurich's policy letter [P9] explicitly confirms no guarantees, GMP, MVR, protected tax-free cash or lifestyling apply. The switching report [P14] records 'No benefits were listed for the existing products'. Scottish Widows [P24] confirms no GMP or guaranteed minimum fund. These corroborate the report's implicit position for Zurich, Aviva and Scottish Widows. However, no passage in the file positively confirms the absence of safeguarded or enhanced benefits for Standard Life — the Standard Life policy info [P20] is brief and does not address this explicitly. This is a lower-materiality gap given the small fund value (£3,002). The People's Pension employer contributions point is correctly identified and addressed in the report: the report explicitly warns that transferring away would cause loss of employer contributions and recommends retaining the plan [P19]. The overall projected maturity values in the switching report show the new solution produces lower projected values at all growth rates when adviser charges are included [P13], which the report does address via the 'outperformance required' disclosure. The alternatives analysis is substantive: five options are considered with plan-specific reasons. The 0.06% more expensive figure for the People's Pension is stated in the report but not corroborated by any charge comparison passage in the file. The Standard Life cost contradiction is the primary concern and is not explained by any false-positive guard: both figures purport to describe the same comparison (Standard Life existing vs new solution) and are directly contradictory. The vulnerability overlay: no vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence that would materially affect this specific replacement analysis. The outcome is Potential Concern at High severity driven principally by the Standard Life cost contradiction.

  [G9.8] Evidence supporting claims that higher costs are justified by additional benefits

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the following benefits of switching for each ceding plan: (1) Scottish Widows (EA2): small pots encashment recommended; net receipt estimated at £170 (25% tax-free, 75% taxed at marginal rate on a ~£211.32 fund). (2) Aviva (EA3): lower costs (0.06% saving per charges table), lifestyling risk avoidance, greater investment choice, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Net benefit conclusion: 'lower costs, greater investment flexibility, and continued professional oversight'. (3) Zurich (EA4): greater investment choice, multi-asset fund selection (Zurich lacks risk level 5 funds, so a blend of risk 7 and risk 2 is used), enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. No lower-cost claim made for Zurich switch. (4) Standard Life (EA5): lower costs stated as 'plan is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), lifestyling risk, greater investment choice, enhanced retirement flexibility, adviser charging facilitation, ongoing monitoring. Net benefit conclusion includes 'lower costs'. The charges table (page 11) shows the recommended plan is +0.26% MORE expensive than Standard Life on a like-for-like basis. The report also states that including adviser fees the new plan is 4.24% more expensive overall, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one, and that based on past performance a 4.8% critical yield is achievable.

    File:     [P14] (charges table, page 11 of suitability report): Aviva existing 0.50%/£18.72; recommended impact -0.06%/-£2.25 (saving). Zurich existing 0.93%/£961.98; impact -0.49%/-£506.85 (saving). Standard Life existing 0.18%/£186.19; impact +0.26%/+£268.94 (increase — recommended plan is MORE expensive). Like-for-like new plan is 0.24% less expensive overall; including adviser fee it is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance. [P15] (suitability report page 8): Standard Life recommendation narrative states 'The plan itself is 0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving of 0.08%). [P18] (suitability report page 11): 'the recommended plan would need to grow by 4.8% per year up to age 75 in order to meet the same value as your current plan. Based on the past performance of your recommended funds, I do believe that this is achievable.' [P4] (pension switch report): All-in switching from all three plans produces a maturity value decrease at every growth rate (e.g. mid: £127,000 existing vs £115,000 new solution, a £12,000/9.4% decrease). [P8] (pension switch report): New solution ranks 5th out of 9 options by projected maturity value (£125,000 vs existing solution £127,000). The Zurich pension alone scores highest at £128,000. [P3] (pension switch report): Retaining Aviva only alternative: existing £4,280, new £3,430. Zurich only retained: existing £120,000, new solution results in lower. [P5] (Zurich policy info): 'Transfer value and early retirement charge — There are no deductions taken from the current value if benefits are transferred or taken.' Fund switch charge £0.00. No guaranteed annuity rates confirmed (checkbox not completed). [P9] (Zurich policy info): Mentions some plans have guaranteed annuity rates; checkbox not ticked confirming presence or absence. [P12] (pension switch report appendix): Zurich fund listed as generic 'fund' with N/A statistics; no detailed fund performance data. [P19] (suitability report): Zurich recommendation explicitly states no lower-cost claim; benefit is fund choice and multi-asset selection. Zurich lacks risk level 5 funds — a blend of risk 7 and risk 2 is used. Internal fund switch not recommended because client's priority is consolidation and drawdown readiness.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life narrative (page 8) states the existing plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. switching saves 0.08%), but the charges table (page 11) shows the impact of the recommended alternative as +0.26% — meaning the recommended plan is MORE expensive than Standard Life, directly contradicting the narrative cost-saving claim for that plan.

      - The derived figures confirm EA5 charge rows are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's own value (£3,002), rendering the Standard Life cost comparison in the charges table unreliable on its own terms.

      - The net benefit conclusion for Standard Life (page 8) includes 'lower costs' as a stated benefit, but the charges table shows a cost increase of +0.26%/+£268.94 for that plan.

      - The report asserts the 4.8% critical yield is achievable based on past performance, but the pension switch report (P4) shows the recommended new solution produces lower projected maturity values than the existing solution at every growth rate when Zurich-only is compared (mid: £115,000 new vs £127,000 existing, a £12,000 decrease), and the new solution ranks only 5th in the market comparison (P8).

      - The report states the like-for-like new plan is 0.24% cheaper, but including adviser fees it is 4.24% more expensive (£4,672 outperformance required). The report presents both without clearly reconciling them for the client.

      - No features-or-guarantees-lost analysis is present in the report for any of the five ceding plans (extraction confirms all five featuresOrGuaranteesLost paths absent as NotPresentInDocument). The Zurich policy document (P9) raises the question of guaranteed annuity rates but the checkbox is not completed, leaving this unresolved.

      - The 'greater investment choice' and 'adviser charging facilitation' benefits are asserted generically across multiple plans without plan-specific evidence that these features are unavailable on the ceding plans.

      - The pension switch report (P8) shows that retaining the Zurich pension alone (ranked 1st, £128,000) outperforms the new solution (£125,000), yet no analysis explaining why the superior-performing Zurich-only alternative was rejected in favour of the lower-returning consolidation is present in the suitability report.

      - The mid growth rate is referred to as 2.94% on page 11 of the suitability report (P18) but the pension switch report table labels it 2.95% (P4), a minor inconsistency.

      - The report's costComparison paths for EA1 through EA5 are all absent (NotPresentInDocument per extraction metadata), meaning no plan-specific cost comparison is documented for EA1 (People's Pension, retained) or EA2 (Scottish Widows, encashed).

    The central concern under G9.8 is whether higher costs are justified by the additional benefits claimed. Several material issues arise.

   

    1. Standard Life cost contradiction (High severity trigger): The report's own charges table (P14) shows the recommended plan is +0.26% more expensive than Standard Life on a like-for-like basis, yet the recommendation narrative (P15) states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution'. These directly contradict each other. The derived figures confirm the Standard Life rows in the charges table are computed on Zurich's fund value (~£103,439) rather than Standard Life's £3,002, meaning the +0.26%/+£268.94 figures themselves are unreliable — but the narrative claim of a 0.08% saving is equally unsupported by any correctly-computed figure. The net benefit conclusion for Standard Life then asserts 'lower costs' as a benefit when neither a properly-computed saving nor a correctly-attributed cost comparison is available. This is a material discrepancy that the guards do not explain away.

   

    2. Overall cost increase: Including adviser fees, the report openly acknowledges the new plan is 4.24% more expensive, requiring £4,672 outperformance in year one and a 4.8% critical yield. The pension switch report (P4) shows the all-in switch produces lower projected maturity values at every growth rate. The assertion that 4.8% is achievable based on past performance is not corroborated by any quantified fund performance evidence in the pack; the Zurich appendix (P12) shows N/A for all statistics, and the pension switch report's own comparison ranks the new solution 5th (P8). The 'higher costs justified by benefits' claim therefore rests on an unsubstantiated performance assertion for the largest plan.

   

    3. Features and guarantees: No featuresOrGuaranteesLost analysis appears anywhere in the report for any ceding plan. The Zurich policy document (P9) explicitly raises guaranteed annuity rates as a question but the checkbox is not ticked, leaving it unresolved. The guard requires provider documentation confirming none exist before this can be cleared; none is present.

   

    4. Generic benefits: 'Greater investment choice' and 'adviser charging facilitation' are asserted for multiple plans without evidence that these features are unavailable on the ceding plans. The guard states a 'better fund choice' benefit needs a fund count or named client requirement to count. For Zurich this requirement is partly met — the report specifically states Zurich lacks risk level 5 funds (P19), which is a client-specific need. For Aviva and Standard Life the assertion is generic.

   

    5. Alternative not addressed: The pension switch report (P8) shows retaining Zurich alone is the highest-ranking option (£128,000 at mid growth). The report does not explain why this superior-return alternative was rejected. While consolidation is cited as a client priority (P19), the report does not quantify what the client sacrifices by consolidating rather than retaining Zurich.

   

    The vulnerability overlay: no specific vulnerability characteristics are identified in the evidence pack as materially affecting this assessment.

   

    On balance, the Standard Life cost claim directly contradicts the charges table, the features/guarantees question is unresolved for Zurich, and the justification for higher overall costs (4.24% including adviser fees) is not corroborated by evidence of fund performance. These are not explained by any guard. The outcome is Potential Concern at High severity.

  [G9.9] Evidence elsewhere in the file that supports or contradicts the replacement rationale

  Potential Concern — severity High — citations partly verified

    Report:   The report asserts the following net benefit conclusions and rationales: (1) Scottish Widows (EA2): encash under small pots rules, net receipt ~£170, would not detract from retirement plan; rationale includes that SW does not offer a single multi-asset fund at risk rating 5, requiring two funds with 20-30% in money market/cash, and the plan does not allow adviser management. (2) Aviva (EA3): switching saves 0.06% in charges; funds in lifestyling that will move outside agreed risk range; new scheme offers wider investment options and allows adviser charges to be deducted. Net benefit: lower costs, greater investment flexibility, continued professional oversight. (3) Zurich (EA4): net benefit stated as greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, continued professional oversight. (4) Standard Life (EA5): switching saves 0.08% in charges; net benefit: lower costs, greater investment flexibility, improved retirement options, continued professional oversight. People's Pension (EA1): retained because it is the current workplace scheme with ongoing employer contributions; transferring would lose employer contributions. The charges comparison table (page 11) shows: Aviva existing 0.50%/-0.06% impact; Zurich existing 0.93%/-0.67% impact; Standard Life existing 0.18%/+0.26% impact.

    File:     File notes (C) confirm: Scottish Widows does not support ongoing adviser charging; alternatives for SW considered (30% liquid fund / 70% Portfolio Three) but discounted as no servicing and reduced flexibility. Aviva alternative discounted as only mixed asset fund available is risk rated 4, not meeting client ATR of 5. Zurich alternative discounted as no risk level 5 funds, blend of 7 and 2 used but funds not performed as well and singular asset types not recommended; client priority is consolidation. Standard Life alternative discounted as all funds within 20-60% equities range are more expensive than Aviva offering. Pension switch report (G) confirms the three plans switched are Aviva, Zurich and Standard Life to Aviva Pension Portfolio Choice SIPP. Zurich policy info (E/P3) confirms: 'Guarantees, bonuses, guaranteed minimum pension, safeguarding, life styling, block/bulk transfers, market value reduction, protected or enhanced tax free cash do not apply to this plan.' Aviva policy info (E/P8) confirms: 'This plan doesn't have any guaranteed rates, including annuity rates' and 'The plan doesn't include a Guaranteed Minimum Pension'; plan has Lifestage Investment Approach (auto-switching). Scottish Widows (E/P10, P14) confirms: 'Money4Life Plans (Plans starting with ZU) do not support ongoing advisor charging, the transferring of Servicing Rights or Commission.' Standard Life (E/P9): transfer value available, no safeguarded benefits evident from retrieved passages but no explicit confirmation in retrieved text that no guarantees exist. Pension switch report (G/P6) shows a product comparison ranking: New Solution ranked 5th (£125,000 maturity value) versus Existing Solution ranked 2nd (£127,000). The like-for-like charges comparison (P22) shows Standard Life impact as +0.26% (an increase, not a saving). The report's page 8 narrative states Standard Life is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (i.e. a saving), directly contradicting the +0.26% shown in the charges table on page 11.

    Discrepancies:

      - Standard Life cost claim contradiction: report page 8 states the existing Standard Life plan is '0.08% more expensive than the recommended solution' (implying switching saves 0.08%), but the charges comparison table on page 11 shows the impact of the recommended alternative for Standard Life as +0.26% — meaning the new plan is MORE expensive for Standard Life, not less. These are irreconcilable.

      - EA5 charge arithmetic error: derived figures confirm the EA5 annual charge of £186.19 at 0.18% implies a fund value of £103,438.89 (Zurich's value), not Standard Life's £3,002 — meaning the Standard Life row in the charges table was computed on the wrong plan's fund value, rendering any cost comparison for Standard Life unreliable.

      - New solution ranks 5th out of compared products (£125,000 maturity value) while the existing solution ranks 2nd (£127,000) in the pension switch report — the recommended solution produces a lower projected maturity value than the existing solution and several alternatives, which is not addressed or reconciled in the report's net benefit conclusions.

      - Net benefit conclusion for Standard Life (page 8) claims 'lower costs' as a benefit, but the charges table directly contradicts this for that specific plan.

      - No explicit confirmation in the retrieved evidence that Standard Life has no safeguarded benefits or guarantees — the Standard Life policy info passage (P9) does not contain an explicit statement that no guarantees exist, unlike the Aviva and Zurich provider documentation which expressly state this.

      - The report states the net benefit for Zurich switching includes 'lower costs' indirectly (via consolidation), but the Zurich rationale section (page 8) does not state a specific cost saving percentage for Zurich while the charges table shows -0.67% — this is not a direct contradiction but an omission in the narrative.

      - People's Pension retained rationale (employer contributions) is supported by evidence, no discrepancy there.

      - Scottish Widows encashment rationale (no adviser management, no suitable single fund) is corroborated by provider documentation confirming no adviser charging — no discrepancy.

    The replacement rationale is partially supported but contains a material internal contradiction and an arithmetic error that together undermine the Standard Life switching justification. The most serious issue is the Standard Life cost claim: the report's page 8 narrative says switching saves 0.08% (existing plan is 0.08% more expensive), while the charges comparison table on page 11 shows +0.26% — the recommended plan is actually more expensive for Standard Life. The derived figures confirm this is not a rounding issue but an arithmetic error: the EA5 charge row was computed using Zurich's fund value (£103,439) rather than Standard Life's (£3,002), corrupting the entire Standard Life cost comparison. This means one of the stated net benefit claims ('lower costs') for Standard Life is contradicted by the report's own charges table. This is within the highest/high severity band per the decision logic (a switch justified on cost savings that the report's own comparison table contradicts). The pension switch report (G/P6) also shows the new solution ranks 5th with a projected maturity value of £125,000 versus the existing solution's £127,000 — a £2,000 or 1.6% decrease. The report does not address why switching to a solution with a lower projected maturity value than the existing arrangement represents a net benefit, other than through qualitative claims about flexibility and oversight. These qualitative benefits (greater investment flexibility, adviser management, consolidation) are corroborated by file notes in category C and provider documentation (SW confirming no adviser charging, Aviva confirming lifestage approach, Zurich confirming no suitable risk-5 funds). The Scottish Widows encashment rationale is well-supported: provider documentation confirms no adviser charging capability. The People's Pension retention rationale (ongoing employer contributions) is stated and not contradicted. The Aviva switch rationale (lifestyling outside risk range, no suitable fund at ATR 5) is corroborated by Aviva policy info confirming the Lifestage Investment Approach and file notes confirming the only mixed asset Aviva fund is rated 4. The Zurich switch rationale is supported by file notes. No vulnerability factors are identified in the evidence that would materially alter this assessment. The Standard Life cost contradiction and the absence of explicit confirmation of no safeguarded benefits for Standard Life (unlike Aviva and Zurich where provider letters expressly confirm this) are the residual concerns. On balance, the Standard Life cost contradiction is a genuine finding not explained by any guard: the guard requires the contradiction to arise from a different charge basis, but here both figures purport to be like-for-like annual recurring charge comparisons for the same plan. The lower projected maturity value for the new solution is a concern that is not addressed in the rationale.
